<note>The title page is framed by an ornate woodcut border. At the top center, a head rests upon a platter—likely a depiction of the martyrdom of John the Baptist—while the side columns show various figures climbing as if scaling the architecture. The bottom panel features a scene with two figures in conversation within a landscape, a common motif in Renaissance book illustration to signify the dialogue of scholars. This decorative style is characteristic of the early 16th-century printing houses in Basel.</note>

->**THE FIRST**<-
->**CENTURY OF**<-
->**MISCELLANIES**<-
->**BY ANGELO**<-
->**POLIZIANO**<-
<note>original: "ANGELI POLITIANI MISCELLANEORVM CENTVRIA VNA." Angelo Poliziano (1454–1494) was a preeminent Italian humanist scholar and poet. This work, the <term>Miscellanea</term> (a collection of philological essays), was first published in 1489; the "Century" refers to the one hundred chapters or "observations" on classical texts contained within.</note>

->**IN THE RENOWNED**<-
->**BASEL.**<-
<note>original: "IN INCLYTA BASILEA." Basel was a major center for the printing of humanist texts during the Renaissance, home to famous printers like Johann Froben.</note>

<note>A blue rectangular library stamp at the bottom center indicates that this volume was once part of the collection of the Munich State Library (original: "Staatsbibliothek München").</note>

<note>A handwritten shelf mark or archival reference, "166/48," is inscribed in ink at the bottom right corner, likely added by a librarian in the 19th or 20th century.</note>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page contains the preface "To the Reader," following the title page. It serves as an introduction by the editor or publisher, praising Poliziano’s scholarship and explaining the features of this specific edition, such as its portable size and the inclusion of Greek translations.</meta>

->**TO THE READER.**<-

Although there is almost nothing, even the smallest detail, in the highly polished writings of Poliziano in which the highest learning combined with the highest eloquence is not to be admired—for that man triumphed so successfully in both languages <note>original: "utraque lingua"; referring to Latin and Greek, the mastery of which was the hallmark of the Renaissance humanist.</note> and in both styles <note>original: "utroque stilo"; referring to prose and poetry.</note> that you would not easily find anyone among the moderns whom you could compare to him—nonetheless, he seems to me to have provided the preeminent example of his talent in these *Miscellany* commentaries. In these, by the immortal God, how profound is the learning, how varied the reading in both languages, how exact the critical judgment, and to say it all at once, how all-encompassing is the teaching found in such a tiny little book! By Hercules, these notes are not only suited to <term>grammarians</term> <note>In the Renaissance, "grammar" referred to the broad field of philology—the study of language, history, and classical literature—rather than just the rules of speech.</note>—even if Poliziano modestly claimed only that role for himself—but they are by no means to be looked down upon by legal experts or philosophers, especially since there is no genre of writing in which Poliziano was not engaged with the highest praise, or which he did not examine most diligently.

Therefore, so that these works might be more readily at hand for students, and could be carried without trouble by those walking about, either in a pocket or in the hand, we have condensed them into this <term>enchiridion</term> <note>From the Greek *en-cheiridion*, meaning "in the hand." It refers to a portable handbook designed to be carried easily, a popular format pioneered by printers like Aldus Manutius.</note>. Truly, as the reader himself will discover, it has been corrected and annotated in no light fashion. We have also added an index of the subjects and words that seemed especially necessary to point out. Finally, we have added as a supplement an interpretation of the Greek passages—which were omitted by the author himself and have now been explained by Jacques Toussain <note>original: "Iacobo Tusano." Jacques Toussain (c. 1490–1547) was a renowned French scholar and a royal professor of Greek in Paris.</note>—prefixing them so that an inattentive reader might not mistake our interpretations or those of another for the original monuments of the author himself, and revere them as such. Farewell.

<note>A blue circular library stamp from the Bavarian State Library (Bayerische Staatsbibliothek München) appears at the bottom of the page, marking the book's historical provenance.</note>

<summary>This preface praises Angelo Poliziano's immense erudition in both Latin and Greek, explaining that this edition was designed as a portable handbook for students and includes new Greek-to-Latin translations by the scholar Jacques Toussain.</summary>
<keywords>Angelo Poliziano, Miscellanea, humanism, Jacques Toussain, enchiridion, philology, Greek translation, Renaissance printing, Basel</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the index of Poliziano’s Miscellanea into a collection of Greek excerpts and fragments, organized by the quires (gatherings of paper) of the manuscript. It focuses on classical references ranging from Homeric epic to Athenian satire.</meta>

# ->OF THE GREEKS<-

## Gathering D

O dear son of Kleinias and Deinomache. <note>original: "ὦ φίλε παῖ κλεινίς καὶ δεινομάχης"; this phrase addresses the famous Athenian statesman Alcibiades by his parentage.</note>

But come now, change your theme and sing of the fashioning of the wooden horse, which Epeius made with the help of Athena. <note>original: "Ἀλλ’ ἄγε δή μετάβηθι..."; these lines are from Homer’s *Odyssey* (Book 8, 492–493), where Odysseus asks the bard Demodocus to sing of the Trojan Horse.</note>

The great wooden horse, in which all the best of the Greeks sat.

But truly they inhabit the furthest corner of the land, near the Syrtes of the Moorish nation. <note>original: "μαυρουσίδος ἔθνεα γαίης"; likely a geographical reference to North Africa used in Hellenistic poetry.</note>

Just as they were also mocked in comedy.

## Gathering E

Which is clear both from the name and from the intent.

It is not in the least becoming for Hermes and Hercules, the overseers of the wrestling school; <note>Hermes and Hercules were the traditional patron deities of the *palaestra* (wrestling school), representing athletic excellence and masculine virtue.</note> instead, he serves as a priest to Cotytto and the other Attic dust-demons. <note>original: "κοτυττοῖ δὲ καὶ τοῖς ἄλλοις ἀττικοῖς κονισάλοις"; Cotytto was a Thracian goddess associated with lewd nocturnal rites. The *Konisaloi* were phallic spirits of the dust, often used in Greek comedy to satirize debauchery.</note>

And he did not wish to behold only the pleasure of swine.

<keywords>Alcibiades, Homer, Odyssey, Trojan Horse, Epeius, Cotytto, Konisaloi, Greek Satire, Palaestra</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly examination of Greek literature by providing Latin translations (labeled as "Interpretatio") of various fragments. These fragments include lyrical snippets, a comedic "Echo" poem, and notable verses from Euripides and the Greek Anthology.</meta>

**INTERPRETATION.**

The pleasant clothing of the purple-clad kings grew red. <note>original: "Amœna purpuratorum rubefiebat ueſtis regum"</note>
No boat-fare for sorrows, nor a pleasing lyre. <note>The word "naula" refers to the coin paid to Charon to cross the river Styx; the line suggests that music and wealth are useless in the face of death.</note>
...those most softly dressed.

<term>Ascopera</term>: a traveler's pouch or a small bag.
Echo, dear to me, resound something. <gloss>what?</gloss> <note>This section is an "Echo poem," a literary game where the final word of a line is repeated by the "Echo" to create a new meaning or answer a question.</note>
I love Corisca, but she does not love me. <gloss>she loves</gloss>
But the time does not allow the opportunity for an approach. <gloss>it allows</gloss>
You, therefore, tell her that I love her. <gloss>I love</gloss>
And you give her the security of money. <gloss>you give</gloss>
Echo, what remains? To gain my wish? <gloss>to gain.</gloss>
Indeed, I measure the manners of an old hostess.
Envy spoke secret things into the ears of Apollo. <note>A reference to Callimachus’s *Hymn to Apollo*, where Envy whispers that a poet is only great if they write a work as vast as the sea.</note>

I do not admire that singer, who does not even sing as much as the sea.
Even if you devour me to the root, yet I will still produce... <note>This begins a translation of a famous epigram by Evenus of Ascalon (Greek Anthology 9.75), where a vine speaks to a goat eating its leaves.</note>
...enough to sprinkle upon you, goat, when you are sacrificed. <insert>enough.</insert>
Even if you devour me, etc.
As much as might suffice to pour over Caesar when he is sacrificed. <insert>might suffice.</insert> <note>The translator or scribe has swapped "the goat" for "Caesar," potentially turning a classical poem into a contemporary political satire or warning.</note>
The Corinthian women have left their homes. <note>A reference to the entrance of the Chorus in Euripides’s tragedy, *Medea*.</note>
Lest you turn anything into a charge against me; for I have known many
who became honorable—some indeed far from the eyes of mortals,
others in foreign parts; but men, through an idle foot, <note>In this context, an "idle foot" refers to those who stay at home or withdraw from public life, often incurring a reputation for laziness or pride.</note>
have contracted infamy and laziness for themselves.
Would that the ship Argo had not flown forth. <note>The famous opening line of Euripides's *Medea*, wishing the voyage that brought Medea to Greece had never happened.</note>

<keywords>Echo poetry, Euripides, Medea, Callimachus, Evenus of Ascalon, Greek Anthology, classical translation, Apollo</keywords>

# **GREEK PASSAGES** <note>original Latin: "GRAECORVM"</note>

Would that the ship Argo had never flown to the land of the Colchians, through the dark-blue Clashing Rocks, <note>These are the Symplegades, mythical islands at the entrance of the Black Sea that crushed ships between them.</note> and that the timber of the pine had never fallen in the glens of Mount Pelion, nor had it provided oars for the hands of the best of men, who went in search of the all-golden fleece for Pelias; for then my mistress Medea would not have sailed to the towers of the land of Iolcus, her heart struck with love for Jason. <note>These are the opening lines of Euripides' tragedy, *Medea*.</note>
It creates a disturbance.
And some aged servant, thinking perhaps that the rages of Pan or some other god had come upon her, raised a joyful cry. <note>A reference to the *Bacchae* of Euripides; ancient Greeks believed sudden, irrational fears or behaviors were "panic" caused by the god Pan.</note>
Athamas, driven to frenzy by the mad whip of Pan,
and always those within hearing.
The thundering sound of the Cronian whip of Pan <note>"Cronian" refers to the lineage of Zeus, son of Cronus.</note>
would that he had previously reached the limit of his mind's defense.
Who shall extinguish the Isaurian courage,
the grandfather-killer, and took away the cheese. <note>This appears to be a fragment of a political epigram or a riddle, possibly referring to the Isaurian emperors of the Byzantine period.</note>

# **Gathering H** <note>original Latin: "Quaternio H"; this refers to the eighth "quire" or bundle of folded parchment sheets in the manuscript.</note>

**Wasps** <note>original: "σφῆκες" (sphekes); likely a reference to Aristophanes’ comedy *The Wasps*.</note>
And I shall flash like lightning, and they make a popping sound.
It is a custom for lightning to make a popping sound. <note>The Greek verb "poppyzein" describes a smacking or clicking sound made with the lips, used here to describe the crackle of lightning.</note>
The yoke-driver makes a popping sound.
Or if there is some unarticulated noise, as in the smacking of lips.
May I be healthy.
It is time to move on to the fast parts without tension. <note>A musical or rhetorical instruction regarding the pace of a performance.</note>

<summary>This page contains a collection of Greek poetic fragments, including the famous opening of Euripides' Medea, and linguistic notes regarding the onomatopoeic word for "popping" or "lip-smacking."</summary>
<keywords>Euripides, Medea, Pan, Greek tragedy, lightning, Isaurians, linguistics, Aristophanes, Gathering H</keywords>

<meta>This page provides the Latin "Interpretatio" (translation and commentary) for the Greek literary fragments presented on the previous page. It continues the scholarly analysis of classical texts, moving from the Greek source to a Latinate explanation intended for Renaissance or early modern students.</meta>

# INTERPRETATION <note>original Latin: "INTERPRETATIO"</note>

...and force; these things are but races and shadow-boxings. <note>original Greek: "σκιαμαχίαι" (*skiamachiai*). This refers to fighting an imaginary opponent or practicing without a real adversary.</note>

<meta>This page continues the philological interpretation of Greek poetic fragments, specifically those of Callimachus. It focuses on the mythological origins of the Horned Altar on Delos, the constellation of Berenice’s hair, and the tragic fates of the giants Otos and Orion.</meta>

# INTERPRETATION

->The Horned Altar.<-
Phoebus was four years old when he laid the first foundations,
in beautiful Ortygia, by the circular lake;
Diana, hunting, continually brought back the horns of Cynthian goats,
but Apollo himself wove them together.
He built the foundations indeed from horns, and he constructed the altar
out of horns, and led the walls of horn all around it. <note>original: "Corneam aram." These lines are a Latin translation of Callimachus’s *Hymn to Apollo*. They describe the mythical construction of the *Keraton* (Horned Altar) on the island of Delos—anciently Ortygia—which Apollo was said to have built as a child using the horns of goats killed by his sister Diana (Artemis) on Mount Cynthus.</note>

A defect of memory. <note>original: "Memoriæ uitium." Likely a philological note on a common literary error or a specific "slip of the pen" in the manuscripts being studied.</note>

In the Roman manner. <margin>One who wrote very many things.</margin> <note>original: "Romane / Qui plurima scripsit." This refers to a prolific author who writes in the Latin language or follows Roman literary conventions.</note>

Drawing it out to the end, as if stretching it out to cool. <note>original: "In summum trahens uelut extra refrigerans." This continues a humorous metaphor from the previous page, comparing a long-winded or tedious writer to someone stretching out a sausage to let it cool.</note>
Either Conon saw me, the Lock of Berenice, in the air,
or she dedicated it to all the gods. <note>original: "Vel me Conon uidit..." These lines refer to the *Coma Berenices* (The Lock of Berenice) by Callimachus. Conon was the court astronomer of Egypt who "discovered" a new constellation and claimed it was the lock of hair Queen Berenice II had sacrificed for her husband’s safe return from war.</note>
Callimachus also mentions those Chalybes,
praying that their race might perish, <note>original: "Meminit ipsorũ & Callimachus Chalybum / ut pereat genus." The Chalybes were an ancient people of Asia Minor famous for inventing iron-working. In the poem, the poet curses them for creating the metal tools and weapons that "cut" the lock of hair from the Queen's head.</note>
who first revealed that evil sprout arising from afar. <note>The "evil sprout" is a poetic metaphor for the discovery and mining of iron ore.</note>
Neither Otos nor Orion sought out good marriages. <note>original: "Nihil quidem Otos, nihil quidem Oarion..." Both Otos and Orion (Oarion) were giants in Greek mythology. Otos attempted to kidnap Hera, and Orion’s pursuit of various women—or Artemis herself—led to his death. Their "marriages" or desires were considered acts of hubris.</note>
Preparing an evil fate for the Boeotian Orion
arising from his consorting with women.
For he did not obtain a nature like that of Orion. <note>This likely refers to a contrast between a specific individual and the legendary giant, or perhaps a commentary on Orion's mortal vulnerabilities despite his size.</note>

But Anticlus the Ortygian, whom [he says] died there... <note>original: "Ortygides aũt Anticlus." Anticlus was one of the Greek heroes hidden inside the Trojan Horse. According to some myths, Ulysses had to silence him by force to prevent him from answering Helen’s imitation of his wife’s voice.</note>

It also touches upon the tragic mythological figures of the Chalybes, Otos, and Orion.</summary>

# GREEK FRAGMENTS

The Achaeans, weeping, performed the funeral rites at the horse. <note>original: "ἵππῳ δακρύσαντες ἐνεκτερέϊξαμ ἀχαιοί." This line refers to the Trojan Horse. In the tradition of the Epic Cycle, the Greeks were said to have performed funeral honors even for the wooden horse or for those fallen during its construction.</note>
But when Anticlus received the sting of Laodameia,
he alone opened his mouth and began to respond,
but Odysseus fell upon him. <note>original: "ἄντικλος δ' ὅτε κέντρομ ἐδέξατο..." This describes a famous episode during the infiltration of Troy. Helen, suspecting Greeks were inside the wooden horse, mimicked the voices of the heroes' wives (including Laodameia). Anticlus was about to answer her, which would have revealed the ambush, but Odysseus forcibly silenced him—in some versions, by throttling him to death.</note>

->On the Baths of Pallas.<- <note>original: "εἰς λυτρὰ τῆς παλλάδος." This is the title of Callimachus's Fifth Hymn, which tells the story of the blinding of Tiresias.</note>

Blessed Tiresias, you saw the naked form of the merciful Athena—
against her will—without being destroyed.
You live, though you have lost the light of your eyes, for Athena
has transferred the gleam of your eyes into your mind. <note>original: "ὄλβιε τειρεσία..." These lines from Callimachus explain that while the prophet Tiresias was blinded for accidentally seeing the goddess Athena bathing, she compensated him by granting him "inward sight" or the gift of prophecy.</note>

Quire P <note>original: "Quaternio P." This is a printer's mark indicating the start of a new gathering of paper sheets in the physical book.</note>

In the work *On Abstinence from Animal Food*. <note>original: "ἐν τῷ περὶ ζώωμ ἀποχῆς." A reference to the treatise by the Neoplatonist philosopher Porphyry, who argued for vegetarianism.</note>

Quire Q <note>original: "Quaternio Q." Another printer's mark.</note>

We were once valiant young men.
We shall be much stronger still.
We are valiant now; if you wish, look upon us. <note>original: "ἄμμες ποτ' ἦμεμ..." This is a famous Spartan triple-choral song described by Plutarch. It was performed by three groups: the old men (the first line), the young men in their prime (the second), and the children (the third).</note>

Long ago, the Milesians were valiant. <note>original: "πάλαι ποτ' ἦσαμ ἄλκιμοι μιλήσιοι." A common Greek proverb used to mock those who boast of past glory while their current state is diminished. It originated after the city of Miletus lost its power.</note>

<keywords>Callimachus, Tiresias, Athena, Trojan Horse, Odysseus, Porphyry, Spartan Song, Miletus, philology, Greek proverbs</keywords>

# INTERPRETATION

The Achaeans, weeping, performed the burial rites at the horse. <note>original: "Equo lachrymantes sepeliuerunt Achiui." This refers to the Greeks (Achaeans) honoring those who died during the construction or the final stratagem of the Trojan Horse.</note>
But when Anticlus received the sting of Laodameia,
He alone began to open his voice in response,
But Odysseus rushed upon him. <note>During the fall of Troy, Helen stood outside the wooden horse and mimicked the voices of the Greek soldiers' wives to lure them out. Anticlus was moved by the "sting" of hearing the voice of his wife, Laodameia, and almost revealed the ambush. Odysseus saved the mission by physically silencing him.</note>

->On the Baths of Minerva<- <note>original: "In lauacra Mineruae." This refers to the Fifth Hymn of Callimachus, titled "The Bath of Pallas."</note>

Blessed Tiresias, you indeed saw, without being destroyed,
the naked form of Minerva, who was unwilling yet merciful.
You live though the light of your eyes is lost; however, Pallas Athena
exchanged the brightness of your eyes for the power of your mind. <note>original: "splendorem cum mente commutauit Pallas." In the myth, the prophet Tiresias was blinded after accidentally seeing the goddess Athena bathing, but she granted him the gift of prophecy—inner sight—as a compensation.</note>

### In the book on abstinence from animals <note>original: "In libro de animalium abstinentia." This refers to the work by the Neoplatonist philosopher Porphyry, who argues for vegetarianism and often quotes older Greek customs.</note>

We were once robust youths. <note>The first part of a traditional Spartan choral song sung by the elderly.</note>
But we shall be much better. <note>Sung by the children, representing the future potential of the state.</note>
And we are so now—if you wish, look back. <note>Sung by the young men in their prime.</note>
Long ago the Milesians were brave. <note>original: "Iam olim erant fortes Milesij." A proverbial expression used to describe those who have fallen from their former glory or to mock those who boast of past ancestors rather than present deeds.</note>

A great contest was proposed long ago concerning the Muses
of Theodorus Gaza, both the Italian and the Greek ones. <note>Theodorus Gaza (c. 1398–1475) was a primary figure in the revival of Greek learning in Italy. "Ausonian" refers to Italy, and "Heliconian" refers to the Greek home of the Muses.</note>
To the latter he owed his birth; to the former, the rewards of his upbringing.
For Greece brought him forth, but Italy nurtured him.
In both, his wisdom and his tongue excelled equally.
Neither while living nor certainly in death did he settle this dispute,
But he wished to lie in Great Greece, <note><term>Great Greece</term>: "Magna Graecia," the historical name for the coastal areas of Southern Italy which were extensively populated by Greek settlers in antiquity.</note>
So that the glory might be shared by both.

# INTERPRETATION OF THE GREEKS

<note>The original OCR indicates a section of text in Greek script here, which the following Latin text translates into English below.</note>

To the Tribes. <note>original: "Tribubus." This likely refers to the "Demoi" (The Tribes or The Demes), a lost play by the comic poet Eupolis, in which he praises the famous Athenian statesman Pericles.</note>
This man was the most excellent of all men at speaking;
Whenever he entered the assembly, just like champion runners,
He would outdistance the other orators by sixteen feet while speaking.
He was swift in speech indeed, for upon that very speed,
A certain **Persuasion** <note>original: "Pithò" (Peitho), the Greek personification of charm and seduction in speech.</note> sat upon his lips.
In this way, he worked his charm, and alone among the orators,
He left his sting remaining in those who heard him.
Truly, upon his lips alone did Persuasion sit.
Golden things for bronze, those worth a hundred oxen for those worth nine, <note>This is a famous reference to Homer’s *Iliad* (Book 6), describing an exchange between Glaucus and Diomedes where Glaucus foolishly trades his gold armor for bronze armor.</note>
And so it happened.

->THE END<-
ANG. <note>Likely an abbreviation for Angelus Politianus (Angelo Poliziano), the renowned Renaissance humanist and translator who often compiled these types of Greek fragments.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the collection of Greek poetic fragments and their interpretations to the formal preface of Angelo Poliziano's "Miscellanea." The previous page ended with a tribute to Pericles and a Homeric proverb; this page begins the primary dedication of the new work to his patron, Lorenzo de' Medici.</meta>

<note>A decorative ornament is centered at the top of the page.</note>

-># PREFACE OF ANGELO POLIZIANO'S<-
-># FIRST CENTURY OF MISCELLANIES<-
-># TO LORENZO DE' MEDICI<-

<note>A large decorative initial 'C' contains an illustration of a rabbit or hare, a motif often used in Renaissance printing to denote speed or as a playful visual element.</note> **W**HEN, in days past, Lorenzo de' Medici, I was reciting these *Miscellanies* of ours to you while we were riding, I believe you were delighted by the very novelty of the subjects and the quite charming variety of the reading. You began to urge me to publish at least one "Century" from them (for each individual book is explained in one hundred chapters).

This is what I now do, led partly by your authority—than which no greater exists for me on earth—and partly by reason. I do so because certain individuals have already swept up <note>original: "cum puluisculo conuerrunt." A Latin idiom meaning to sweep something up completely, including the dust; here Poliziano uses it to suggest that others have been "sweeping up" his research and notes to claim them as their own.</note> these writings of mine, so much so that they take far too much pleasure in claiming the title of my labors for themselves and others. 

Although I know these books will face great envy and be the subject of much gossip, as those who [treat of]...
                                                                      A

<summary>Angelo Poliziano begins the preface to his "Miscellanies," dedicated to his patron Lorenzo de' Medici, explaining that he has decided to publish the work to protect his intellectual property from plagiarists.</summary>
<keywords>Angelo Poliziano, Lorenzo de' Medici, Miscellanea, Preface, Plagiarism, Renaissance Humanism, Philology, Centuria</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues Poliziano’s defense of his scholarly method. He distinguishes himself from critics who attack others to gain fame, asserting his own sincerity and commitment to truth over reputation.</meta>

...authors of **great name** <note>original: "magni nominis"</note> pronounce freely. But that is not our example. For almost all writers defend their own positions in such a way that they attack those of others, and they usually seek an opportunity for praise from the errors of others. Furthermore, even those prominent figures (if I may call them that) and certain leaders of letters are often criticized by those who are more insignificant and common, or of the lowest rank—such as myself, or a **Cluvienus** <note>Cluvienus was a famously bad poet mentioned by the Roman satirist Juvenal; Poliziano uses the name here as a self-deprecating joke.</note>.

However, there is an old saying: **"Conscience is a thousand witnesses"** <note>original: "Conscientia mille testes."</note>. It knows that absolutely nothing here was given out of hatred, nothing from spite; we have brought forth everything candidly and simply, nothing either aggressively or malevolently, nothing from a desire for sniping, nothing from a wish for showing off. We have always been more inclined toward approving (if it were allowed) than toward disapproving. 

Nor, evidently, have we conducted this business with tricks or **hidden tunnels** <note>original: "strophis aut cuniculis." 'Cuniculis' literally refers to underground mines or rabbit burrows, used here metaphorically for deceitful or subversive scholarly methods.</note>, but with free examination and the open face of truth. And we did not seek this—to cast some small stain upon learned men—but rather we took care lest the faith of students be endangered under their authority. 

Finally, this **wedge** is aimed explicitly at those, this **battering ram** is intentionally tempered against those, who harden their brows against the face of truth in favor of their own local, yet imaginary, sense. We prick most sharply with the point of our pen those who have not learned to **"turn the stylus"** <note>original: "stilum vertere." This refers to the Roman practice of using the flat end of a metal stylus to smooth over wax and erase mistakes; Poliziano means those who have not learned to correct their own errors.</note>—that is, those who have not learned to strike out their own mistakes. 

But as for this disordered and confused collection, to be called a "forest" <note>original: "silvam." A traditional term for a collection of raw materials or miscellaneous poems.</note> or a **medley** <note>original: "farraginem." Literally a mix of grain for animal feed, used to describe a literary miscellany.</note>, because it is not [composed] continuously...

<summary>Poliziano defends his "Miscellanies" against potential critics, asserting that his work is driven by a sincere search for truth rather than a desire to disparage other scholars. He emphasizes the importance of self-correction and warns against blindly following authority.</summary>
<keywords>literary criticism, scholarship, intellectual honesty, Cluvienus, stylus, truth, Miscellanies, Angelo Poliziano</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Poliziano’s defense of his scholarly method. Having established his sincerity in the previous page, he now justifies the "miscellaneous" and fragmented nature of his work by citing the authority of classical Greek and Latin precedents.</meta>

# OF MISCELLANIES.

...and continuously, but we write by leaps and by snatches <note>original: "saltuatim... uellicatim." Poliziano describes his non-linear, episodic style of scholarship.</note>, so far are we from grieving over this that we have sought no other title than *Miscellanies*. In this, we follow the Greek Aelian <margin>Aelian.</margin> <note>Claudius Aelianus was a 2nd-century Roman who wrote in Greek; his *Varia Historia* is a collection of anecdotes and natural history.</note> and the Latin Gellius <margin>Gellius.</margin> <note>Aulus Gellius, author of the *Attic Nights*, a foundational "miscellany" of grammar, philosophy, and history.</note>, both of whose books are more charming <margin>Variety</margin> for their variety <margin>is charming.</margin> than for their strict order. Although even the commentaries of Clement of Alexandria <margin>Clement</margin>, a bishop and apostolic man, were titled *Stromateis* <margin>of Alexandria.</margin> <note>original: "Στρωματεῖς." Clement's *Stromata* (Patchworks/Tapestries) was an early Christian work written in a deliberate "miscellany" style to prevent uninitiated readers from easily grasping secret truths.</note>—which you might call "embroidered tapestries"—they certainly suggested no other title to us than that of this very variety. Indeed, I am silent regarding the commentaries of Aristoxenus <note>A Greek philosopher and pupil of Aristotle, primarily known for his work on music.</note>, which Porphyry <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote a commentary on Ptolemy's *Harmonics*.</note> cites with a nearly identical title in that volume which he composed on the *Harmonics* of Ptolemy. Finally, if variety itself—the expeller of boredom and the provoker of reading—is to be blamed in these *Miscellanies*, then by the same effort the nature of things itself might be rebuked; of which nature I profess myself a disciple through such diversity. 

But if these writings descend into every little detail <note>original: "minutiem." Poliziano is defending his focus on "small" philological points like grammar or punctuation.</note>, this is indeed permitted among the Latins by the example of Caesar, Varro, Messalla, Cicero, Pliny, Quintilian, and others like them, whom it is honorable even to err in imitating. Therefore, what was fitting for those "patricians" <note>original: "patricios." He refers to the great Roman authors as the nobility of literature.</note>, as it were, should not be unbecoming for me, who am of the "lesser clans" <note>original: "minorum gentium." A Roman term for the secondary tier of the Senate; Poliziano uses it to humbly position himself as a minor scholar.</note>. A similar fame of names either inserts Greek phrases or interprets them at convenience, and that now according to the sense, now according to the word—both of which occur here. Now if to anyone any parts seem insufficiently explained, perhaps even too harsh...

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;A  ij

<meta>This page continues Poliziano’s defense of his literary style in the *Miscellanea*. The text begins mid-word, completing the thought from the previous page regarding those who might find his work difficult or obscure.</meta>

# PREFACE

...[ob]scure, certainly he would be of neither a sufficiently lively mind, nor of solid and reliable learning. If in some places certain barbaric and obsolete words are found, we have indeed scattered them intentionally, even so that we might please the barbaric and unlearned, and that the book might become more saleable with its popularity gathered from all sides <note>Poliziano is being slightly sarcastic here, suggesting that he includes "barbaric" (non-Classical) Latin terms partly to appeal to a wider audience, but also to show the breadth of his vocabulary.</note>. If some say that words are found here too remote to be believed as taken from common usage, I think they will change their opinion once they have advanced as far as the middle of their reading. For there are some to whom even (as Marcus Tullius <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman orator and statesman whose Latin style was considered the gold standard of the Renaissance.</note> says) an alabaster jar full of ointment would seem to stink. 

Then, in writings of this kind—which do not hawk themselves to the common people, but are prepared only for the few—the use of such recondite materials, especially when modest, is by no means disapproved of by good men. Nor indeed should it be a shame to renew those things which have now almost faded away, provided that these things themselves <margin>To grow old.</margin> <note>original: "Vetustesco." To reach old age.</note> cannot yet be said to grow old, but can grow old <margin>To become old.</margin> <note>original: "Veterasco." To become long-established or rooted. Poliziano is making a subtle grammatical distinction: he wishes to revive ancient words so they can become "long-established" in the reader's mind once again, rather than simply "growing old" and dying out.</note> anew. 

If, meanwhile, some people are offended by certain turns of phrase that are perhaps less familiar to a man who has read only ten pages of Cicero and nothing else, I certainly appeal from him to the learned; I believe they will especially praise the things that he will disapprove of. Perhaps nothing is more intolerable than for an unlearned man to pass judgment on you, who nevertheless seems to himself most learned. If someone, when a point is refuted, desires a mass of either authorities or reasons, let him know that victory [is claimed] by us in that place...

<summary>In this preface, Poliziano defends his use of obscure and archaic vocabulary, arguing that such "recondite" language is appropriate for scholarly works intended for an elite audience rather than the masses. He playfully rebukes critics who only value a narrow range of Ciceronian Latin, appealing instead to the judgment of truly learned scholars.</summary>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues Poliziano’s defense of his literary style. He is arguing that the variety and lack of strict structure in his Miscellanea are intentional design choices rather than flaws, designed to keep the reader engaged through contrast.</meta>

MISCELLANIES

...not to seek the satisfaction of victory. If some chapters are thought to be a bit long, and others again a bit short, let us believe this is also the law of this new work: that it should have nothing uniform, be equal to itself nowhere, always limp with dissimilarity, and uphold this one rule: that nothing be recalled to a rule <note>original: "regulam"</note>, nor to a plumb line and level. <note>original: "perpendiculum, libellamque." Poliziano uses metaphors from masonry and construction to argue that his writing should not be perfectly square or mechanical, but organic and varied.</note> If a freer style is judged to be of an insolent nature, perhaps a kinder interpreter will attribute it instead to a simplicity which does not disgrace high-born men. Furthermore, wherever custom <margin>Custom.</margin> resists, then truth—which does not make trifles—or a better custom acting as a surrogate for truth, will defend it. For that which is accepted against authority is said to be "taken by custom," and an error of custom is often charged by the learned. Then someone said beautifully thus: "Custom without truth is the antiquity of error." <note>This is a famous quote from Saint Cyprian of Carthage (Epistle 74), a 3rd-century bishop. Renaissance humanists frequently used this phrase to justify overturning long-held medieval traditions in favor of rediscovered "truths" from antiquity.</note> Finally, if we have occasionally sprinkled in a few things that are thorny and anxious, or more truly "sub-acid" <note>original: "subacida." Poliziano uses a culinary metaphor; his writing is like a tart or acidic dish that cleanses the palate after too much sweetness.</note>, whether from philosophy—for which we have long been candidates—or from that circle of disciplines <note>original: "orbe illo disciplinarum." This is the Latin translation of the Greek *enkyklios paideia*, from which we get the word "encyclopedia." It refers to the complete circle of the liberal arts.</note> which serve the study of wisdom, yet those things will perhaps refresh and sharpen the reader's stomach, which is wilting from over-sweetness. For in order that all these things might harmonize in their pleasing turn, and be allied as if by a certain antithesis, we have run almost "from the lime-kiln into the charcoal-works" <margin>Proverb.</margin> <note>original: "de calcaria in carbonariam." A Latin proverb, similar to "out of the frying pan into the fire," but here used to describe a sudden shift from one extreme subject to another.</note> (as the saying goes), and we have taken care that even opposites should follow one another in turn, so that for us these complaints...

<summary>Poliziano justifies the irregular chapter lengths and varied difficulty of his work, arguing that variety prevents reader fatigue. He invokes the principle that "custom without truth" is merely an old error to defend his scholarly departures from tradition.</summary>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues Poliziano’s defense of the varied and "uneven" style of his Miscellanea. Having previously argued that truth and custom justify his choices, he now explains that his work aims to please a wide variety of readers by mixing rough and polished elements.</meta>

# PREFACE

...modes. Nor indeed do we permit those things to be lacking among ourselves which are more pleasant and delightful—not to say alluring—rather than merely useful or necessary. After all, as Varro <note>Marcus Terentius Varro (116–27 BCE) was a prolific Roman scholar. Humanists like Poliziano revered him as a primary authority on the Latin language and ancient traditions.</note> says: one thing is enough for a man, <margin>Man. Humanity.</margin> but another is required for "humanity" <term>humanitas: the Roman ideal of refined culture, liberal arts, and the civilizing education that distinguishes humans from animals.</term>, even when there is no immediate profit to be gained. Meanwhile, beautiful things are sought after, by Hercules, no less than a profit stripped of beauty.

Therefore, just as some of these unpolished and rough parts will delight those rustic and "shaggy" <note>original: "hircosos," literally "goat-like" or "smelling of goats." Poliziano uses this to describe readers with a primitive or unrefined taste who prefer a "natural," unworked style over modern elegance.</note> readers—passages more roughly-hewn than finely-carved, having felt neither the file, nor the plane, nor the rasp—so, on the other hand, a style that is occasionally "mosaic-like" <note>original: "uermiculata dictio," referring to *opus vermiculatum*, a highly intricate form of mosaic work. In a literary sense, it means prose that is carefully woven together from many small, precious fragments of ancient texts.</note> and variegated with many-colored tiles will captivate those more delicate sorts, the smooth and polished. Thus, by blending these styles from both sides, I hope that I am neither raised to the heavens by uniform applause nor cast to the ground by a universal hiss.

For those who stand in the middle, marked by neither extreme, a certain "Medley" <note>original: "Cinnus." Cicero used this term to describe a stylistic hodgepodge or a mixed drink. Poliziano uses it to justify his "Miscellany" format.</note> of diverse elements will satisfy; it excels in no single part, yet participates in all. For not everyone has the same taste; each has their own palate. Indeed, I believe some at a banquet will spit out a dish as being distasteful or meager, only to lick it up greedily in the kitchen later as something sweet and rich. 

We have certainly prepared these things not for the public forum or the senate-house, but for the bedroom and the school. To that end, we have strived—though I know not if we have succeeded—that whatever is found here should be entirely easy and simple in its elegance, not laborious and painted allurements. It should possess a color and brilliance that is true and natural, not feigned or from artifice...

<summary>Poliziano explains that his work intentionally mixes rough and refined styles to satisfy different types of readers, from the unpolished to the sophisticated. He defines his writing as a "cinnus" (medley) intended for private study rather than public oratory.</summary>
<keywords>literary style, humanitas, Varro, Cicero, audience variety, prose aesthetics, Miscellanea, mosaic style</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Poliziano’s preface to his Miscellanea. Following his discussion on blending various styles to please different readers, he now addresses the potential for criticism and the moral duty of the scholar to prioritize the truth of "letters" (literature and philology) over personal reputation or the fear of making enemies.</meta>

-># OF THE MISCELLANY<-

...a well-fitted habit, and unencumbered, not burdensome or excessive. Finally, in whatever manner these writings go forth, or in whatever way they are received, we certainly do not refuse the outcome; yet neither do I think we shall escape that common lot of writers—to be spoken ill of—provided it so pleases the readers. <margin>Whatever sort.</margin> Indeed, we expose our head to this roll of the dice, nor do we look for any defense, which we have voluntarily denied ourselves by our own judgment. Let judgment rather be passed against us; let us be traduced and harassed, rather than allow the public deception regarding letters <note>Poliziano is referring to the "fraud" of poor scholarship or corrupted texts that mislead the public; he believes it is better for him to be criticized than for academic errors to go uncorrected.</note>—whose honor is more precious to us than our own advantages—to not be redeemed, even at some cost to our own reputation or leisure. For to what end, I ask, is this profession of our common studies, if matters have come to such a point that we allow all the duty we owe to universal learning to cease because of some ignoble suspicion? By what means can the integrity of honorable letters endure any longer or even exist, if all critical judgment is utterly abandoned, while we all equally dread envy? <margin>Envy.</margin>

Although (as far as I am concerned), I wish that my labor might be met with contempt rather than envy. For that is almost my wish, if only what Livy <note>Titus Livius (59 BCE – 17 CE), the great Roman historian. Poliziano is citing a sentiment found in Livy’s history regarding the tendency of jealousy to target those at the top.</note> says is true: "Envy, like fire, seeks the high places." <note>original: "Inuidia uelut ignis alta petit."</note> Come then, best cultivators of studies, each one eager for his part: tear up this invincible weed by which the good fruits of humanity <term>humanitas: the Renaissance ideal of "the humanities," referring to the cultural and moral refinement gained through the study of classical Greek and Roman literature.</term> are being choked. Tear away those ivies which, as if most ruinous, grow up among the sacred trunks and [drain] all their lifeblood...

<meta>This page continues Poliziano’s preface. Having established that he values the integrity of scholarship over his own reputation, he now turns his focus to the "parasites" and "interpolators" who corrupt ancient texts, declaring his intention to expose these scholarly frauds regardless of the public's reaction.</meta>

# PREFACE

...[parasitic] <note>The OCR begins mid-word; based on context, Poliziano is likely referring to *parasiticis*, those who feed off the work of others.</note> consumers, break them off entirely, and cut them away. There is no lack of those who corrupt the truth; let there also be no lack of those who restore it. I beg that some champion and protector of the endangered disciplines arise. For I, as one of the common people, will certainly try what is permitted to me. Whether I shall achieve anything worthwhile, I will see later. However it turns out, or wherever the matter falls, I will take it in good part. I will not only swallow the poison of all malice and detraction, but (given my constitution) I will even digest it, certain that it is almost the nature of truth, as soon as it appears in public, to be hateful and hostile to the common crowd. Therefore, although others may turn a blind eye and <margin>Mutter: they dare not mumble openly.</margin> hesitate to speak the truth, I alone (whatever happens) will not conceal my judgment, I will not suppress what I feel, nor will I indulge such people with patience any longer; rather, I will whisper to this little book of mine, at least with some freedom, as follows:

<margin>From Persius. <note>A reference to the Roman satirist Aulus Persius Flaccus, known for his critiques of societal corruption.</note></margin>

I have seen, I myself have seen, little book—and daily I see—many capital crimes committed in literature: foreign works craftily plundered, things fabricated according to whim, and then attributed to suitable authors who would not even recognize them. I see authors cited who do not exist, and manuscripts cited as ancient that appear nowhere. I see all books filled with the most laborious vanities—falsehoods instead of truths, adopted things instead of native, novelties instead of the vernacular. Everything is substituted, polluted, corrupted, smeared over, encrusted, distorted, confounded, thrown into ruin, and subverted, with no faith, no [shame...] <note>The Latin text cuts off at *nullo*, likely intending *nullo pudore* (with no shame).</note>

<summary>Poliziano issues a fierce polemic against the scholarly corruption of his day, vowing to act as a "restorer" of truth against those who forge, plagiarize, and distort ancient manuscripts.</summary>

# MISCELLANIES

...[exist,] having neither shame nor judgment; and what is more ruinous than all of these is that, through the opportunity of the recent craft <note>The "recent craft" (*recens artificium*) is a common 15th-century reference to the invention of the printing press, which Poliziano suggests allows errors to spread faster than ever before.</note>, even the most foolish opinions are propagated in an instant into a thousand offshoots of volumes. Finally (to put it plainly), even now many men possess the ears of an ass. <note>original: *auriculas habere asini*. This continues the allusion to the legend of King Midas from the previous page's quote of Persius, implying that many readers and critics lack the "ears" to distinguish true scholarship from noise.</note> At this point, someone might say: "But what about you?"

Indeed, I confess that I can be mistaken, being only human; but I certainly deceive no one knowingly, and though I might perhaps state a falsehood, I certainly do not lie. <note>Poliziano makes a classic rhetorical distinction: a "falsehood" (*mendacium*) is an objective error, whereas "lying" (*mentiri*) involves the subjective intent to deceive. He admits he might be wrong, but never dishonest.</note> "Then I forgive myself," says Mevius <note>Mevius was a notoriously bad poet in Roman tradition, often paired with Bavius as a symbol of incompetence.</note>; on the contrary, however (all joking aside), I do not even forgive myself, and if I should put forward anything too unlearned or careless, I desire to be refuted by anyone, being ready to refute myself if I am corrected. <margin>Aulus Gellius <note>Poliziano cites the 2nd-century author of *Attic Nights*, whose work served as the primary model for this genre of "miscellaneous" scholarly notes.</note></margin> For I do not love myself so perversely that I would rather others remain in error than that my own error be brought to light. Rather, I am of that number who write while making progress, and make progress by writing. <note>This is a famous sentiment borrowed from St. Augustine (*Epistle 143*), emphasizing that scholarship is a journey of continuous learning rather than a finished state of perfection.</note> As for that matter, let fortune see to it. This much is most true...

<summary>Poliziano critiques the printing press for rapidly spreading scholarly errors and asserts that his own work is a process of continuous learning, inviting correction rather than claiming perfection.</summary>

->PREFACE<-

...[considered], and written on the spur of the moment rather than with labor, although he attends to the business of style and polishes his expression almost to excess, whom ancient truth and good faith especially concern. Indeed, lest idle men <note>original: *maleferiati*, literally "badly holidaying" or "misusing their leisure," referring to those who spend their time in frivolous criticism.</note> think that we have drawn these things, whatever they are, from the dregs, or that we have overstepped the bounds of the grammarians, we have immediately prefixed the names of the authors after the example of Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder famously provided a list of sources for each book of his *Natural History*, a practice Poliziano adopts here to prove his scholarly rigor.</note>, but only those who are honorable and ancient, from whom these matters derive their authority and from whom we have borrowed <note>original: *versuram fecimus*. A financial metaphor for taking a loan, suggesting he is "borrowing" the wisdom of the ancients.</note>. Moreover, these are not authors whom others have merely cited while their own works have perished over time, but those whose very treasures we ourselves have handled and through whose letters we have traveled.

Furthermore, the antiquity of manuscripts, the reliability of coins, and the antiquities carved in bronze or marble—which you, Lorenzo, have supplied to us—also greatly support our commentaries, in addition to the variety of books. In order that we might teach by example that the tricks and impostures of charlatans <note>original: *circulatorum*. This refers to itinerant peddlers or street performers, here used as a metaphor for dishonest scholars who "perform" knowledge they don't possess.</note> must be abolished, and that the license of certain people—which now spreads far and wide like a cancer, of straying idly and freely from what is prescribed—must be restrained, we have never cited the testimony of ancient writing in such a way that anything added or superimposed might prevail. Rather, we have represented everything in its own likeness and applied those notes from which authority can be sought out thoroughly, right from the root. And so that no [expectations] at all of this task of ours...

->**MISCELLANIES**<-

...lest appendices <margin>Appendix.</margin> be missed, we have even gathered the very headings of the matters, enclosed in the shortest titles, but in which the utility is greater than their number <gloss>feminine gender</gloss> <note>The OCR reads "fœm. gen.", likely a grammatical gloss for "utilitas" (utility), though in many editions this is "foeneris," meaning "profit" or "interest."</note>. And by a certain balancing turn, just as we shall provide the former with something to kiss, so we shall provide in abundance for the latter something to gnaw upon.

Moreover, I think some certain rabid men will bite at our works—those whom <margin>Fault-finders.</margin> Cato elegantly calls "vitiligators" <note>original: *vitiligatores*. A term attributed to Cato the Elder, combining *vitium* (fault) and *litigator* (litigant) to describe those who find pleasure in malicious and petty criticism.</note>—chiefly because of that vice of litigiousness...

<meta>This page continues the preface of Poliziano’s *Miscellanea*. On the previous page, Poliziano described his intention to defend his scholarly work against critics and "barbarians," comparing his defense to Teucer hiding behind the shield of Ajax. Here, he describes the specific nature of his detractors—envious critics and "pulpit-critics" who reject classical learning.</meta>

->PREFACE<-

...not only a clearer day, but they cannot even bear a faint shadow. For no one doubts those whom the moth of malice consumes, whom the rust of envy gnaws away, who make another’s glory their own punishment and another’s happiness their own torture; who waste away at another's progress, and whom ill-will, sickening at the success of others, continually tortures like an internal executioner. But also those pulpit-critics <note>original: *censores pulpitarij*. Poliziano refers to conservative preachers or theologians who used their position in the pulpit to attack the new "humanist" interest in classical literature.</note> (though I am not speaking now of the learned and the good) are often accustomed to prod us and make a noise against these studies of ours. Indeed, full of barrenness, they defame the humanities before the ignorant common folk, I might almost say solemnly, with puffing cheeks <margin>Fierce little men.</margin> and with a coarse, rustic fierceness—the only thing which (as Jerome <note>St. Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), though a great scholar himself, was often cited by both sides of the humanist debate regarding the "holiness" of simple speech versus polished rhetoric.</note> says) they hold for holiness. Thus they rage against these studies and those who pursue them with bombastic proclamations, gnashing their teeth and foaming at the mouth, so that they clearly declare themselves to also disapprove of and almost hate those Greeks—truly holy souls—Basil, Chrysostom, the Gregories <note>Refers to the influential Greek Church Fathers: Basil the Great, John Chrysostom, Gregory of Nazianzus, and Gregory of Nyssa.</note>, as well as the Latins, Cyprian, Ambrose, Augustine, and Jerome himself, and others of that kind, the high priests of our religion, who were packed with the learning of all nations and languages, as if with the spoils of the Egyptians. <note>original: *opibus Aegyptiorum*. This is a common theological metaphor based on the Israelites taking gold from the Egyptians during the Exodus; it justifies "spoiling" pagan classical literature to enrich Christian thought.</note>

Because of this, your favorite, Mariano of Genazzano <note>Mariano da Genazzano (1450–1498) was a famous Augustinian friar and a rival of Savonarola; he was highly esteemed by Lorenzo de' Medici for his eloquence.</note>, seems all the more admirable to me; he is second to none in theology, and of all those we have heard preaching in the church, he is not only the wisest but also the most eloquent, with...

<summary>Poliziano continues his defense of humanist studies, attacking envious critics and "pulpit-critics" who claim that classical learning is contrary to holiness. He argues that the great Fathers of the Church were themselves masters of Greek and Latin "spoils."</summary>
<keywords>humanism vs. scholasticism, envy, Church Fathers, Mariano of Genazzano, rhetoric, spoils of the Egyptians, polemics</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues Poliziano’s defense of humanist studies. Having previously addressed the "pulpit-critics" who despise classical learning, he now argues for a balance between moral severity and the "poetic charms" of literature, asserting that scholarly work should be written for future generations rather than immediate praise.</meta>

->OF MISCELLANIES<-

...his eloquence is neither suspect to those people striving for virtue <note>original: *ad bonam frugem*, literally "toward good fruit," a Roman idiom for moral improvement.</note>—since it is commended by an incredible severity of life—nor, on the other hand, is it a gloomy or formidable austerity, since it does not spurn poetic charms and that beautiful variety, luster, and delights of literature. 

Therefore, just as wool, intended to drink in a true purple dye, is first treated with certain mordants <note>original: *medicamentis*. In ancient dyeing, these were substances used to prepare the fabric to hold the expensive purple pigment.</note>, so I believe minds ought to be cultivated by such literature and doctrines (as Cicero says in the *Hortensius* <note>The *Hortensius* was a famous, now lost, dialogue by Cicero that exhorted readers to the study of philosophy; it famously set St. Augustine on his path to conversion.</note>), and should be steeped and prepared to receive wisdom. 

Yet, despite those critics, I will not be loath either to polish with my pen whatever this work of my late-night studies <term>lucubrations<gloss>From the Latin *lucubratio*, meaning work performed by lamplight; a common term for scholarly effort produced during the night.</gloss></term> may be, or to test what is permitted against those <margin>He imitates Juvenal <note>Decimus Junius Juvenalis (late 1st/early 2nd century AD), a Roman satirist known for his savage critiques of social corruption and literary pretense.</note></margin> who conceive capital frauds in the business of letters. 

For I also forgive Philoxenus <note>Philoxenus of Cythera (c. 435–380 BC) was a poet imprisoned by the tyrant Dionysius for criticizing the ruler's bad poetry. When released to hear more poems, he allegedly said, "Take me back to the quarries," preferring prison to lying about the quality of the work.</note>, just as the same Cicero did when writing to Atticus, who preferred to return to prison rather than surrender his judgment—especially since it is fitting for writers to serve not the present age, but, as it were, eternity, just as Thucydides <note>The Greek historian Thucydides (c. 460–400 BC) described his work as a "possession for all time" rather than a piece for immediate competition.</note> also said. 

And for that reason, I think books themselves are called "monuments" <margin>Why books are called monuments.</margin> because they should look toward the memory of posterity—which will judge more incorruptibly—rather than to the foolish little favors of the present time. 

Therefore, so that this wickedness of those who corrupt everything indiscriminately may (if allowed) not only be extinguished but even crushed, and so that this worn-out and prostituted effrontery—this great hardness of face—does not set an example for the following centuries, for their...

<summary>Poliziano argues that classical literature serves as a necessary preparation for wisdom and justifies his scholarly "lucubrations" as works intended for the impartial judgment of posterity rather than the approval of his contemporaries.</summary>
<keywords>humanism, Cicero, Philoxenus, Thucydides, literary integrity, posterity, monuments, purple dye metaphor</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Lorenzo de' Medici. Poliziano is transitioning from a defense of his scholarly pursuits to an explanation of his methodology and his intent to protect his intellectual property from "plagiarists."</meta>

**PREFACE**

Having undertaken (I think) a beneficial endeavor, we in no way promise a mere taste based on this first installment. For those whom we now handle with a soft touch, whom we reach for with a light and slow arm, I think we will later press more firmly and urge onward; and being satisfied for a little while in the meantime with a preliminary skirmish <note>original: *praelusoria, uelitarique pugna*. This refers to the *velites*, the light-armed skirmishers of the Roman army who fought before the main battle lines engaged.</note>, soon, having gathered our strength, we will battle with the full force of our army. Moreover (since the tunic is closer than the cloak <note>original: *tunica propior pallio*. A common Latin proverb—similar to "charity begins at home"—suggesting that one must look after one's own interests first.</note>), we shall boldly and diligently reclaim our own works from plagiarists and petty thieves <note>original: *furunculis*, literally "little thieves" or "boils," here referring to literary scavengers who steal the work of others.</note>, and we will lay claim to them in a suit for freedom <note>original: *liberali causa manum*. In Roman law, this was a formal legal gesture used to assert that someone being held as a slave was actually a free person; here, Poliziano uses it to "emancipate" his stolen ideas.</note>. 

We wish only those things to be accepted as ours now which we ourselves, as originators, have hammered out with great labor and industry; we will never allow them to be coaxed away or extorted from us as long as it is permitted—our things, I say, not refuse, but pledges of our talent. For just as we leave these present writings in the public square like gifts <note>original: *missilium*. In the Roman circus, these were tokens or small gifts thrown by the Emperor to the crowd to be scrambled for.</note> to be snatched up and trampled, so we reclaim and defend those others; these we set forth, those we both acknowledge and take up. Yet we do so in such a way that we do not wish to imitate monkeys, who are said to admire their own offspring, however deformed, as if they were the most beautiful.

<margin>The love of monkeys for their young.</margin>

Nor, I think, will these studies of ours—though they are works of lighter effort, or more truly, "playthings"—bring any disgrace to you, Lorenzo de' Medici, to whom they are now dedicated. They are dedicated, moreover, not so much to prove me grateful for your favors or to repay my debt to you—for having had you as a helper and counselor—as to ensure they go forth under favorable omens, and so that in them, your...

<summary>Poliziano explains that this work is a preliminary skirmish before more intensive scholarly battles, while asserting his right to defend his original ideas from plagiarists. He concludes by dedicating the work to his patron, Lorenzo de' Medici, seeking his protection and favorable influence.</summary>
<keywords>literary property, plagiarism, Lorenzo de' Medici, philological method, Roman law metaphors, intellectual authorship</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page concludes the dedication to Lorenzo de' Medici. Having previously discussed his legal and moral right to reclaim his intellectual work, Poliziano now positions this specific volume within the broader context of the Florentine Renaissance, acknowledging other great scholars under Lorenzo's patronage.</meta>

# MISCELLANIES

...may be kept in memory, so that the book, gaining authority from this, may be commended by the fame of a great name. Then, since the whole sum of my studies is owed to him, this portion too shall rightly begin with him. And by Hercules, let these writings be like sweets <note>original: *bellaria*. These were dainties or desserts served at the end of a Roman banquet, following the main courses.</note>, served at the second course. For those weightier matters take the place and form of the main meal, such as those many things which either Marsilio Ficino <margin>Ficino</margin> (the interpreter of Plato) or Argyropylus of Byzantium <margin>Argyropylus</margin> (the interpreter of Aristotle) have gathered for you from the storehouse of philosophy. For concerning that man who is to be admired above all others—not Pico anymore, but (as I am accustomed to call him) rather the Phoenix, <margin>Pico della Mirandola</margin> who now nests in your laurel <note>original: *phœnice... in tua lauru nidificat*. A double pun: the mythical Phoenix was said to build its nest in a laurel tree, and "Lauru" (Laurel) is a poetic play on the name of his patron, Lorenzo de' Medici.</note>—my expectation is so great that I dare to sing along with Propertius: "Yield, you Roman writers; yield, you Greeks!" <note>original: *Cedite Romani scriptores, cedite Graij*. A famous line from the Roman poet Propertius (Elegies 2.34.65), originally used to announce that Virgil’s *Aeneid* would surpass all previous literature.</note> Farewell, most pleasant patron, and (as you are doing) continue to favor the learned, continue to rouse literature, continue to rescue the Latin language from neglect and the Greek from destruction, so that the memory of your name may be utterly protected from the injury of oblivion.

<summary>Poliziano concludes his dedication by modestly comparing his "Miscellanies" to a dessert following the "main course" of philosophy provided by Ficino and Argyropylus, while praising the young genius Pico della Mirandola.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, Marsilio Ficino, John Argyropylus, Pico della Mirandola, Propertius, Latin literature, Greek literature, Renaissance patronage</keywords>

| **Callistratus** <note>A distinguished Roman jurist of the 3rd century AD, known for his writings on judicial procedures.</note> | **Eusebius** <note>Eusebius of Caesarea, a 4th-century historian often called the "Father of Church History."</note> |

| ->**SACRED AUTHORS**<- | **Basil** <note>Basil of Caesarea, a 4th-century theologian and one of the most influential "Cappadocian Fathers."</note> |
| **Paul** <note>Either the Apostle Paul or Julius Paulus, a prolific 3rd-century Roman jurist; given the context of legal names, the latter is likely meant.</note> | **Theodoret** <note>Theodoret of Cyrus, a 5th-century influential theologian and historian of the Christian Church.</note> |

There are also other ancient wise men cited, of unknown authority, such as the writer of the *Poëmenicon* <note>original: "Poëmenicon"; likely a reference to "The Shepherd" (Poimandres) or a specific pastoral commentary</note>, and the commentators of Aristophanes, Xenophon, Aristides, Euripides, Callimachus, and Synesius. Finally, even the foreign <note>original: "barbari"</note> Hebrew and Chaldean authors are not without their own foundational contributions to these origins.

# CHAPTERS OF THE FIRST CENTURY
<note>In early modern scholarly works, a "Century" refers to a volume containing one hundred separate essays or observations.</note>

### CHAPTER I.
**Cicero** is defended from slander regarding the explanation of a new word found in **Aristotle**, which is <term>Endelechia</term> <note>original: "Endelechia"; a Greek term for "continuity" or "persistence," often confused in the Renaissance with "Entelecheia" (actuality/perfection).</note>, showing that the term is wrongly altered.

### CHAPTER II.
What the <term>crepidæ</term> <note>A type of Greek sandal or half-boot with a thick sole.</note> in the works of **Catullus** called *Carpatinæ* are; it is shown that this word is correctly written.

### CHAPTER III.
With what words **Horace** signified the <term>Chamelopardalis</term> <note>The archaic name for a giraffe, derived from the Greek for "camel-leopard."</note>, what the appearance of that animal is, by what names it is known, and the time it was first seen in Italy.

<summary>This page concludes the list of cited authorities, including legal and religious figures, and presents the first three chapters of the work, which address linguistic and natural history corrections in classical texts.</summary>
<keywords>Callistratus, Eusebius, Cicero, Aristotle, Endelechia, Catullus, Horace, giraffe, philology, classical commentary</keywords>

->**MISCELLANIES**<-

### CHAPTER IV.
How many things must be read by the interpreters of poets, and that **Persius** <note>A Roman satirist known for his complex and stoic-influenced poetry.</note> modeled his Satire <note>original: "Satyram"</note> about **Alcibiades** from **Plato**.

### CHAPTER V.
Who the **Duratean horse** <note>original: "Durateus equus"; the "wooden" horse of Troy, from the Greek 'dourateos'.</note> is in **Lucretius**. Also, that in **Valerius Flaccus**, one should read not indeed "Doric night," but "Duric," or rather "**Duria**," in the second book of the *Argonautica*.

### CHAPTER VI.
In what sense the **sparrow** of **Catullus** <note>The "passer" in Catullus’s poems is a famous subject of scholarly debate, often interpreted either as a literal pet or an erotic metaphor.</note> is to be understood, and a passage in **Martial** indicated as well.

### CHAPTER VII.
That **Juvenal** <note>A fierce Roman satirist of the late 1st century AD.</note> implies **pathic** <note>term: "pathicos" - a term used in Roman antiquity to describe men who took a passive role in sexual acts, often used as a social or moral critique.</note> men are those who "scratch their head with one finger," <note>A Roman idiom suggesting a man was too concerned with his hairstyle to use his whole hand, thereby signaling effeminacy.</note> and this is from the verses of the poet **Calvus**.

### CHAPTER VIII.
Why the names of the **wandering stars** <note>The planets.</note> were

<column-break/>

[Right Column]
assigned to the **days**, and the reasons for their order therein, which are pleasant to know and to relate.

### CHAPTER IX.
What should be believed in the writings of **Domitius**, <note>Likely Domitius Calderini, a 15th-century humanist scholar whom the author frequently critiques.</note> and to what extent; and concerning his evident and shameful error regarding the word ***Attegius***. <note>A rare word referring to a type of hut or cottage.</note>

### CHAPTER X.
Concerning the **Baptae** and **Cottyto**, <note>The Baptae were priests of Cottyto, a Thracian goddess whose secret nocturnal rites were notorious for their perceived immorality.</note> and concerning the **Cottytia** [festivals]; then the correct reading and explanation indicated in **Juvenal** and **Horace**.

### CHAPTER XI.
An opinion on **Claudian** <note>A late Latin poet (c. 370–404 AD).</note> set forth by the telling of a very charming little story. Then another story, no less sweet, likewise translated from the **Greek**, regarding **Venus and the rose**.

### CHAPTER XII.
How **purple dye** <note>The famous Tyrian purple, a status symbol in the ancient world derived from sea snails.</note> was discovered, and at the same time a passage of the Greek poet **Nonnus** explained.

Regarding "With a companion mule," which most read as "With a companion hunchback" <note>original: "Gibbo"; likely a reference to a specific person or character name that was confused with the word for mule.</note>; at the same time, the reasoning in it is explained, and the truer meaning is given.
**Chapter XXIV.**
Certain things recorded concerning the old woman Hecale <note>A character from a lost poem by Callimachus who famously offered hospitality to the hero Theseus.</note>, and regarding the poetry of Callimachus <note>A highly influential Hellenistic Greek poet and scholar at the Library of Alexandria.</note>; then a passage explained from the *Priapeia* epigrams <note>A collection of ancient Latin poems dedicated to the fertility god Priapus.</note>, and likewise another in Statius <note>Publius Papinius Statius, a Roman poet of the 1st century AD.</note>, and what is incorrectly read concerning it in the manuscripts of Apuleius <note>The Roman author of the famous novel *The Golden Ass*.</note>.
**Chapter XXV.**
How many things in the *Letters to Friends* <note>original: "epistolis familiaribus"; one of the four main collections of Marcus Tullius Cicero’s personal correspondence.</note>, which are now attributed to Cicero, are out of order. Then, into what order they should be restored.
**Chapter XXVI.**
Little verses in the *Fasti* <note>Ovid's poetic calendar of the Roman year, which explains the origins of various festivals.</note> taken from the Greek concerning the vine and the goat; then a certain obscure parody in Suetonius <note>A Roman historian known for his biographies of the Caesars.</note>.
**Chapter XXVII.**

<column-break/>

[Right Column]
Words in a letter of Cicero taken from the *Medea* of Ennius <note>Quintus Ennius, an early Roman poet who translated and adapted Greek tragedies into Latin.</note>, which was itself taken from the Greek of Euripides <note>One of the three great Athenian tragedians of the 5th century BC.</note>.
**Chapter XXVIII.**
Why "Panic terrors" <note>original: "Panici terrores"; sudden, overwhelming fears attributed by the ancients to the god Pan.</note> are so called, and the most reliable witnesses cited for it.
**Chapter XXIX.**
From where come the "Teuthrantian crowd" <note>Teuthras was a legendary king of Mysia in Greek myth.</note> and the "daughters called of Thespius" <note>Thespius was a king who famously had fifty daughters, all of whom bore sons to the hero Heracles.</note> in Ovid.
**Chapter XXX.**
Words rare to find, yet not unique: *Cucuma* <note>term: "Cucuma" - a vessel for boiling water; a kettle.</note>, *Proseuca* <note>term: "Proseuca" - a Greek word for a place of prayer or a synagogue.</note>, *Scruta* <note>term: "Scruta" - a collection of junk, rags, or secondhand goods.</note>.
**Chapter XXXI.**
What that phrase in Juvenal <note>A Roman satirist known for his critiques of Roman society.</note> means, "As if you had three names" <note>original: "Tanq̃ habeas tria nomina"; referring to the *tria nomina* (praenomen, nomen, and cognomen) which were a mark of full Roman citizenship.</note>, and that those manumitted <note>The legal process of freeing a slave.</note> took the names of their patrons for themselves; and regarding adoption into a name by a will.
**Chapter XXXII.**
Restored to the manuscripts of Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, known as Pliny the Elder, author of the encyclopedic *Natural History*.</note>.

An epigram of the same poet [Ausonius] similar to a riddle, cleansed of errors and interpreted.

That in the *Digests* of the Emperor Justinian <note>The "Digesta," a massive collection of Roman legal juristic writings compiled in the 6th century.</note>, "diffused day" <note>original: "diem diffusum"</note> is wrongly read instead of "deferred day" <note>original: "diffisum"; a legal term for a day on which a case is postponed.</note>, just as in Gellius "to be spread" and "definitions" are read instead of "to be split"...

**Chapter XLII.**
That thumbs were pressed down to show favor, just as they were usually turned to deny favor; and from this, the meanings of passages in Horace, Juvenal, and Prudentius are clarified.
**Chapter XLIII.**
How many diphthongs <note>A sound formed by the combination of two vowels in a single syllable.</note> the ancients possessed.
**Chapter XLIIII.**
That "Pegasean nectar" <note>original: "Pegaseium nectar"; referring to the fountain of the Muses created by the hoof of the winged horse Pegasus.</note> ought to be read in the works of Persius, not "melody" <note>original: "melos".</note>.
**Chapter XLV.**
That Achilles was younger than Patroclus, contrary to what either Aeschylus recorded or what is commonly believed.
**Chapter XLVI.**
That *cacoethes* <note>original: "cacoëthes"; a Greek term used by Juvenal to describe an "insatiable itch" or "malignant habit," specifically the "itch for writing" (*scribendi cacoethes*).</note> in Juvenal is four syllables, not *cacoë-

thos*. And that one should read "Lyde with the stored-away box," not "disordered." At the same time, a passage in Martianus [Capella] is explained, I believe, with no small amount of diligence.
**Chapter XLVII.**
What Pliny recorded regarding the "pending" titles <note>original: "titulis... pendentibus"; referring to how ancient artists signed their work in the imperfect tense (e.g., "was making" rather than "made") to suggest the work was always a work-in-progress.</note> of ancient craftsmen, and how these are also recognized on the old monuments in Rome; likewise, something concerning the appearance of the Virgilian aegis <note>The shield or breastplate of Athena/Jupiter described in Virgil's *Aeneid*.</note>.
**Chapter XLVIII.**
That others wrote about Megabyzus what Pliny wrote about Alexander [the Great]: that he was accustomed to frequently visit the workshop of Apelles <note>The most famous painter of ancient Greece.</note>.

A competition of epigrams between the Greek Posidippus and the Latin Ausonius concerning the statue of Opportunity <note>original: "Occasionis imagine"; referring to the deity Kairos or Occasio, often depicted with a forelock of hair but bald behind to show she must be grasped as she approaches.</note>; also, a most beautiful <term>ecphrasis<gloss>a literary description of a work of visual art</gloss></term> by the Greek Callistratus.
