<meta>This page continues the introduction to John Argyropoulos, the Greek scholar who taught the Medici family. It transitions from praising his role as a teacher to discussing his controversial criticisms of the Roman orator Cicero, which serves as the primary subject of this chapter.</meta>

BOOK

the glory of us all, heir to ancestral virtue and the pillar of the Republic. This man [Argyropoulos] possessed great authority and favor, to which he even dedicated the books of Aristotle that he had translated into Latin; he also imbued the young **Lorenzo**—then barely an adolescent—with dialectics <note>the art of reasoning and logic</note> and that part of philosophy which deals with ethics. However, being a Greek man, he bore with a resentful spirit that famous and (as Theodore Gaza <note>A 15th-century Greek humanist and translator of Aristotle</note> thinks) not inappropriate exclamation by **Marcus Tullius Cicero**, in which he pronounced that Greece was sometimes lacking in words—a point on which Greece considers itself abundant. Cicero’s statement was perhaps no more eloquent than it was true. For this reason, the Greek master, somewhat angered by the father and prince of Latin eloquence <note>referring to Cicero</note>, even dared to repeatedly claim—something that even now ears can hardly tolerate—that Cicero was ignorant not only of philosophy but also (heaven help us!) of Greek literature. For it can hardly be expressed how reluctantly that nation [the Greeks] sometimes admits us—the Latin people—into the sharing of their language and learning. They believe that we possess the refuse <note>original: "quisquilias"; literally dregs or waste</note> of letters, while they have the fruit; that we have the trimmings, while they have the body; that we have the shells, while they have the kernel. 

But to return to Argyropoulos: while he was impressing these views upon the vision of our parent <note>referring to Lorenzo de' Medici’s father or grandfather, who supported Argyropoulos</note> during his life, he used this particular argument as his strongest proof: that in the first book of the *Tusculan Disputations*, Cicero writes that Aristotle believed there was a certain "fifth nature" beyond the four elements from which the mind is composed. Finally, Cicero’s own words are read as follows: *He adds a fifth kind, lacking...* <note>original: "Quintum genus adhibet uacans"; this begins a quote from Cicero's "Tusculan Disputations" 1.10, where he discusses Aristotle’s concept of the 'entelecheia' or the fifth element of the soul.</note>

MISCELLANIES. 2

...lacking a name, and so he <note>Referring to Cicero</note> calls the soul itself *Endelecheia* <note>original: "Endelechian"; a term implying "continuity"</note> by a new name, as if it were a certain continuous and perennial motion. <margin>continuity</margin> <note>original: "ἐνδελέχεια"</note> But indeed, there is no one (he [Argyropoulos] said) who is even slightly familiar with the reading of Aristotle who does not know that <margin>perfection</margin> <note>original: "ἐντελέχεια"</note> *Entelecheia* <note>original: "entelechian"</note> is rather the Aristotelian word, by no means signifying what Cicero thinks—continuous and perennial motion—but rather a certain perfection or consummation. For when Aristotle wished to create a nomenclature for his own concepts, he conceived certain new words in his mind, and then, as if giving birth to them, he produced this one as well, by which the form and <margin>Budé writes most extensively on this in his book *On the As*.</margin> <note>A reference to the scholar Guillaume Budé and his 1514 treatise on ancient weights and measures, *De Asse*.</note> essence is indicated, to which every thing owes its own perfection. For what any thing is, it is most especially from this *entelecheia*. Cicero, however, was deceived by the trickery of similarity and explained not this Aristotelian term, but another entirely adjacent and bordering it. For *endelechos* <note>original: "Endelechôs"</note> means <margin>continuously</margin> <note>original: "ἐνδελεχῶς"</note> continuously in motion, and *endelechismos* <note>original: "endelechismos"</note> is continuous mobility; <margin>continuous movement</margin> <note>original: "ἐνδελεχισμός"</note> from these, Cicero thought Aristotle's *entelecheia* was derived—a word which, however, has a "t" rather than a "d" in the second syllable. He added that the authors standing against Cicero were as great as any of the Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle’s school of philosophy</note>. And as he was a man who seemed (at least at that time) most sharp in disputing, and having his ear (as Persius says) washed with biting vinegar <note>An idiom from the Roman satirist Persius meaning a person with a sharp, critical, and discerning mind</note>, and moreover a very great proclaimer of our words, he easily [convinced] either us, or the others, then who...

<meta>This page continues the defense of Marcus Tullius Cicero against the criticisms of the Greek scholar John Argyropoulos, who argued that Cicero lacked knowledge of Greek and philosophy. [Argyropoulos] had persuaded his followers to such an extent that—though it is almost a sacrilege to say—it was taken as a given among us that Marcus Tullius <note>The Roman orator and philosopher Cicero.</note> knew neither philosophy nor Greek literature. Truly, once the whole matter had been investigated more diligently, I judged it to be my duty, as it is the duty of any professor of Latin, to defend with every effort the glory of Cicero—by which we stand most firmly against the Greeks—as if defending our very lives. This is so even though his reputation has been placed beyond the reach of any blow and above injury, not only by its antiquity but by a certain near-divinity. 

Now, first of all, the authority of this man Argyropoulos—which carries no small weight with me—must be refuted by other authorities, and (I believe) much stronger ones. Then it must be shown that, because of the very objections raised, the acclaim for Cicero actually increases <margin>Much less.</margin> <note>original: "Nedum." This relates to the phrase "far from decreasing" in the main text.</note> rather than diminishes. And there will come forward (I believe) enough suitable praisers of Cicero <margin>Praisers of Cicero</margin> regarding philosophy (to be content for now with as few as possible): Boethius, Macrobius, and Augustine. For who is sharper than Boethius <margin>Boethius.</margin> in dialectics <note>The formal study of logic and argumentation.</note>? Or more subtle in mathematics? Or richer in philosophy? Or more sublime in theology? Even the more recent philosophers—whom I consider by far the most perceptive of all—held him in such high regard, such as Saint Thomas <margin>Thomas.</margin> <note>Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), the influential Scholastic theologian.</note> Aquinas, that divine man, and that Great teacher of Thomas <margin>Albert the Great.</margin> <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), known for his immense breadth of knowledge and for teaching Aquinas.</note> (good God, what men he deserved as his commentators!); they departed so little from his [Boethius's] opinion that there is now almost no...

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous section, where the author began a defense of Cicero’s philosophical reputation. ->MISCELLANIES. 3<-

...vocation. Furthermore, regarding Macrobius, whom, I ask, should we believe more than Boethius, who praises him especially? Finally, the authority [Marginal note: Macrobius] of Augustine himself is so deeply rooted in nearly all disciplines that it is placed beyond all risk <note>original: "extra omnem aleam," literally "beyond any game of dice," meaning beyond doubt or chance.</note>, so much so [Marginal note: Augustine] that it is considered a sin to deviate from it by even a "nail's breadth" <note>original: "transversum unguem," a common idiom meaning the smallest possible distance.</note>, as the saying goes. For it is well known that he possessed such a swiftness of intellect that he understood whatever is taught regarding the art of speaking and debating, whatever concerns the dimensions of shapes, music, and numbers, even without great difficulty and with no man teaching him. He then excelled with such sharpness of argument that the defense of all truth seems, by right, to rely most especially upon this one man. 

Let us consider, therefore, what these individuals at least thought of Cicero and how much they attributed to him in philosophy; then let us boldly believe that it is not for just anyone to stir up a false accusation against a man who has been so uniquely praised by the most highly acclaimed men. Thus, Boethius not only repeatedly uses the testimonies of Marcus Tullius <note>Cicero</note> as firm and inviolable arguments, but he is also not ashamed to undertake an interpretation of Cicero’s *Topics* <note>The *Topica* was Cicero's work on the systematic treatment of arguments; Boethius's commentary on it was a standard textbook in the Middle Ages.</note> (which is the greatest honor). 

Macrobius, moreover, occupies himself with even a small part of one and the same book <note>Referring to Cicero’s *Dream of Scipio*, which Macrobius famously commented upon.</note> in such a way that he did not hesitate to make Cicero an equal to Plato, nor to distinguish him with the titles of all branches of knowledge. Finally, Augustine, when in his own *Academics*—though speaking through the persona of another—calls him now "his Cicero," now "a wise man,"

<meta>This page continues the defense of Cicero’s philosophical standing. ## BOOK

...finally, he <note>Augustine</note> says that by him <note>Cicero</note> philosophy in the Latin language was both begun and perfected; then in his *Confessions*, where he is by no means wearing a mask <note>original: "personatus," meaning he is speaking sincerely and without artifice</note>, he [Marginal note: Unmasked] openly condemns those who admire Cicero’s tongue <note>his style</note> but not his heart <note>his philosophical substance</note>. Rather, he admits that he himself changed his disposition, that he turned his prayers and vows toward God, that he no longer held the same desires as before, that every vain hope grew cheap to him, and that he sought the immortality of wisdom with an incredible heat of the heart—finally, that he already began to rise up to return to God. He confesses all these things were Cicero’s gift, [Marginal note: Cicero’s *Hortensius*.] and he attributed the entire occasion of his salvation to that one book of his called *Hortensius* <note>A now-lost dialogue by Cicero that argued for the study of philosophy; it famously set Augustine on the path to Christianity.</note>. "For that book," he says, "was not offered to sharpen my tongue, nor did it provide me with speech <note>rhetoric</note>, but rather it persuaded me by what it spoke." 

Would that those men had read this, who cast in our teeth [Marginal note: Jerome’s dream.] the dream of the sick Jerome being flogged before the tribunal <note>St. Jerome famously dreamed he was whipped by God for being a "Ciceronian rather than a Christian," a story often used by critics to discourage reading Cicero.</note>, not even considering this: that Jerome himself—when he was later accused of perjury by Rufinus for returning to the reading of Cicero—complained most bitterly that his own dreams were being thrown back at him by that man. 

But (to return to the point), let Argyropoulos <note>Johannes Argyropoulos (1415–1487), a Greek scholar who famously criticized Cicero’s knowledge of Greek philosophy.</note> or any other [Marginal note: Cicero-scourge.] "Cicero-scourge" <term>Ciceromastix: a Greek-style compound meaning 'scourge of Cicero,' used to describe relentless critics.</term> certainly prevail. But I ask that he first dare to surmount these masses of authority which we have set against him. Indeed, regarding Greek literature, the opinion is easier and more favorable. For who has an ear so rough, who is so...

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of Cicero’s intellectual legacy. While the previous page focused on Cicero's spiritual influence on St. Augustine, this page shifts to his linguistic mastery, specifically his ability to compete with and even critique the Greek language, which was traditionally considered superior to Latin in philosophy.</meta>

->MISCELLANIES. 4<-

He is an enemy to truth who—if he merely catches the scent of a few, almost hurried Greek words scattered here and there even in Cicero's own letters—would ever say that he has found anything more elegant or more "Attic" <note>Referring to the refined, pure style of Athens.</note> in the whole of Greece. Thus, he [Marginal note: Posidonius] rightly discouraged Posidonius—that most learned man—from writing; and he rightly threw the Greek nation into confusion with his own Greek commentary on his achievements. In that book, he says he exhausted the entire "scent-box" [Marginal note: Isocrates] [Marginal note: Scent-box (μυροθήκιον)] of Isocrates <note>original: "Myrothecion," a Greek term for a chest of perfumes or stylistic refinements.</note>, and all the "jewel-boxes" of his disciples, as well as no small amount of Aristotelian "pigments" <note>Rhetorical colors or flourishes.</note>. By this confidence, it seems to me, he [Marginal note: From Cicero’s letters to Atticus, borrowed. But Politian would have seen this with his own reasoning.] [Marginal note: Cicero and Budé certainly argued the opposite, and not with trivial arguments.] sometimes extended the *pomerium* <note>original: "pomerium protulisse." The *pomerium* was the sacred boundary of the city of Rome; Cicero uses it here as a metaphor for expanding the limits and authority of the Latin language.</note> of the Latin language no less than that of the Greek. Let it be as evidence—so that we may be content for now with a single example—that he denies the Greeks have a common name for the word "innocence," but says they could perhaps use *eulabeia* <note>original: "εὐλάβειαν," meaning caution, discretion, or piety.</note> [Marginal note: εὐλάβεια (caution)]. Then he also occasionally rebukes a certain sluggishness in their vocabulary, as when he says they indeed wish to make a distinction but are weak in their choice of words. He proves this in the case of those terms like "melancholy" and "madness," or likewise in the fact that they use the word *ponos* [Marginal note: πόνος (toil)] <note>original: "πόνον," meaning toil or labor.</note> too confusedly for "labor," as they receive it as both "pain" and "toil" equally. Then he indicates that we [Latin speakers] say something better than they do, such as our word for a "banquet" <note>original: "convivium," literally "living together," which Cicero preferred over the Greek "symposium" or "drinking together."</note> and other things; and that certain things are marked much better by Latin words than by Greek. This, he signifies, is found in many places, and he even points out that the Greeks lack a name for a thing with which they are most familiar.

C iij

<summary>This page discusses Cicero's mastery over the Greek language, arguing that he expanded the expressive power of Latin to the point of surpassing Greek in technical precision and vocabulary.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the defense of Cicero’s linguistic and philosophical contributions. Having previously discussed Cicero's efforts to find Latin equivalents for Greek concepts like "innocence," the author now recounts a famous anecdote from Plutarch to prove that even the greatest Greek rhetoricians conceded victory to Cicero’s eloquence.</meta>

**B O O K**

<margin>Inept vocabulary</margin> ...imitates. None of them, however, no matter how much they might have tortured themselves in searching, has yet been able to find or devise a more suitable word. <margin>Molo on Cicero.</margin> Now indeed, is it not said that even the famous <margin>Apollonius</margin> Apollonius Molo <note>A renowned Greek teacher of rhetoric on the island of Rhodes who taught both Cicero and Julius Caesar.</note>—the most celebrated of all the rhetoricians of his time—having once heard Marcus Tullius [Cicero] deliver a speech in Greek, and an extemporaneous one at that, stood transfixed for a long time? Finally, it is said he pronounced in wonder: "I indeed both praise and admire you, Cicero, but I pity and grieve for the fate of the Greeks; for the honors of culture <note>original: "humanitatis." This refers to the concept of *humanitas*, the development of human virtue through education and the arts.</note> and eloquence, which were the only things we had left to ourselves, are now also, through you (as far as I can see), being claimed by the Romans." Truly, this judgment was recorded in literature not by a Latin author, nor by some foolish person, but by Plutarch himself <note>The famous Greek biographer (c. 46–120 AD) in his *Life of Cicero*.</note>—a man of Greek intellect but Roman gravity. Therefore, in this very manner, the ancients have left us testimony concerning his knowledge and his skill in the Greek language. 

Come now, let us also weigh that <margin>Endelechia</margin> *endelechia* <note>original: "endelechian." From the Greek *endelecheia* (continuity/duration). This is a famous philological debate; Cicero was accused of confusing *entelecheia* (actuality/soul-form) with *endelecheia* (continuous motion).</note>, that late slanderer of Cicero. Is he then criticized in this matter because he said Aristotle had introduced a certain "fifth nature" <note>This refers to the *quintessence*, a substance distinct from earth, air, fire, and water, which Aristotle associated with the heavens and, according to some interpretations, the soul.</note>—distinct from the four elements—and thus named the soul itself with a new term? This is shown by Alexander of Aphrodisias <note>A celebrated commentator on Aristotle who lived around 200 AD.</note>, who was most highly praised among all the Peripatetic philosophers even by Averroes <note>The influential 12th-century Islamic philosopher and commentator.</note>, in those remarkably subtle books of *Natural Questions*, which Saint Jerome <note>The primary translator of the Latin Vulgate Bible.</note> boasts he has read; it is also shown by Porphyry <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher and critic of the 3rd century AD.</note> in his commentary on Aristotle's ten categories...

...published inquiries and responses; and Themistius <note>A 4th-century Greek philosopher and statesman known for his influential paraphrases of Aristotle.</note> regarding these, in his second paraphrase of *On the Soul*; and Simplicius <note>One of the last great Neoplatonist philosophers (c. 490–560 AD) who wrote extensive commentaries on Aristotle’s works.</note> where he interprets the Aristotelian volume of the same subject; and Quintilianus Aristides <note>A Greek author of a treatise *On Music*, likely active in the 3rd century AD.</note> in the third book of *On Music*; and finally, in a certain letter of his, Saint Ambrose <note>The Bishop of Milan (c. 340–397 AD). Or is it rather this that is being blamed: that he [Cicero] neither spelled the word *endelechy* <note>original: "endelechiæ." From the Greek *endelecheia*, meaning "continuity" or "persistence."</note> with its proper letters, and applied a different meaning to it than Aristotle did (as we have said above)? Therefore, I first ask of those critics, by what argument in particular have they concluded that Aristotle wrote *entelechy* <note>original: "entelechian." From the Greek *entelecheia*, meaning "actuality" or "perfection." The debate centers on whether the soul is "continuous motion" or "perfected actuality."</note> rather than *endelechy*? For this word is just as much a novelty as that one; nor can the soul be signified by the one any less than by the other; nor does Aristotle himself declare with a new term that "perfection" <note>original: "perfectionem."</note> is intended rather than that "continuous motion" <note>original: "motionem illam... continuam."</note>. Let them indeed have believed so: Porphyry, Themistius, Simplicius, or any of their peers...

[Apell]icon <margin>Apellicon of Teos</margin> himself, being more a collector of books than a student of philosophy, and having dared to emend them according to his own judgment (or so he indeed thought), defiled the texts with very many errors. His descendants hid the books in a trench to prevent them from being seized for the Royal Library of Pergamum.</note></margin> For the ancient Peripatetics <note>The followers of Aristotle, named after the *peripatos* or "walkway" in the Lyceum where they held their discussions.</note>, lacking those standard works by which all philosophy is structured, possessed only a few of those they call <margin>Exoteric <note>original: "εξωτερικοί" (exoterikoi). Therefore, there is no reason why we should now doubt that, concerning the primal integrity of this reading—especially with such a small difference—there is either room for suspicion on both sides, or (if one must choose between them) it is safer to prefer the authority of Cicero. He lived long before Alexander <note>Alexander of Aphrodisias (fl. c. 200 AD), one of the most famous ancient commentators on Aristotle.</note>, who was the first of that group of Aristotle's commentators we mentioned. Alexander flourished in the time of the Emperor Severus <note>Septimius Severus (reigned 193–211 AD).</note>, as his own work *On Fate* indicates; he was almost a contemporary, or perhaps (I believe) a little older, than those great Academics, Origen and Plotinus <note>Origen the Pagan and Plotinus (c. 204–270 AD) were foundational figures in Neoplatonism.</note>.

Referring to the master copy or original manuscript.</note></margin> of Aristotle's books which were published in his own age—certainly not uncorrupted, but (as we said) scribbled upon? <margin>scribbled upon <note>original: "conscribellatam". <margin>The harmony of Plato and Aristotle.</margin>

Porphyry <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 234–305 AD) and student of Plotinus who sought to show the underlying unity of Plato and Aristotle’s systems.</note> is recorded to have composed seven books in which this primary argument was gathered: that the school of thought <term>sect <note>original: "haeresin". In this context, it refers to a philosophical school or set of principles rather than the modern religious sense of heresy.</note></term> of Plato and Aristotle is entirely the same. My friend Pico della Mirandola <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), a brilliant Renaissance polymath and close associate of the author, famous for his "900 Theses."</note> also treated this in the preface of one of his disputations. Unless my judgment fails me, he will prove it to be truer than truth itself in a most extensive and (I believe) most beautiful work which he labors over and hammers out <note>original: "molitur, & cudit". This evokes the image of a blacksmith at a forge, suggesting the intense intellectual effort Pico put into his unfinished work *De Concordia* (On Harmony).</note> day and night regarding this very harmony between Plato and Aristotle.

Thus, it will be understood that our Cicero not only did not err in that particular term <note>Referring to "endelechy," discussed on the previous page.</note>, but that he wrote either somewhat more learnedly than the others, or more suitably for his own philosophical sect. Consequently, what the authors—who are as reliable as those we showed above—promised concerning the doctrine of so distinguished a man, he himself provides most generously and abundantly, even in that specific point of diversion where he suffers the most criticism...

## WHAT THE CARPATINIAN SLIPPERS IN
## Catullus are; how this correctly written
## term is wrongly changed.

### CHAPTER II.

[Large initial V]alerius Catullus <note>Gaius Valerius Catullus (c. 84–54 BC), a celebrated Roman lyric poet known for his emotional intensity and linguistic precision.</note> says thus in a certain epigram <note>Original: "epigrãmate". This refers to Catullus, Poem 98, directed at a man named Victius.</note>:
->“That tongue of yours, if the need should arise, you could use<-
->to lick buttocks and 'Carpatinian' slippers.”<-

It has indeed been asked by many, yet explained by no one until now, what these “Carpatinian” <margin>Carpatinian</margin> or “Carbatinian” slippers are. For both are correct, though they are also called <margin>Carbasinian</margin> “Carbasinian.” Certain impostors <note>original: "plani". Historically, these were wandering tricksters or quacks.</note> and literary shysters <margin>Slippers.</margin> <margin>Wanderers, and</margin> <margin>impostors.</margin> <note>original: "tenebriones". Literally "dwellers in darkness," used here to describe scholars who obscure the truth or deal in shady interpretations.</note>, having erased the ancient word, substitute I know not what—either "Cercopythas" or "Coprotinas" <note>Invented or nonsensical words intended to mock the bad editors; 'Coprotinas' suggests something born of dung (*kopros*).</note>—words brought forth from a pigsty, not a school; naked words, hollow names, and sounds of nothingness. We, drawing from the Greek toolkit <note>original: "Græco instrumento". This refers to the vast body of Greek literature and lexicography which the author uses to explain Latin obscurities.</note> as if from a pantry, shall bring forth authorities of no small repute nor dismissible credibility, by which the reading may be restored intact and the cloud of interpretation may be dispelled. Now, first of all, Julius Pollux <margin>Carbatinian</margin> <note>A Greek grammarian and sophist of the 2nd century AD, author of the *Onomasticon*, a thesaurus of Greek terms.</note> himself, in his ninth book dedicated to Caesar Commodus, says that "Carbatinian" <note>original: "Carbatinas".</note> are a rustic footwear, so named after the Carians <note>A people of Asia Minor.</note>. Aristotle, however, in the second book of his *History of Animals*, writes that camels are shod with "Carbatinian" slippers so that they do not grow weary during long marches in the army. But also...

<margin>Books of the
Pastorals</margin>

Four very elegant little books exist in Greek under the title *Poemenicon* <note>original: "Pœmenicõn". These are the *Pastorals*, likely referring to Longus’s *Daphnis and Chloe*.</note>, in the second of which an old man is introduced with a travel pouch and carbatinian slippers. Also Lucian <note>Lucian of Samosata (c. 125 – after 180 AD), a Hellenistic satirist and rhetorician.</note>, in the dialogue titled either *Alexander* or *The False Prophet*, says that certain orators from Paphlagonia were shod in carbatinian slippers. Finally, Xenophon the Socratic <note>Xenophon (c. 430–354 BC), a student of Socrates and a soldier-historian.</note>, in the third book of the *Anabasis*, says that when their old shoes had failed, they wore carbatinas made from fresh ox-hides; Suidas <note>The *Suda*, a massive 10th-century Byzantine Greek encyclopedia and lexicon.</note> also cites this passage, though without naming the author. Furthermore, a certain commentator on that same Xenophon adds that carbatinas are a "barbaric footwear."

feet of land or aquatic animals, for it did not move them <margin>Alternately.</margin> alternately <note>original: "vicissatim"</note>, but moved both right legs together, and then both left legs together. From this it is called the *Camelopardalis*..."

# HOW MANY THINGS MUST BE READ BY INTERPRETERS OF POETS, AND HOW PERSIUS FASHIONED A SATIRE BASED ON PLATO'S *ALCIBIADES*.

## CHAPTER IV.

He who undertakes the interpretation of poets must have burned the midnight oil <note>The Latin "lucubrasse" refers to "lucubratio"—study or work done by lamplight late into the night.</note> not only (as the saying goes) by the lamp of Aristophanes <note>Aristophanes of Byzantium was a legendary Greek scholar and grammarian; his "lamp" symbolizes meticulous linguistic and grammatical study.</note>, but also by that of Cleanthes <note>Cleanthes was a Stoic philosopher; his "lamp" symbolizes the necessity of deep philosophical understanding to truly grasp poetic meaning.</note>. Nor is it enough to merely look forward to the doctrines of the philosophers...

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument from the previous chapter regarding the extensive breadth of knowledge required for the proper interpretation of poetry, moving from general education to specific examples of literary imitation.</meta>

->BOOK<-

...household, but also of legal scholars, of physicians, and likewise of dialecticians <note>experts in logical argumentation</note>, and whoever else makes up that "circle of learning" which we call the <margin>Encyclopedia.</margin> <term>Encyclopedia</term> <note>From the Greek "enkyklios paideia," meaning "all-around education" or the circle of arts and sciences.</note>, but also of all philologists. These subjects must not only be looked at from afar, but rather looked into deeply; nor, as the saying goes, should they be greeted only <margin>From the threshold.</margin> from the threshold and the entryway <note>original: "ab limine, ac uestibulo"</note>, but rather summoned into the inner chambers and into an intimate familiarity. This is necessary if we desire to aid the Latin cause and to rout the ignorance that grows stronger every day; otherwise, a half-learned diligence, combined with great self-assurance, results in detriment rather than benefit. Nevertheless, one may find very many things, especially in the works of the philosophers, which have been adopted into the books of our poets. For instance, as I indicated some years ago while publicly interpreting the poet Persius <note>Aulus Persius Flaccus (34–62 AD), a Roman satirist known for his difficult, metaphorical style and Stoic philosophy.</note>, his fifth satire <note>original: "satyram ipsius quintam"; the author refers to the poem beginning "Rem populi tractas," which in modern editions is usually numbered as Satire 4.</note>—which begins with the words "You handle the people’s business"—was sketched out according to the model of the Platonic dialogue called the *First Alcibiades*. Persius should be understood to have not only modestly tasted those themes which Socrates discusses there with Alcibiades concerning the just and the unjust, and concerning each person's self-knowledge, but also to have drawn from that same source certain passages which, if not of great weight, are certainly full of charm <note>original: "Veneris," literally "of Venus," referring to rhetorical grace</note> and wit <note>original: "leporis"</note>. 

<margin>Of this kind.</margin> Of this kind is the line: "Say this, ward of great Pericles" <note>Persius, Satire 4, line 3. Pericles was the famous statesman of Athens' Golden Age.</note>, since Socrates also shows that it seemed the greatest thing of all to Alcibiades that Pericles, the son of Xanthippus, had been left to him by his father as a guardian. Likewise, the phrase "I am the son of Dinomache" <note>Persius, Satire 4, line 20. Dinomache was the mother of Alcibiades.</note> is taken from what is found in Plato...

In Plato it is written: "O dear son of Cleinias and Deinomache" <note>original Greek: "ὦ φίλε παῖ κλενίȣ ϗ δ' εινομάχης"; the opening address to Alcibiades in Plato's dialogue.</note>. ## In what sense Catullus’s sparrow is to be understood, and a passage also indicated in Martial.

### CHAPTER VI

That sparrow of Catullus, in my opinion, allegorically <note>original Greek: "ἀλληγορικῶς" (allegorikos)</note> conceals a certain more obscene meaning <margin>A more obscene [meaning]</margin>, which we cannot state without violating modesty. That I should believe this, Martial persuades me in that epigram of his, of which these are the final verses:

*Give me kisses, but Catullan ones,*
*Which, if they are as many as he said,*
*I will give to you Catullus’s sparrow.*

For the poet would be excessively dull <margin>Dull</margin> <margin>poet.</margin> (which is a sin to believe) if he were saying he would give the boy an actual sparrow and finally nothing else—which I suspect is something more—after the kisses. What this thing might be, for the sake of the modesty of my pen, I leave to each person's own conjecture, based on the natural lustfulness of the sparrow. <note>In Roman lore, sparrows were associated with Venus and were considered symbols of intense sexual activity.</note>

<meta>Continuing the translation of Cassius Dio's explanation for the names of the days of the week, following the mention of the seven "wandering stars" or planets.</meta>

...it began to be commonly used among men <note>The OCR "mines" likely completes "homines" (men/people) from the previous page.</note> not so long ago (so to speak). However, the ancient Greeks (as far as I know) were entirely unaware of this matter in any way. But since this custom has become so widespread among other nations, and especially among the Romans, to the point that it now seems almost ancestral, I will discuss a few points about them and explain in what manner or for what reason they are ordered as they are. I have heard, therefore, two reasons, and truly neither is difficult to understand, yet both require careful inspection. 

For if someone takes the harmony called the <term>diatessaron<note>A musical interval of a fourth. In ancient cosmology, the intervals between planetary spheres were often thought to correspond to musical ratios, known as the "Music of the Spheres."</note></term> <note>original: "dià tessarōn"</note>, in which it is believed the entire power of all music is contained, and applies it to these stars—by which the whole adornment of the sky is distinguished—in the same order in which they move, and starts from that highest sphere assigned to Saturn, then, skipping the next two, names the master of the fourth, and then again skipping two others, adds the seventh, and proceeding in the same way, recounts the gods presiding over those spheres in order and thus counts the days, he will find that they all correspond by a certain musical reasoning with that celestial arrangement and decoration. <note>The author explains how to arrive at the order of the days (Saturn, Sun, Moon, etc.) by taking every fourth planet from the orbital sequence: Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Sun, Venus, Mercury, Moon.</note>

However, this is the first reason that is given. The second is of this kind. If you begin to count the hours of the day and night starting immediately from the first, so that you assign it to Saturn, then the next to Jupiter, the third to Mars, the fourth to the Sun, the fifth to Venus, the sixth to Mercury, and the seventh to the Moon, following that same sequence of the spheres...

MISCELLANY 13

...which is handed down by the Egyptians. If you do this same thing again, until twenty-four hours have passed, you will find that the first hour of the following day belongs to the Sun. Then, if you repeat this for those twenty-four hours in the same way as before, you will assign the first hour of the third day to the Moon. If you subsequently observe this same pattern for the remaining days, each day will clearly adopt its own corresponding god. These explanations from Dio <note>Cassius Dio (c. 155–235 AD), a Roman historian of Greek origin. The author is referencing Dio’s *Roman History*, Book 37.</note> are quite charming and clever, unless I am mistaken.

But since the "Sabbath" <note>Saturday</note> and the "Lord’s Day" <note>Sunday</note> <margin>Sabbath. Lord's Day.</margin> have commonly lost their ancient names, while the others even now retain them, it is fitting for students to know that the former was called the Day of Saturn, and the latter the Day of the Sun. For concerning the Day of the Sun (as far as I can recall now), both the Greek historian Sozomen <note>Salamanes Hermias Sozomenus, a 5th-century Christian historian.</note> and Saint Jerome <note>A priest and historian (c. 347–420 AD) best known for translating the Bible into Latin (the Vulgate).</note> have mentioned it. Sextus Julius Frontinus (c. 40–103 AD), a Roman civil engineer and author of *Strategemata* (Strategems).</note> in his *Strategems*, or the elegiac poet Tibullus, or finally even Tertullian <note>An early Christian author (c. 155–220 AD) from Carthage.</note> in his *Apology*, and Gaudentius in his sermons have made mention of it. Even Faustus of Mileve <note>A 4th-century bishop of the Manichaean sect, known primarily through St. Augustine's polemic against him.</note>, that greatest defender of the Manichaeans <note>Followers of a major dualistic religion that competed with early Christianity.</note>, said this: "It is pleasing to take up the leisure of the Sabbaths, and to place one's hands in the chains of Saturn." <note>original: "Placet suscipere sabbatorum ocium, & Saturniacis manus insertare catenis." This quote illustrates how even non-Christians linked the Sabbath to the planet Saturn.</note> Nor should I omit that the names of those days were changed <margin>The names of the days changed by Constantine.</margin> to those now used by Christians by the authority of the Emperor Constantine <note>Constantine the Great (r. 306–337 AD), who issued the edict making Sunday a day of rest in 321 AD.</note>, as Nicephorus <note>Likely Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos, a 14th-century Byzantine historian.</note> mentions in his Greek panegyric <note>A speech or text in high praise of someone.</note> of him.

## WHAT SHOULD BE BELIEVED IN THE WRITINGS OF DOMITIUS
and to what extent; also concerning his evident and shameful error regarding the word *attegias*.

### CHAPTER IX.

**D**estroy the huts <note>original: *attegias*. A rare Latin word of likely African or Celtic origin referring to a small cottage or hut.</note> of the Moors. Let us now examine (if you have the time) whether Domitius explains this half-line from the fourteenth Satire of Juvenal in a way that is learned, faithful, or even insightful. But <margin>Domitius Calderinus</margin> <note>Domitius Calderinus (1446–1478) was a famous Italian humanist scholar known for his influential but often controversial commentaries on classical poets.</note> first, let us say a few words about the man, in case anyone is perhaps interested in these things. Domitius Calderinus of Verona, then, who died ten years ago, was a man of sharp intellect and great industry in literature; he was most diligent in the Latin language, not uncurious about Greek, and not unskilled in composing either prose or poetry. However, he was (as his own works testify) an excessive admirer of himself, and once he had committed to an opinion, he remained stubborn and obstinate <margin>Stubborn.</margin> even against the truth. Because of this, he was also rather boastful in his writing, a despiser of everyone compared to himself, a mocker of all on the slightest occasion, and a nitpicker without discrimination. Consequently, he stirred up more than a few rivalries with highly learned men, and he suffered from their far-from-hidden hatred. Among the general public, his authority was so great that at Rome, while still a young man, he won for himself the highest celebrity among professors...

...not with a small oar <note>original: *remulo*. From the proverb *non remulo sed velis* ("not with an oar but with sails"), meaning to move with great force rather than modest effort.</note> (as they say), but carried along with full sail, and having published many notes on that man’s commentaries on Martial, he was the first to check the man’s arrogance, which was advancing with great strides and was by then becoming utterly fierce. 

We also then, in the flower of our early youth—not indeed to strike his books plainly from [readers'] hands, but to wake up the sleepy youth who were "sleeping on both ears" <note>original: *in aurem utranque dormientem*. A classical idiom used by authors like Terence and Plautus to describe a state of total unconcern or deep, carefree sleep.</note>—in the very beginning of our professional apprenticeship, we gently struck him at times, as if with a sparing hand. We declare that we shall do the same now, however the matter turns out, so that our integrity and freedom may be established. 

For we shall not suffer, as far as our strength allows, the credulous youth to be mocked with impunity and simple minds to be tripped up. Since, however (as it is found in Plautus), "happily wise is he who is wise through the danger of another," <note>original: *feliciter sapit is, qui periculo alieno sapit*. This quote from the Roman playwright Plautus suggests it is better to learn from the mistakes and misfortunes of others than to suffer them oneself.</note> we ourselves shall take care, with all our manly effort, not to include anything in these books that cannot be defended by authorities. 

But as to what we think of him in total, if we weigh his vices against his virtues and estimate which part of his talent was most prominent, <margin>An Epigram</margin> let our own epigram bear witness. # MISCELLANIES, Book 15

->Whom the wave of eddying Benacus <note>original: *Benaci*. Lake Garda in northern Italy, the region where Domitius was born.</note> harasses,<-
->The Muse often exchanges this place for Libethron <note>A mountain or cave in Thrace sacred to the Muses.</note> itself,<-
->And the fountain of Sisyphus <note>The fountain of Pirene in Corinth, often associated with the labor of Sisyphus.</note>, and the thickets of Permessus <note>A river on Mount Helicon in Greece, sacred to the Muses and a favorite spot for poets.</note>,<-
->For on this soil Domitius first cried as an infant,<-
->He, that learned man, he whom you know so well,<-
->Giving lessons to the Roman youth,<-
->Drawing out wondrous meanings from the poets’ storehouse.<-
->Go now, traveler, you have seen enough with your own eyes.<-

But perhaps I have said too much about the man, especially since I promised only a little; yet I did so with the intention of tossing a kind of morsel to those two-penny <term>diobolaribus: a derogatory term for cheap or low-quality scholars, derived from the "diobol," a Greek coin of very small value.</term> grammarians who see everything as if through a mist. These are men who consider nothing correct unless it is something they themselves have finally picked out from their own worn-out and trampled little notebooks of lectures. 

Now, as for the word *attegiae* <note>A Latin term for huts or shacks, usually referring to those used by nomadic tribes in North Africa.</note>, these are the words of Domitius himself. "Others," he says, "understand that in the language of the Moors, *attegiae* signifies *mapalia* <note>A Punic or Numidian word for the portable huts used by nomads.</note>. I, however, understand it to mean Mauritania at the furthest part of Libya." He then cites Dionysius <note>Dionysius Periegetes, an ancient geographer who wrote a description of the world in hexameter verse.</note>:

->Near the edge of Libya they inhabit the *attegiae* waves,<-
->Where the Maurusian people dwell by the pillars of Hercules.<-

This is what he says—clearly "seeing the better path, yet following the worse" <note>original: *videns utique meliora, deteriora sequens*. A famous allusion to Ovid's *Metamorphoses*, where the character Medea laments her inability to act on her better judgment.</note>. I truly wanted to believe those claims, but common report denies they are true. Domitius cites Dionysius as the author, who if ever...

# MISCELLANIES, BOOK 2

If anyone is found to have made any mention of that word <note>The word *attegiae*, which Poliziano argues does not belong in the text Domitius is citing.</note>, I will yield; I will throw up my hands then and there, and I will not refuse to be held the most foolish of all men. But, setting aside all bluster <note>original: *uessica*, literally "bladder," a metaphor for inflated or empty talk</note>, let us consider <margin>The matter of Priscian</margin> the matter of Priscian itself. These are not verses of Dionysius; they are not even a <margin>translation.</margin> translation of Dionysius, but rather they belong to Priscian, from the little book in which he interprets the poet Dionysius <note>Dionysius the Periegete, a 2nd-century author of a poetic description of the habitable world.</note> from the Greek. Domitius, however, corrupts and distorts them. 

For where Priscian originally wrote, "to the waves of Tethys" <note>original: *ad Tethyos undas*. Tethys was a sea goddess whose name was a common poetic metonym for the ocean.</note>, Domitius—having deleted and falsified the earlier writing—substituted "the *attegian* waves" in its place. He would never have done this (in my judgment) if he had consulted the logic of the poem, or the lengths and pauses of the syllables <note>Poliziano is criticizing Domitius’s lack of skill in "scansion," the practice of analyzing the rhythm of a verse.</note>. For he placed the word *attegias* with the third-to-last syllable long, against the testimony of the very man he interprets, and then with the last syllable short, against the authority of everyone.

It is well that the works of Dionysius survive, for I believe these Greek verses on that subject belong to him:

"But indeed, they dwell upon the furthest point / near the Sylaian people of the Moorish land." <note>original: ἀλλ’ ἤτοι πυμάτην μὲν ἐπὶ γλωχῖνα νέμοντα / ἀγχῠ συλάων μαυρουσίδος ἔθνεα γαίης. These lines (174-175) of Dionysius's *Periegesis* describe the North African coast.</note>

Even if you unroll the whole work now, you will see that there is not even the slightest suspicion of "attegiae" in Dionysius. You will see one person cited for another, the true text erased, a false one substituted, and no account taken of the syllables or the meter of the poem. And will anyone hesitate to disagree most freely with him now, and to refute these trifles, even if a long-held <margin>Prejudiced opinion.</margin> prejudiced opinion regarding the man's talent and learning creates a cause for envy...

## CONCERNING THE *BAPTAE*, COTYTTO, AND THE COTYTTIAN RITES, AS WELL AS THE CORRECT READING AND EXPLANATION IN JUVENAL AND HORACE.

MISCELLANIES. 17

...of Eupolis. <note>Completing the name "Baptae" from the previous page.</note> What the plot of the comedy was, and what its subject matter was, will be shrewdly tracked down through the very traces that survive today. The Athenians, therefore, at that time had adopted certain sacred rites, foreign religions, and new gods, for whom nocturnal vigils <note>The term "nocturnae peruigilationes" refers to all-night religious festivals, which were often associated by conservative commentators with moral corruption.</note> had been established—later the cause of great scandals, just as the Bacchanalia <note>The Bacchanalia were Roman festivals of Bacchus that were famously suppressed by the Roman Senate in 186 BCE due to reports of ritual violence and sexual deviance.</note> were in Rome. 

But among the other sacred rites, there were also those called the *Cotyttia*. <margin>The Cotyttian rites.</margin> We believe that Eupolis wrote the *Baptae* against these nocturnal *Cotyttia*, but we see that other comic poets, including Aristophanes, also mocked this entire category of all-night vigils and foreign influences. Thus Juvenal <note>A Roman satirist (c. 55–138 CE) known for his biting critiques of Roman society.</note> says that the things happening in Rome contrary to the ritual of ceremonies were not unlike those which the *Baptae* <note>original: "Baptæ". The word translates to "the dippers" or "the baptizers," referring to the priests of the goddess Cotytto who bathed or "baptized" initiates in water.</note> committed in the nocturnal *Cotyttia* at Athens. 

Cicero, in his work *On Laws*, speaks thus: "But as for what displeases me regarding nocturnal rites, the comic poets indicate; had such license been granted at Rome, what would that man have done <margin>Nocturnal rites must be forbidden.</margin> who brought premeditated lust into a sacrifice where it was not even lawful to cast the shamelessness of one’s eyes?" <note>original: "Quid autē mihi dißpliceat in nocturnis, poëtæ indicāt comici..." Cicero is referring to Clodius Pulcher, who famously sneaked into the women-only rites of the Bona Dea.</note> 

And shortly after, the same author says: "And (lest I seem perhaps too harsh) Diagondas the Theban <margin>Diagondas</margin> abolished all nocturnal rites in the heart of Greece by a permanent law." 

Truly, Aristophanes—the most witty poet of Old Comedy—so harasses these new gods and the nocturnal vigils used in their worship, that in his work <margin>Aristophanes.</margin> Sabazius <note>Sabazius was a nomadic horseman and sky god of the Phrygians and Thracians whose rites were viewed with suspicion by traditional Greeks.</note> and certain other foreign gods <margin>Sabazius.</margin> are judged and expelled from the city. # BOOK

...that both Aristophanes and other poets of the Old Comedy <note>The early period of Athenian comedy (5th century BCE), known for its biting political and social satire.</note> turned the sharp edge of their pens against "new gods" and against nocturnal vigils. Since it is clear from the preceding arguments that the *Baptae* was a comedy in which shameful and obscene behaviors were criticized, and since we find here mention of the *Baptae* and the nocturnal rites of the Athenians—which we shall show were called the *Cotyttia*—who would not now understand that the *Baptae* was, in fact, the very play in which such things were primarily attacked? 

Strabo, <note>A Greek geographer and historian (c. 64 BCE – 24 CE) whose *Geography* is a vital source for ancient customs.</note> moreover, in the tenth book of his *Geography*, relates that the *Cotyttia* and the <margin>The Bendidia.</margin> *Bendidia* were sacred rites practiced among the Thracians, from which the Orphic mysteries took their origin; he also names the goddess <margin>Cotys.</margin> Cotys, whom Aeschylus <note>The "Father of Tragedy" (c. 525–456 BCE).</note> mentions. Then, a little later, the same author says that the Athenians accepted many things from foreign rites, "so much so that they were even satirized in comedies," he says. <note>original: ὥς τε καὶ ἐκωμῳδήθησαν</note> But among the other foreign rites he records as having been adopted by them, he lists first the Thracian ones, which we have shown above were called the *Cotyttia* and the *Bendidia*. 

It is clear, therefore, that the *Cotyttia* were foreign rites usually observed by the Athenians, and it is also clear that they were attacked by the comic poets. We are led by conjecture, however—since mention is made here of the *Baptae* (which we know was a comedy by Eupolis) and of the nocturnal rites of the Athenians—to the conclusion that this specific kind of ritual was harassed in that play above all others. Furthermore, we believe the goddess who presided over such rituals and vigils was <margin>Cotytto.</margin> called Cotytto, from whose name the <margin>The name of the Cotyttia.</margin> designation "Cotyttia" was derived by a direct linguistic rule; and since this goddess in comedies...

...most infamous in the Old Comedy; thus Synesius, <note>Synesius of Cyrene (c. 373–414 CE), a Neoplatonist philosopher and bishop whose letters provide insight into late antique culture.</note> in a certain letter whose beginning is *“He whose name and intent are both clear,”* <note>original: ὁ καὶ τ’ οὔνομα δῆλος καὶ τὴν προαίρεσιν</note> writes in exactly this manner: *“He is not in the least suited to Hermes and Hercules, the overseers of the wrestling grounds, but serves as a temple warden for Cotytto and the other Athenian 'Dust-kickers.'”* <note>original: ἑρμῇ μὲν καὶ ἡρακλεῖ τοῖς παλαίστρας ἐφόροις οὐδὲ κατὰ μικρὸν πρέπει. κοτυττοῖ δὲ καὶ τοῖς ἄλλοις ἀττικοῖς κονισάλοις νεωκορεῖ. The "Dust-kickers" (Konisaloi) were lewd, phallic spirits associated with the rites of Cotytto.</note> By these words it is signified that the man he discusses in the letter is not compatible with Mercury or Hercules, the patrons of the wrestling school, <note>Hermes and Hercules were the traditional models of masculine athletic virtue.</note> but is instead an overseer of the temple of Cotytto and of the others most despicable among the Athenians. 

Therefore, we must read *Cotytto* and not *Cocytus*, <note>Cocytus was the "River of Wailing" in the Underworld; Poliziano is correcting a common scribal error where the goddess's name was mistaken for the river.</note> especially since the quantity of the syllables is inconsistent; for the first syllable is not short even if you remove the second 'c', as Domitius <note>Domizio Calderini (1446–1478), a rival humanist scholar whom Poliziano often critiqued for sloppy work.</note> prescribes—quite ignorantly indeed (if I may be forgiven)—I say quite ignorantly and carelessly, as he did not consider that the first syllable of the name is long, not just by position <note>In classical meter, a syllable is "long by position" if a vowel is followed by two consonants.</note> (which is the only case he addressed), but also by its very nature. 

Some, however, have preferred this goddess to be called *Cotys* <margin>Cotys.</margin> rather than *Cotytto*, just as Strabo did above, and as the same Synesius did elsewhere in these words: *“I boldly declare the man to be half-woman, a literal devotee of Cotys.”* <note>original: θαῤῥῶν ἀποφαίνω τὸν ἄνδρα ἡμίγυνον αὐτόχρημα θιασώτην τῆς κότυος.</note> “Boldly,” he says, “I pronounce that man a half-male right away, and a companion of Cotys.” 

Explaining this passage, Suidas <note>The *Suda*, a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia and dictionary of the ancient world.</note> says that Cotys is a goddess worshipped as the overseer of shameful things. From her, he says, come the likes of Clisthenes and Timarchus, <margin>Timarchus.</margin> and whoever cultivates beauty for the sake of profit <margin>Clisthenes.</margin> <note>Clisthenes and Timarchus were real Athenian figures frequently mocked by comic poets like Aristophanes for their perceived effeminacy and lack of self-control.</note> for the purpose of unspeakable lust, just like effeminate men and...

### CHAPTER XI.

<margin>Aphthonius’s *Rhetorical Exercises*.</margin>
**C**laudian, in the second book of *The Rape of Proserpina*, speaking of the goddess Venus gathering purple roses, says: *“Thus she spoke, and plucked the emblems of her own blood.”* <note>original: "Sic fata, cruoris / Carpit signa sui." Claudian was a late 4th-century Latin poet; here he alludes to the myth that the rose took its color from the goddess's blood.</note>

This passage could indeed seem obscure, were it not that in Aphthonius’s *Rhetorical Exercises* <note>Aphthonius of Antioch (4th century CE) wrote a textbook of "Progymnasmata" (preliminary rhetorical exercises) that remained influential for over a millennium.</note>, the story is explained nearly in this way: *“Let him who wonders at the beauty of the rose consider the wound of Venus. The goddess loved Adonis, but Mars...”* <note>Adonis was a mortal youth of great beauty; Mars (Ares), the god of war and Venus's jealous lover, frequently appears in these myths as a rival.</note>

MISCELLANY. 19

herself. And just as the goddess was to Adonis, so Mars was to Venus. The god loved the goddess, and the goddess pursued the mortal man. Their desire was equal, even if their status was unequal. Mars, consumed by jealousy, sought to destroy Adonis, believing that the death of Adonis would bring an end to the love. Mars strikes Adonis. Venus runs to help him; in her haste, she stumbles into the roses and becomes entangled in the thorns. Thus her foot is pierced, and the blood that flowed from the wound colored the rose from its own substance. This is how the rose, which was previously white, became what is seen today. We ourselves touched upon this little fable in our poem *The Countryman*, in that verse: 

*“The modest rose dyes her breast with Idalian blood.”* <note>original Latin: "Idalio pudibunda sinum, rosa sanguine tinguit." This line is from Poliziano's own poem, *Rusticus* (1483), a work celebrating country life and classical agriculture. "Idalian" refers to Mount Idalium on Cyprus, a site sacred to Venus.</note>

But this story has also been most beautifully expressed in vernacular verses <note>Poliziano is referring here to Italian poetry rather than Latin.</note> by Lorenzo de' Medici, the Mœcenas <note>Gaius Maecenas was the legendary patron of Virgil and Horace; by calling Lorenzo the "Maecenas of our age," Poliziano highlights his patron's role as the greatest supporter of the arts in Renaissance Florence.</note> of our age, who is as eminent in the poetic art as he is in every other virtue. 

<margin>The poet Nonnus.</margin>
The wonderful poet Nonnus, however, in the 1st and 41st books of the *Dionysiaca*, tells the story that Mars himself, by whom Adonis was killed, turned himself into a boar. Writing of Venus, he says: *“She alone, like a prophetess, did not wish to look upon the delight of boars, since Mars, mad with jealousy, in the image of a boar’s form, was about to weave the fate of Adonis by launching a death-bearing venom from his jagged teeth.”* <note>original Greek: "μούνηρ ὃ συῶν οὐκ ἤθελε λεύσειν τερπωλὴν ἅτε μάντις, ἐπεὶ συὸς εἰκόνι μορφῆς ἄρης καρχαρόδων θανάτηφόρον ἰὸν ἰάλλων ζηλομανὴς ἤμελλεν Ἀδώνιδι πότμον ὑφαίνειν." Nonnus of Panopolis was a 5th-century Greek poet whose sprawling epic, the *Dionysiaca*, was a major source for Renaissance scholars.</note> In these verses, Nonnus denies that Venus was delighted by boars, because she had foreknowledge that Mars would one day take the shape of a boar and kill Adonis out of jealousy. Yet that most charming poet of epigrams <note>Likely a reference to the poets of the *Greek Anthology*, though the specific "lepidissimus" poet is not named here.</note> does not remain silent on the fact that the rose is otherwise pleasing to Venus.

->“Go, happy rose,” (he says) “and with soft wreaths<-

Thus, they refused to enter the contest until Venus had removed that "medicine" from herself—for that is what they called the girdle. “But,” Venus said, “not even you are without your charms. You wear a golden diadem in your hair, and you likewise wear a golden helmet on your head. But let it be permitted to me, by your leave, to have these ornaments of mine, and let it be permitted to me to adopt another decoration and adornment for myself in place of the girdle.” Thus, yielding to the goddesses' wishes, she proceeded to the Scamander <note>A river near the city of Troy.</note> to gather flowers, where a grassy meadow spread out beneath the bank. At the same time, the waters themselves were charming and clear, <margin>Enticement.</margin> and enticing for bathing. The goddess, having bathed there, immediately (for this was the reason she had come) sought out an adornment for herself. Then, some...

...story. The Tyrians say (he says) that Hercules, captured by love for a certain local nymph named Tyro <margin>Tyro.</margin>, was followed by his dog (they say) in the ancient custom, by which dogs would accompany their masters even to a public assembly. Therefore, this dog of Hercules, having spotted a purple-fish <note>A murex sea snail, the source of the famous dye.</note> crawling among the rocks and having bitten into the small piece of flesh, stained its own lips with a Phoenician blood-red color.

When, therefore, Hercules approached the girl, she was delighted by that unusual dye which she saw blooming, as it were, on the dog's lips. She declared that she would have nothing to do with him thereafter <margin>The creature found.</margin> unless he brought her a garment even more brilliant than that dog's lips. Whereupon Hercules, having found the creature and collected the blood, brought the gift to the girl, being (as the Tyrians frequently say) the first author of the Phoenician dyeing process. We, therefore, in our *Rusticus* <note>original: "Ruſtico noſtra," likely referring to the author’s own work titled *Rusticus*.</note>, for this very reason finally called it the "Herculean shell" <margin>The Herculean shell.</margin> when we were referring to purple. Furthermore, the verses of the poet Nonnus <note>Nonnus of Panopolis, a 5th-century Greek poet who wrote the *Dionysiaca*, an epic on the life of Dionysus.</note> concerning this very fable are found thus in the fortieth book of the *Dionysiaca*:

<note>original Greek: Καὶ τυρίης σκοπίαζε δεδευμένα φάρεα κόχλῳ / πορφυρέας σπινθῆρας ἀκοντίζοντα θαλάσσης. / ἧχι κύων ἁλιεργὸς ἐπ’ αἰγιαλοῖσιν ἐρέπτων / ἐνδόμυχον χαροποῖσι γενειάσι θέσκελον ἰχθῦν, / χιονέας πόρφυρε παρηΐδας, ἔνδοθι κόχλου / χείλεα φοινίξας διεερῷ πυρὶ, τῷ ποτε μούνῳ / φαιδρὸν ἁλιχλαίνων ἐρυθαίνετο φᾶρος ἀνάκτων.</note>

And he looked upon the Tyrian robes steeped by the shell,
darting forth sparks of the purple sea;
where a seafaring dog, creeping on the shores,
having caught the wondrous fish in its bright-eyed jaws,
purpled its snowy cheeks, staining its lips
with a moist fire from within the shell—
the very fire with which the bright sea-cloaks of kings

### MISCELLANIES, PAGE 21

## WHAT THE AUGURY OF SAFETY IS
## according to Suetonius.
## CHAPTER XIII.

**S**uetonius Tranquillus, in his life of Augustus, <margin>The Augury of Safety.</margin> writes as follows: *He restored several of the ancient ceremonies that had gradually fallen into disuse, such as the Augury of Safety,* etc. <note>original Latin: "salutis augurium." This was a ritual performed by the Roman Augurs to determine if it was a propitious time to ask the gods for the well-being of the Roman state.</note> However, I see that what this Augury of Safety actually was is unknown even to some learned men.

We found the explanation of this matter in the thirty-seventh book of Dio’s <note>Cassius Dio (c. 155–235 AD), a Roman consul and historian who wrote a massive history of Rome in Greek.</note> histories, where he writes to this effect: *Then indeed they also performed the so-called Augury of Safety after a long interval. It is a certain type of augury through which they ask if God <note>original: "Deus." Though the Romans were polytheistic, Renaissance translators often used the singular "God" or "the Divinity" when discussing the divine principle in classical texts.</note> permits them to pray for the safety of the people; it is as if it were not even lawful to seek safety from the gods unless the gods themselves first granted permission for the request.* 

A single day was observed every year on which no army could set out for war, no one could prepare for defense, and no one could fight. For this reason, in times of constant danger—and especially during civil wars—it was never performed. For it was particularly difficult to keep a day entirely "pure" from all such activities, and it could seem quite absurd to willfully bring incredible harm upon themselves through internal rebellions—which would be ruinous for the defeated and the victors alike—and then immediately after seek safety from the immortal gods. Nevertheless, at that time, an augury of this kind could indeed be performed. Yet not even that proceeded without blemish, because adverse...

<summary>This page defines the "Augury of Safety" (salutis augurium), an ancient Roman ritual restored by Augustus, explaining that it was a prerequisite ceremony to ask the gods' permission before praying for the state's safety.</summary>
<keywords>Suetonius, Augustus, Augury of Safety, Cassius Dio, Roman rituals, religious law, civil war, Roman history</keywords>

**I** consider ***Naulia*** to be a diminutive form, or perhaps *nablia*, derived from those things which we call either *naula* or *nabla*. For we have often noted in ancient records that the letters **b** and **u** <note>original: "b literas & u." In many ancient manuscripts and inscriptions, the sounds and characters for 'b' and 'v/u' were frequently exchanged, a linguistic phenomenon known as betacism.</note> were positioned almost interchangeably as the same thing. Indeed, Plutarch cites a verse by Sophocles: *No naula for lamentations, no friendly lyre,* <note>original Greek: "οὐ ναῦλα κωκυτοῖσιν, οὐ λύρα φίλα." ...philosophy, wrote almost to this effect: the man of Croton, he says, loves the wild olive <note>The wild olive branch was the prize for victors at the Olympic Games; thus, the people of Croton were known for their athletic prowess.</note>; the Spartan loves armor; the Cretan loves hunting; the Sybarite loves luxury; and the Ionian loves dances. <note>original: "Nullum crimen abeſt..." from Juvenal's *Satires* (6.294). The poet argues that Roman virtue was preserved by poverty, and that once wealth arrived, the vices of places like Sybaris infected the city. The Ister is the Danube river.</note>
Strabo also agrees, who narrates that the Sybarites, despite all their prosperity, were driven out by the people of Croton in only seventy days because of their delicacies and luxury. Indeed, Plutarch also attributes a saying to a Sybarite in his *Life of Pelopidas*, <margin>The Sybarite’s</margin> <margin>silly saying.</margin> denying that it was a wonder the Spartans threw themselves into death during wars, since they did so to escape such great labors and that meager diet which they used. <note>This is a famous ancient joke: the Sybarites claimed Spartans were brave only because their lives and food (like the infamous "black broth") were so miserable that death was a welcome relief.</note> Aristotle, moreover, in the first book of the *Eudemian Ethics*, named Smindyrides the Sybarite—a man comparable to Sardanapalus <note>Sardanapalus was a legendary king of Assyria, often cited in antiquity as the ultimate example of effeminate luxury.</note>—among the notorious seekers of pleasure. The title refers to a "staked girdle" or "embroidered belt," used metaphorically by Africanus to describe a collection of varied knowledge.</note> For he narrates that the Sybarites reached such a point of luxury that they introduced horses into their banquets, which were so trained that upon hearing the music of a flute, they would immediately rear themselves up on their hind legs; with their forelegs acting in the place of hands, they would perform certain gestures of <term>chironomy</term> <note>From the Greek *cheironomia*, a form of expressive pantomime or rhythmic movement using the hands and arms.</note> <margin>Chirono-</margin> <margin>my.</margin> and dance-like motions in time to the beat. But, he says, a certain flute-player there, having been treated with an insult, fled to their enemies, the Crotoniates (who had been conquered by the Sybarites a little while before), and because...

A player of the aulos, a double-reed instrument often used in both military and festive contexts.</note> was appointed as military leader by the people of Croton; he summoned all those in the city who practiced the same craft, taught them a melody, and when the time seemed right, ordered them to advance against the enemy. But the Sybarites, swollen with pride because they were superior in cavalry, marched out to meet them and joined battle. At this point, at a suddenly given signal, all the flute-players played together in unison. And look! <margin>The horses' dances.</margin> Immediately upon recognizing the melody and that familiar song, the Sybarite horses reared up and stood on their hind legs, throwing off their riders; the dance they had learned at home, they now performed even in the line of battle. <note>The Sybarites were so devoted to luxury that they reportedly trained their war-horses to dance to music for their entertainment, a vanity that the people of Croton exploited by using musicians to disrupt the Sybarite charge.</note> Thus all the cavalrymen were captured while lying on the ground, and all the horses were captured while performing their dances. So much from [Julius] Africanus <note>Sextus Julius Africanus (c. 160 – c. 240 AD), an early Christian traveler and historian whose work "Cesti" contained a variety of historical and natural curiosities.</note> concerning the Sybarites.

A PASSAGE IN THE TRAGEDY
of Seneca is corrected, and explained by a more remote Greek
myth concerning the Nemean lion.

CHAPTER XVII.

[Large initial I] n the tragedy of Seneca which is titled *Hercules Furens* <note>original: "Hercules furens," also known as *The Mad Hercules*.</note>, this short six-foot verse is read: <margin>A passage from Seneca.</margin>
*Let high Atlas conceive beasts from the moon.* <note>original: "Sublimis atlas luna concipiat feras." This refers to line 83 of the play.</note>
For an ancient manuscript from the public library of the Medici family has it differently, and not "Atlas," as is found in the common copies. <margin>Common copies.</margin> You would not easily find the understanding of this passage from any source other than Achilles <note>Achilles Tatius, a 2nd-century Greek astronomer and commentator on Aratus, not to be confused with the novelist of the same name.</note>, an author whom Julius Firmicus <note>Julius Firmicus Maternus, a 4th-century Roman lawyer and astrologer.</note> also cites and praises in his books on *Mathematics* <note>original: "Matheseon." In this period, "mathematics" often referred specifically to astrology and astronomy.</note>. Therefore, in his commentaries on Aratus, when he speaks of the moon, he writes word for word as follows: <margin>Habitations of the Moon.</margin>
"They say there is another dwelling place upon [the moon], and rivers and all things that are upon the earth, and they relate in myth that the Nemean lion fell from there." <note>original: "εἶναι δὲ ἐπ’ αὐτῆς οἴκησιν ἄλλην ποταμούς τε καὶ ὅσα ἐπὶ γῆς, καὶ τὸν λέοντα τὸν νεμαῖον ἐκεῖθεν πεσεῖν μυθολογοῦσιν."</note>
That is to say: In it there are also inhabitants, and rivers, and other things, just as on earth. Indeed, they tell the story that the Nemean lion fell from there.
From this, we wrote in our poem *The Nurse*: <note>original: "Nutrice." This refers to Poliziano’s own work, the *Nutricia*, a poem on the history of poetry.</note>
*...and the Nemean*
*wastes that claimed the moon-born*
*beast as a lie.* <note>original: "Nemiaeáq; tesqua lunigenam mentita feram." Poliziano is referencing the myth that the lion was not native to Earth but fell from the moon.</note>

->MISCELLANIES. 27<-

WHAT "LITTLE BROTHER OF A GIANT"
means in Juvenal. Also what "Son of Earth" means in other authors, and likewise concerning the name "Manius."

CHAPTER XVIII.
Why I would rather be the little brother of a giant. <note>original: "Vnde fit ut malim fraterculus esse gigantis." This is a quote from Juvenal, *Satires* 4.98.</note>

[Large initial W] hoever has attempted to interpret this verse by the poet Juvenal from his fourth Satire has, as far as I can see, remained stuck in a rut. <note>original: "hæret in salebra." A Latin idiom meaning to be stuck in a difficult or rugged place.</note> They do not seem to me to truly explain it, but rather to hunt for a laugh with old wives' tales, or more accurately, they deserve to be laughed at themselves. Indeed, some invent stories about giants being turned into monkeys; others claim that after the giants, a monkey was produced by the Earth to show contempt for the gods. Other explanations are even more "chilly" than these, which, like that orator mentioned by Martial, could <margin>To cool the Neronian baths.</margin> cool down the Neronian baths. <note>Martial (3.25) joked that a certain "cold" (boring) rhetorician was so frigid he could lower the temperature of Nero's famously hot steam baths. The author is calling other scholars' theories boring and lifeless.</note> For these trifles are told, as far as I can understand, with no sense and (to my knowledge) no supporting author. To me, "little brother of a giant" seems to signify <margin>Little brother of a giant.</margin> a man born of no ancestors, of unknown lineage, and obscure—since the ancients were accustomed to commonly call anyone whose parents were unknown a "Son of Earth." <note>original: "Terræ filium." This term was used for people of low or obscure birth, as if they had just sprung from the ground with no family tree.</note> From this, Persius writes:
*Manius is ready to be my heir,*
*A son of the earth; ask me who my fourth*
*Father was, I could not easily say, yet add one more.* <note>original: "Præsto est mihi Manius hæres / Progenies terræ, quære ex me quis mihi quartus / Sit pater, haud prompte dicam, tamẽ adde etiam unũ." From Persius, *Satires* 6.57-60. Manius was a name typically associated with beggars or people of the lowest social class.</note>

Indeed, one is even now a "son of the earth." <note>original: "Vnũ etiam, terræ eſt iam filius." <meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the idiom "son of the earth" (terrae filius). Having previously cited Persius and Cicero, the author now turns to Early Christian apologists—Lactantius, Minucius Felix, and Tertullian—to show how the myth of Saturn was rationalized using these same linguistic conventions to denote obscure or sudden origins.</meta>

->BOOK<-

In the first book of the *Divine Institutes*, he uses these words: <note>The author refers to Lactantius (c. 250 – c. 325 AD), an early Christian author who often used classical logic to critique pagan myths.</note> Minucius Felix, in that book titled *Octavius*, argued thus: that Saturn, when he had been put to flight by his son and had come into Italy, was called a "son of Heaven" <note>original: "Cæli filium"</note> because we are accustomed to say of those whose virtue we admire, or who arrive suddenly among people, that they have "fallen from heaven." He was called a son "of the Earth," <note>original: "Terræ"</note> however, because we name those born of unknown parents "sons of the earth." Thus far Lactantius. 

But Tertullian also, in his *Apology*, writes thus concerning the same Saturn: "He was called the son of Earth <margin>Sons of Heaven.</margin> and the son of Heaven, because those who are unknown, or who appear unexpectedly, are said to have arrived from heaven—just as the common people call 'sons of the earth' those whose lineage is uncertain." 

Therefore, since he preferred this—to be an obscure man rather than a noble youth (because in former times, old age among the nobility was almost a miracle, seeing as the Roman emperors raged against them), he says he prefers to be a "little brother of a giant," <note>This is a reference to the satirist Juvenal (Satire 4), who joked that surviving to old age as a nobleman under a tyrant was so rare it was a miracle; it was safer to be a "son of the earth" or a "little brother of a giant."</note> that is, a "son of the earth." This, as we have shown, signifies someone obscure, rather than someone noble and patrician. For the giants were brought forth from the earth. He used the more charming term "little brother" <note>original: "fraterculum"</note> rather than "brother," lest he seem to desire that vastness and mass of a giant’s body, but rather only the lowliness of their ignoble origin.

<summary>This page explores how early Christian writers like Lactantius and Tertullian interpreted the mythological origins of Saturn. They argue that titles like "son of heaven" or "son of earth" were not literal, but were common idioms used to describe people of unknown or sudden arrival.</summary>

<keywords>Lactantius, Tertullian, Saturn, son of the earth, terrae filius, giants, Roman nobility, etymology, classical myth interpretation</keywords>

# MISCELLANEA. 28

## ON THE AFOREMENTIONED ASPIRATION,
along with an explanation of Catullus’s noble epigram.

### CHAPTER XIX

**Q**uintilian, in the first book of the *Institutes of Oratory*, <note>Marcus Fabius Quintilianus (c. 35 – c. 100 AD) was a Roman rhetorician whose textbook on oratory remained influential for centuries.</note> dealing with the matter of aspiration, speaks as follows: <margin>The use of aspiration.</margin> "The ancients used it very sparingly, even with vowels, when they used to say *aedos* and *ircos* <note>original: "ædos, ircosq;" — These refer to goats (*haedos*) and bucks (*hircos*). In early Latin, the initial 'h' was often dropped.</note>; then, for a long time afterward, the practice was maintained of not aspirating consonants, such as in *Graccis* and *triumpis* <note>original: "Graccis, & in triumpis" — Later standard Latin used the aspirated versions *Gracchis* and *triumphis*.</note>. Soon, an excessive usage erupted, such that *choronæ* (crowns), *chenturiones* (centurions), and *præchones* (heralds) still remain in certain inscriptions. Regarding this matter, there is a noble epigram by Catullus." Thus says Quintilian. We believe, however, that it is this epigram in particular that is being cited:

->Arrius used to say *Chommoda* <note>An over-aspirated version of *commoda*, meaning "advantages" or "profits."</note> whenever he meant to say *commoda*,<-
->And *hinsidias* <note>An over-aspirated version of *insidias*, meaning "ambushes" or "snares."</note> when he meant *insidias*.<-
->And then he hoped he had spoken marvelously,<-
->When he had said *hinsidias* with as much breath as he could.<-
->I believe his mother, his free uncle,<-
->And his maternal grandfather and grandmother had spoken thus.<-
->When this man was sent to Syria, everyone's ears found rest;<-
->They heard these same words [pronounced] softly and lightly.<-
->Nor did they fear such words after that,<-
->When suddenly a horrible messenger arrives...<-
<note>This is Catullus 84. The poem satirizes Arrius, a man of lower-class origins trying to sound sophisticated by over-using the 'h' sound (aspiration), a habit he likely inherited from his uneducated family.</note>

F iij <note>This is a signature mark, "F3," used by early printers to keep the folded sheets of the book in the correct order.</note>
