Having introduced Catullus’s satire of Arrius’s over-aspiration on the previous page, the author now concludes the poem and moves into a deeper historical investigation of how 'h' was used by the ancients versus the Romans of Cicero’s time, citing major grammatical authorities.</meta>

8 BOOK

->the Ionian waves, after Arrius had gone there,<-
->were no longer Ionian, but "Hionian." <note>original: "Hionios" — The poet Catullus jokes that Arrius's habit of adding a breathy 'h' to the start of words was so infectious it even changed the name of the Ionian Sea.</note><-

It must indeed be observed that in pronunciation, just as in writing, one should aspirate the words. This is a practice which the Greeks now retain only with consonants, while the Latins have not retained it even with consonants. <note>In Greek, "aspiration" (the rough breathing) is marked on initial vowels and 'rho', and exists in consonants like phi (φ), chi (χ), and theta (θ). The author notes that Latin usage has become less consistent.</note> On the contrary, the ancient Latins used to use aspiration with vowels even when speaking. Regarding this, Cicero writes as follows in his *Orator* addressed to Brutus: <note>Cicero’s *Orator* (46 BC) is a treatise on the ideal speaker and the history of style.</note> "Indeed, I myself, though I knew our ancestors spoke in such a way that they never used aspiration except with a vowel, used to speak so that I said *pulcros* (beautiful), *Cetegos* (the Cethegi), *triumphos* (triumphs), and *Cartaginem* (Carthage). <note>original: "pul-cros, Cetegos, triumphos, Cartaginẽ" — In this passage, Cicero describes his resistance to the then-new fashion of adding 'h' to consonants (turning *p* into *ph*, etc.) under Greek influence. He eventually gave in to popular usage.</note> Eventually, and quite late, due to the protest of my ears—when the truth had been wrung from me—I conceded the manner of speaking to the public, <margin>The usage of speaking to the people.</margin> <margin>Triumph.</margin> while I reserved the true knowledge for myself." So says Cicero. 

But I myself recently saw a gold coin at the house of Lorenzo de' Medici <note>Lorenzo 'the Magnificent' (1449–1492), the Florentine statesman and famous patron of humanist scholarship.</note> inscribed with the title T R I V M P V S. And likewise another silver one with the more common spelling, T R I V M P H V S. Furthermore, Aulus Gellius, in the second book of his *Attic Nights*, <note>Aulus Gellius (c. 125 – 180 AD) was a Roman author whose miscellany preserved many fragments of older Latin literature and linguistic lore.</note> testifies that the ancients inserted the letter 'h' (or the breath) into many words to "firm up" and "strengthen" them (as he says), so that their sound would be more "fresh and vigorous." Moreover, Priscian <note>Priscian (fl. 500 AD) was a Latin grammarian whose work *Institutes of Grammar* was the standard textbook for the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</note> says that aspiration is attached to vowels externally so that it sounds very little, but to consonants internally so that it sounds most prominent. Therefore, if you wish for the wit of Catullus’s epigram to be clear to you, you must understand that aspiration in its proper place, which...

And yet, when I was still almost a youth, I reported on this very passage to Domizio <note>Domizio Calderini (1446–1478) was a prominent Italian humanist. Aurelio of Rimini knows this (to leave others aside), who now lives in Padua, a young man of outstanding talent and literacy; he knows how many years ago he received that explanation of ours while he was then living in Florence, and how (as he himself later recounted in Padua) it seemed entirely new and unknown even then to everyone to whom he spoke of it. Furthermore, we lectured on the entirety of Catullus at Verona—Baccio Ugolini <note>Baccio Ugolini was a Florentine entertainer and diplomat close to the Medici family.</note>, a man of most free-spirited talent, knows this—inside a certain workshop where the rain had driven us. There were also two men, by no means unlearned, who were then teaching in Verona, as well as a large band of students. ## THE WORD "ASCOPERA" EMENDED AND EXPLAINED IN SUETONIUS.

### CHAPTER XX.

The manuscripts of Suetonius are found to be corrupted in the *Life of Nero*. For in almost all of them, the text reads: *Around the neck of another, both a broom was tied, and at the same time a label: "I did what I could, but you deserved the sack."* <note>original Latin: "Alterius collo, & scopa deligata, simulq; titulus. Ego quod potui, sed tu culleum meruisti." The "sack" refers to the *poena cullei*, an ancient Roman punishment for parricide where the condemned was sewn into a leather bag with a dog, a rooster, a viper, and a monkey, then thrown into the sea. This was a direct jab at Nero for the murder of his mother.</note> However, "broom" <note>original: *scopa*</note> is not used in the singular number in Latin, and even if it were used in that way very frequently, a broom has no connection or relevance to the "sack."

But indeed, in the ancient copies, a trace remains (as I believe) of the uncorrupted and true reading, in this manner: *Around the neck of another, an "ascopa" was tied.* Therefore, if you write the word by including the penultimate letters, it becomes <margin>Ascopera.</margin> **Ascopera**, which I think is the most correct reading. For an *ascopera* is a leather pouch; it is a compound name in Greek <note>original: *askopera* (ἀσκοπήρα)</note> derived from "wine-skin" <note>Greek: *askos*</note> and "traveler’s bag" <note>Greek: *pera*</note>. Regarding this word, Suidas <note>The *Suda* is a massive 10th-century Byzantine Greek historical encyclopedia. In the Renaissance, "Suidas" was mistakenly believed to be the name of the author.</note> writes as follows: *An "askopera" is a pouch or a little bag.* <note>original Greek: ἀσκοπήρα τὸ μαρσύπιον ἤτοι σακκοπήνιον</note> Suidas indicates that "ascopera" signifies a pouch—

<summary>Poliziano defends his scholarly originality and corrects a corrupted passage in Suetonius's Life of Nero, arguing that the word "scopa" (broom) should actually be "ascopera" (a leather pouch).</summary>
<keywords>Suetonius, Nero, Ascopera, philology, textual criticism, Suda, Greek etymology, Roman punishment, parricide</keywords>

MISCELLANIES. 30

...teaches us about the *ascopera*. This, therefore, was the *ascopera* <note>original: *ascopera*, a Greek-derived word for a leather travel bag</note> tied to the neck of Nero’s statue—a symbol of the leather sack, since he was a murderer of his mother. For because parricides are sewn into a leather sack <note>original: *culleus*</note> made of ox-hide, the person who tied the *ascopera* [to the statue] represented a kind of likeness of that very sack. Juvenal writes thus concerning the parricide and the sack:

*And he must be led to the sea in an ox-hide, with which*
*An innocent monkey is enclosed by a cruel fate.* <note>original: "Et deducendum corio bouis in mare, cum quo Clauditur aduersis innoxia simia fatis." Juvenal, Satire 13. The monkey was one of the animals famously sewn into the bag with a parricide.</note>

## WHAT THE "MINERVAL" OR THE "MINERVAL GIFT" IS,
## AND AN EXPLANATION OF THE OPINIONS OF JUVENAL AND VARRO BASED ON THIS.

### CHAPTER XXI.

*Whoever still worships Minerva, won with a single penny...* <note>original: "Quisquis adhuc uno partā colit asse Mineruā." The opening of Juvenal’s Satire 10, describing a schoolboy’s humble aspirations.</note>

Domitius <note>referring to Domitius Calderini, a scholar often criticized by Poliziano</note> explains this passage from the tenth Satire of the poet Juvenal so poorly that he seems to have in no way perceived its true meaning. He interprets "Minerva won" <note>original: *Mineruam partam*</note> to mean she whose Quinquatrian games <note>The Quinquatrus was a festival of Minerva held in March, often associated with the school calendar.</note> the student obtained so that he might be able to compete. What could be more absurd or more awkward than this? I ask you, can anyone achieve the ability to compete publicly in eloquence for a single penny? Rather, it seems that Juvenal could have been hinting at the "minerval gift"—which is also called the *minerval*—namely, the small fee for teaching usually given to masters by their students...

<summary>Poliziano concludes his argument that a leather bag (ascopera) was used to mock Nero as a parricide, then begins a new chapter defining the "Minerval" as a teacher's fee rather than a prize for competition.</summary>

...and I judge it most <note>The text begins mid-word, likely "fitting" or "useful."</note> before the Quinquatria <note>A five-day Roman festival held in March in honor of Minerva, the goddess of wisdom and school-children.</note>, during which days boys were on holiday from school. Thus Horace <note>A leading Roman lyric poet during the time of Augustus.</note> writes at the end of his epistle:

<margin>One must be free from schools during the Quinquatria.</margin>

Rather, like a boy once did during the festive Quinquatria, 
May you enjoy this brief and pleasing time in haste. 
<note>original: "Ac potius puer ut feſtis quinquatribus olim, / Exiguo, gratóq; fruaris tempore raptim."</note>

This was the custom, just as it was during the Saturnalia <note>A mid-winter festival in honor of Saturn, characterized by gift-giving and a temporary reversal of social roles.</note> and the Calends <note>The first day of the month; "Calends" often referred to New Year’s Day gifts.</note>. Despise her; she will draw in new disciples. 
<note>original: "Nec uos turba feri cenſu fraudata magiſtri, / Spernite, diſcipulos attrahet illa nouos."</note>

He says: "Let the Bishops and priests read this, who educate their sons in secular literature and make them read comedies and sing the filthy writings of stage actors—perhaps even educated at the expense of the church. That which a virgin, or a widow, or any poor person pouring out their entire substance had offered to the <term>corban</term> <note>A Hebrew term for an offering or sacrifice dedicated to God; here referring to the church treasury.</note> for their sins, the grammarian and rhetorician turns into a New Year’s gift for the Calends,

Varro <note>A Roman scholar and writer on agriculture.</note> also, in the third book of *On Agriculture*, says this regarding the <term>Minervale</term>: Axius says, "My dear Merula, receive me as a student of villa-husbandry." 

<margin>Minervale.</margin>

He replies: "As soon as you have promised the <term>Minervale</term> fee, I shall begin."

For this is how everyone commonly reads it, and it is set forth as the most correct version by whoever is most celebrated in the cultivation of letters. We, on the contrary, have found it thus in many ancient manuscripts of less wavering authority and reliability: *Atlas is no less laughed at with a companion mule.* <note>original: "Non aliter ridetur Atlas cum compare mulo." This refers to Martial's *Epigrams* 5.17.4.</note> With a "mule," I say, and not a "hunchback." <note>The common but corrupt reading Poliziano is correcting was "gibbo" (hunchback) instead of "mulo" (mule).</note> And lest it be argued with "deaf testimonies" <margin>Deaf testimonies.</margin> (as the saying goes), in this very public library of the Medici family, a most ancient manuscript is kept in Lombardic script <note>original: "Langobardis literis." A style of handwriting used in the early Middle Ages, often difficult for Renaissance scholars to decipher.</note>, which Domitius <note>Domizio Calderini (1446–1478), a prominent Italian humanist and rival of Poliziano.</note> also once read through in Florence. But also in Verona, a certain young man named Bernardinus—who at that time seemed quite noble <margin>Bernardinus, relative of Domitius.</margin> and was a relative of Domitius—lent me some pages of an equally ancient volume, while Baccius Ugolinus <margin>Baccius Ugolinus.</margin> <note>Baccio Ugolini, a Florentine diplomat and poet who was a close friend of Poliziano.</note>, a man of most perfect and brilliant genius, was there with me. Nor was it any different in Rome, where Bernardinus Valla <margin>Bernardinus Valla.</margin>—a man to be celebrated for his devotion to poetry, his knowledge of law, the nobility of his lineage, and his wealth, as well as for a certain uncommon refinement—showed us a volume of Martial likewise in Lombardic characters and allowed us to read it. Moreover, in Florence, besides these, Pandulfus Oricellarius <margin>Pandulfus Oricellarius.</margin> <note>Pandolfo Rucellai (1436–1504), a Florentine merchant and diplomat from a prominent family.</note>, a citizen of the highest nobility, gave us another semi-ancient manuscript to use, in every one of which you may find this reading which we advocate. *From these mules of yours, you need not fear a fall,*

<note>original: "His tibi de mulis non est metuenda ruina, / Altius in terra penè sedere soles." This couplet mocks the dwarf for riding a mule so small that he is practically already on the floor.</note>

<note>original: "Et molles dare iussa quod choreas / Nigro bellua nil negat magistro." This refers to trained elephants in Roman arenas.</note>

This fits the purpose and argument of the poet [Martial] against that man who—though young, strong, and poor—was nevertheless repeatedly carried in a litter by six men, who were themselves also young, strong, and poor. <note>The poet is mocking the social pretension of a poor man acting like a wealthy noble.</note> And just like those mentioned above, he provided an occasion for everyone to mock him no less than they did the others.

<meta>This page transitions from the satirical commentary on Martial found on the previous page to a new chapter of philological inquiry. ->**BOOK**<-

<margin>The sacred Hecalesia.</margin>
...is described, by whom Theseus, while he was once a young man, was kindly received with hospitality. From this, a sacred festival called the *Hecalesia* was celebrated throughout the villages in honor of "Hecalean" Jupiter, including honors for Hecale herself. They also used to call her "Hecalene," using a diminutive form of her name, because she herself, in an elderly and grandmotherly fashion, was accustomed to greeting the still-youthful Theseus and receiving him with the affectionate endearments of such diminutive names. Since she had vowed a sacrifice to Jupiter for Theseus’s return when he set out for battle, but she died before he could return, she received this tribute in exchange for her hospitality by Theseus’s command, as Philochorus <note>An ancient Athenian historian and expert on religious customs, active in the 3rd century BCE.</note> has recorded in his history. Plutarch also mentions this matter in his *Life of Theseus*. Furthermore, Callimachus wrote a proper poem about her titled *Hecale*, as a verse from a very well-known epigram also suggests:

*I sing of the ways of that hospitable old woman.*
<note>original: "Μέλπω δὲ γραὸς τῆς φιλοξένου τρόπους." This line emphasizes the moral focus of Callimachus’s lost epic: the virtue of humble hospitality.</note>

And many authors are found here and there citing Callimachus’s *Hecale*. For even Pliny occasionally lists edible herbs which that country-woman Hecale serves in Callimachus’s work,
<margin>Samphire. Sow-thistle.</margin>
such as samphire <term>Crethmon: a succulent herb found on coastal rocks, often pickled</term> in his twenty-sixth book. So too he mentions sow-thistle <term>Sonchon: a common wild edible plant, similar to a dandelion but taller</term> in the twenty-second book; although in the newer manuscript copies "Hecate" is written there instead of "Hecale." However, a trace of the true reading still remains in those ancient manuscripts which are kept in the public library of the Medici family. <note>Poliziano frequently used the private and public collections of his patrons, the Medici, to correct errors that had crept into Latin texts over centuries of copying.</note> Indeed, where Callimachus says in his *Hymn to Apollo*:

*Envy spoke secretly into the ears of Apollo.*
<note>original: "ὁ φθόνος ἀπόλλωνος ἐπ᾽ οὔατα λάθριος εἶπεν." This famous passage concludes Callimachus's hymn, where Apollo defends the poet's refined, shorter style against those who prefer massive, unrefined epics.</note>

MISCELLANIES. 34

“I do not admire the singer who does not sing as much as the sea.”
<note>original: "οὐκ ἄγαμαι τ' ἀοιδὸν, ὃς οὐδ᾽ ὅσα πόντος ἀείδει." This line from Callimachus’s *Hymn to Apollo* (line 106) serves as his poetic manifesto, where the "sea" represents the vast, muddy epics he rejected in favor of shorter, refined works.</note>

Regarding this passage, the commentator explains it roughly as follows. By these words, he says, he accuses those who mocked him, as if he were unable to produce a long poem. Because of this, he was compelled to write the *Hecale*. The commentator’s actual words are: “Through these lines, he rebukes those who mocked him for being unable to create a great poem; hence he was forced to write the *Hecale*.”
<note>original: "ἐγκαλεῖ διὰ τούτων τοὺς σκώπτοντας αὐτὸν μὴ δύνασθαι ποιῆσαι μέγα ποίημα, ὅθεν ἠναγκάσθη ποιῆσαι τὴν ἑκάλην."</note>

From this, I wrote in my poem *The Foster Mother* <term>Nutricia: A didactic poem by Poliziano himself, which served as an introduction to his lectures on the history of poetry.</term>:

*The son of Battus <note>Callimachus was often called Battiades, or "son of Battus," referring to the legendary founder of his birthplace, Cyrene.</note> sings of Hecale and the deeds at Marathon, loftier than his usual tone.*

But Statius also speaks of this same woman at the end of his *Thebaid*:

*Nor did the hospitable old woman shed empty tears.*

And Suidas <note>The *Suda*, a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia and dictionary.</note> states that Hecale is a heroine of some kind in Callimachus’s work. Finally, Apuleius, in the first book of his *Metamorphoses*, writes (I believe) of the same woman: “If you are content,” he says, “with a small hearth, you will emulate the virtues of your father, that namesake of Theseus, who did not despise the humble hospitality of the old woman Hecale.” 

However, I am not unaware that the text is corrupted almost everywhere; for indeed, you will often find it written not as “the old woman Hecale,” but “the old woman Ales,” <margin>A passage of Apuleius restored.</margin> because several letters have been cut off from the start. Let us take this opportunity to cleanse the manuscripts of Apuleius in passing, so that we may send the reader away laden, as if with a bonus gift <term>superpondio: a Latin term for an "extra weight" or "surplus," used here to mean an additional scholarly insight provided to the reader for free.</term>.

**BOOK**

**HOW MANY MATTERS IN THE**
***Familiar Letters*** **now attributed to Cicero**
**are out of order, and into what order**

**CHAPTER XXV.**

<margin>A manuscript of Cicero's letters.</margin>

I have obtained a very ancient volume of Cicero’s *Letters to Friends* <note>original: *Epistolae Familiares*. These letters are a cornerstone of Roman history and Latin prose style.</note>, of which I have already spoken above; and then another copy transcribed from that very one, which some believe to be in the hand of Francesco Petrarch. That it was indeed transcribed from the first is clear from many proofs that I shall omit for now. However, this latter manuscript I mentioned was so poorly bound together by a careless bookseller <note>The Latin *bibliopola* refers here to the person responsible for the physical assembly and binding of the book.</note> that one misplaced gathering of ten pages <term>decuria: a grouping of ten leaves or pages in a manuscript.</term> can be detected, contrary to how the pages are actually numbered. This book is kept in the public library of the Medici family <note>The *Bibliotheca Medicea*, now known as the Laurentian Library in Florence, where Poliziano served as a curator and scholar under Lorenzo de' Medici.</note>. From this single source, as far as I can gather, all the manuscripts of these same letters currently in existence have clearly flowed, as if from a fountainhead; and in all of them, the order of the text is jumbled and distorted, which I must now restore to its proper place and, as it were, renew. Therefore, I ask for your close attention, whoever you are who embraces these liberal studies. In the eighth book, then, a letter from Caelius <note>Marcus Caelius Rufus, a brilliant but volatile orator and protégé of Cicero.</note> to Cicero begins thus:

*“Certainly,” I say, “he has been acquitted; with me acting as his representative, the verdict was pronounced, and indeed by all the ranks, and even by individual votes in every class.”*
<note>original: "Certe, inquam, absolutus est, me repræsentante, pronunciatū est, & quidem omnibus ordinibus, sed & singulis in unoquoq; genere sententijs." This refers to the acquittal of a defendant across all the "orders" (senators, knights, and tribunes) who made up the Roman juries.</note>

"Look now," you say, "up to this point the order has not yet been interrupted." But the words that immediately follow—***you will have shown by letters*** <note>original: *literis ostenderis*</note>, and so forth—you will find to be entirely different from the preceding ones, and indeed part of another letter. Go forward then from that very letter, counting the following ones in order, until you reach the twenty-fourth, which begins thus: ***No, by Hercules, for nothing ever*** <note>original: *Non me Hercules, nihil unquam enim*</note>. This letter is actually continuous with the former one, so that you should read them together as follows: "Certainly, I say, he was acquitted; while I was present, the judgment was pronounced, and indeed by all ranks, but also by individual votes in every category. Look now," you say. ***No, by Hercules. For nothing ever.*** And so weave the rest together, until from that one which we said belonged to another letter, you arrive at the tenth following one, which begins: ***So you, you say, regard Hirtius?*** <note>original: *Sic tu, inquis, Hircium?* A reference to Aulus Hirtius, a close associate of Julius Caesar.</note> Read that until you fall upon these words: ***if any hope*** <note>original: *si ullam spem*</note>. As soon as you have come across them, you shall delete this entire section. Or, ***in a roof of a flaw*** <note>original: *in tecto vitii*. This is a corrupt reading Poliziano is identifying in the manuscript.</note>, the rest will be approved by me. All this, I say, you will delete or strike out <term>induces: literally "to lead over," referring to the scribal practice of drawing a line through text to cancel it.</term>, since these words are clearly foreign to this place. Soon you will return again to those things which were wrongly placed in that letter of Caelius, of which we spoke first, and thus you will continue the reading. If you read ***if any hope you have shown by letters*** <note>original: *si ullam spem literis ostenderis*</note> in a continuous flow, you will arrive at the twenty-third letter from this one, which begins: ***I shall respond to two of his letters*** <note>original: *Duabus eius epistolis respondebo*</note>, and close it with this ending.

Certainly these two verses were made from a very famous and witty couplet <term>disticho: a poem of two lines</term> by a Greek poet, which is especially celebrated—

...and flies, learned, through the mouths of men. <note>Poliziano is completing a quote from the poet Ennius: "I fly living through the mouths of men," used here to describe how these famous verses circulate among the public.</note> For when Ovid records that "someone" said those things, he surely pointed his finger at Evenus <note>Evenus of Ascalon, a Greek epigrammatist from the 1st century BCE/CE whose work is preserved in the *Greek Anthology*.</note> himself, the author of the poem. > Even if you should consume me down to the root, yet I will still bear fruit, 
> enough to pour as a libation upon you, O goat, when you are sacrificed.

That most ingenious poet <note>Referring to Ovid.</note> translated these lines as closely as possible; and yet, there are certain things in the Greek which our poet expressed with less detail. Indeed, if truth be told, our poet did not even touch upon that certain "overseas Venus" <note>By "overseas Venus" (transmarinam Venerem), Poliziano refers to a specific type of foreign, likely Greek, stylistic grace or charm.</note> found in the original. This fault lies perhaps with a language that is less playful rather than one that is insufficiently rich. But since we have stumbled upon the mention of this Greek distich <term>distich: a pair of poetic lines making a complete sense</term>, let us also bring forward what is found in Suetonius's life of Domitian, so that we may, in passing, shed the light of interpretation on that not-at-all obscure <margin>A passage of Suetonius.</margin> passage, and give, as it were, an addition that is by no means less valuable than the goods themselves. The words are as follows:

"To grant a pardon regarding the published edict about cutting down the vineyards, he is believed to have been compelled by no other thing more than the fact that pamphlets were scattered about containing these verses:

> Even if you should consume me down to the root, yet I will still bear fruit, 
> enough to pour as a libation upon you, Caesar, when you are sacrificed."
<note>original: κἤν με φάγῃς ἐπὶ ῥίζαν, ὅμως ἔτι καρποφορήσω / ὅσον ἐπισπεῖσαι καίσαρι θυομένῳ.</note>

For what the vine previously threatened against the goat, it now threatens—by Hercules, a most beautiful parody!—against Caesar. Saying, in effect: "Even if you devour me to the root, I shall nevertheless produce enough wine as can be poured out when Caesar is sacrificed." <note>Domitian had issued an unpopular edict to destroy half the vineyards in the Roman provinces to promote grain production. The "parody" changes the word 'goat' to 'Caesar,' implying the Emperor will be killed and the wine used for his funeral rites.</note>

# BOOK [I]

For it was the custom among the ancients that wine should be poured over the heads of sacrificial victims while they stood directly beneath the blow and the knife. This is seen, for instance, in Maro <note>Maro is the cognomen of the poet Virgil.</note>:

> Beautiful Dido herself, holding the libation bowl in her right hand, 
> pours it between the horns of a gleaming white cow.
<note>original: "Ipſa tenens dextra paterā pulcherrima Dido / Candentis uaccæ media inter cornua fundit." — Virgil, Aeneid 4.60-61.</note>

Therefore, people used to mark the Emperor Domitian for death by circulating these verses in pamphlets. For, as the same Suetonius writes, once when there was a supreme abundance of wine but a scarcity of grain, Domitian—thinking that the fields were being neglected because of an excessive devotion to vineyards—issued an edict that no one in Italy should plant new vines, and that in the provinces, vineyards should be cut down, leaving at most half of them; however, he did not persist in carrying out the order. 

Let scholars remember, however, that in this discovery we have served the common interest in more than just one way. This is because in many copies of Suetonius still in circulation, and even in some ancient ones, you will not only fail to find these Greek verses, but not even a trace or a place for them to be inserted. But because we have possessed them for a long time, being as they are most charming, we have tracked them down from obsolete and faulty manuscripts by carefully weighing each detail, and by gradually identifying and restoring the letters.

...the slow-moving noblewomen, whom she, with those most white-washed hands <note>original: "gypsatissimis." No one among those whose opinions have been published has yet suspected that these are not purely Cicero's own words, but rather those of some poet, who was permitted to use voices and figures of that sort—such as those phrases, "who held Corinth as a high citadel" and "wealthy noble matrons." Nor, at the very least, did they notice that two whole iambic verses <note>An iambic verse is a poetic meter common in both Greek and Roman drama.</note> still stand intact in these lines, and that the "torn-apart limbs" of others can be recognized. ->BOOK<-

->WHAT PANIC TERRORS ARE<-
called, and the most abundant witnesses cited for them.

->CHAPTER XXVIII.<-

In Cicero’s letter to Tiro, which is placed at the very end of the volume of *Letters to Friends* <note>Known today as the *Epistulae ad Familiares*.</note>, it is written as follows: "Even if our friend Atticus, who once understood that I was moved by panic terrors <note>original Greek: πανικοῖς (*panikois*)</note>, always thinks the same and does not see with what defenses of philosophy I am hedged about, by Hercules, because he himself is timid, he creates a disturbance <note>original Greek: θορυβοποιεῖ (*thorybopoiei*), meaning to cause a stir or a frantic commotion.</note>." 

"Panic" <margin>Panic.</margin> terrors are called (as I believe) by the Greeks those sudden terrors and consternations, such as "nymph-struck" fears <note>original: "lymphatici metus." In ancient folklore, being "struck by nymphs" (lymphaticus) meant a sudden, irrational madness or terror similar to being "pan-struck."</note>, which are so irrevocable that they lack not only reason but even the use of the mind. The same word is used by Cicero in a letter to Atticus in the fifth book. "For you know," he says, "that certain things are called panic <note>original Greek: πανικὰ (*panika*)</note>." 

Hyginus <note>Gaius Julius Hyginus, a Roman mythographer and astronomer of the 1st century CE.</note> also makes mention of "Panic terror" in the passage concerning the constellation Capricorn in these words: "For this creature is said, when Jupiter was attacking the Titans, to have been the first to cast into the enemies that terror which is called Panic, as Eratosthenes <note>A Greek polymath (c. 276–194 BCE) who wrote on astronomy and geography.</note> says." Germanicus <note>Germanicus Caesar, who translated Aratus's poem on the stars into Latin; the "Arateo" refers to these commentaries.</note> also hints at this fable in his commentary on Aratus. 

Euripides in *Medea* says thus:
"And some aged servant, thinking that either the rages of Pan <note>original Greek: πανὸς ὀργὰς (*panos orgas*). This refers to the god Pan, who was believed to cause sudden, groundless fear in humans and animals.</note> or of some other god had come upon her, raised a ritual cry <note>original Greek: ἀνολόλυξε (*anololyxe*), a loud cry often used in religious contexts, here used as a reaction to perceived divine possession.</note>."

<summary>Poliziano explains the origin and meaning of "panic terrors" by citing Cicero's letters and mythological accounts of the god Pan causing irrational fear. He supports his definition with references to Hyginus, Eratosthenes, and Euripides's Medea.</summary>

<keywords>panic terrors, Cicero, Pan, Medea, Euripides, Hyginus, Atticus, mythology, etymology</keywords>

Euripides calls them "the wraths of Pan," just as it is found in his most ancient commentator <note>Specifically, a scholiast on Euripides's *Medea* or *Rhesus*.</note>: "panic terrors" <note>original Greek: τὰ πανικὰ δείματα (*ta panika deimata*)</note>; that is, "because they attribute the cause of sudden fears and disturbances to Pan" <note>original Greek: ὅτι τὴν τῶν αἰφνιδίων φόβων καὶ ταραχῶν αἰτίαν τῷ πανὶ ἀνατιθέασιν</note>. For, he says, they attribute the cause of sudden terrors and consternations to the god Pan. And in Synesius <note>Synesius of Cyrene (c. 373–414 CE), a Neoplatonist philosopher and bishop.</note>, in his book *On Providence*, I find it almost thus: "and panic disturbances seized the army during the day" <note>original Greek: καὶ πανικοὶ θόρυβοι μεθ' ἡμέραν τὸ στράτευμα κατελάμβανον</note>. And: "Panic tumults occupied the army by day." In which place it is also amply explained what kind of terrors these finally were. Furthermore, some commentator or other explains these very words of Synesius in such a way as to say they are called "Panic" terrors when men and horses in an army are suddenly thrown into confusion, with no apparent cause appearing. For he says that women, inspired by the power of the divinity, are accustomed to celebrate the rites of Pan with shouts, by which those who hear them—being sudden—are struck with fear. But also Nicetes Choniates <margin>Nicetes the rhetorician.</margin> <note>A Byzantine Greek historian and writer (c. 1155–1217 CE).</note>, a Greek author by no means to be despised, mentions "Panic apparitions" in one of his orations. Theon <margin>Theon.</margin> <note>Theon of Alexandria, a 4th-century commentator known for his work on Aratus.</note> also, the commentator on the poet Aratus, says that the god Pan fought against the Titans, and that he seems to have been the first to discover that twisted and spiral shell used as a trumpet, which is called a *Cochlos* <margin>Spiral shell.</margin> in Greek <note>original Greek: κόχλος (*kochlos*)</note>. It was through his effort that his allies had the opportunity to arm themselves, while the Titans were driven into flight by whatever sound that was, which is called "Panic." But also the poet Nonnus, in the tenth book of the *Dionysiaca* <note>original Greek: Διονυσιακῶν (*Dionysiakōn*)</note>, describing him raging—

...concerning the daughters of Thespius who were embraced by Hercules. Why they 
<margin>The Teuthrantian crowd.</margin> are called the "Teuthrantian crowd" is so unknown and obscure to most people that literary scholars dare to replace that word "Teuthrantia" with "Thespeia," which they do quite improperly and ignorantly. We have found information on this in Eustathius, the commentator on Homer, specifically where he explains the "Catalogue of Ships." <note>Eustathius of Thessalonica was a 12th-century scholar; the "Catalogue of Ships" is a famous section of the *Iliad* listing the Greek forces.</note> "The author of the *Ethnica* says that Thespia is a foundation of Thespiades, or according to others, of Thespius, the son of Teuthras and grandson of Pandion." <note>Original Greek: φησὶ δὲ ὁ τῶν ἐθνικῶν συγγραφεὺς ὅτι κτίσμα ἐστὶν ἡ θέσπεια θεσπιάδου κατὰ δ’ ἔνιους θεσπίου. υἱοῦ Τεύθραντος τοῦ πανδίονος. The "author of the *Ethnica*" refers to Stephanus of Byzantium.</note> Based on the authority of someone who wrote about nations, he records that <margin>Thespia.</margin> Thespia was founded by Thespiades, though some write that it was by <margin>Teuthras.</margin> Thespius, the son of Teuthras and grandson of Pandion. Therefore, the daughters of Thespius are called the "Teuthrantian crowd" after their grandfather, Teuthras. Finally, even Aeschylus, in his tragedy *Agamemnon*, called the city of Thespia "of Teuthras."

...we read in Petronius Arbiter: "Then she also tasted a little bit of the meat, and while she was replacing the pig’s head—which was just as old as she was—back onto the meat-rack with a fork, the rotten stool, which had added to her height, broke, and the weight of the old woman sent her tumbling onto the hearth. The neck of the kettle <term>cucuma</term><note>A *cucuma* was a vessel used for boiling water, similar to a modern kettle.</note> was therefore broken, and it extinguished the growing fire, and covered her whole face with the stirred-up ash." <note>This quote is from Petronius’s *Satyricon*, describing the mishap of the priestess Oenothea.</note> From this, I find the derived diminutive <margin>A small kettle.</margin> *cucumion* in Arrian’s writings on Epictetus, specifically in the chapter titled "On Cynicism"—that is, concerning the Cynic profession. I will set down Arrian's words: "He must have a *cucumion* where he can make water warm for the child so that he may bathe it." <note>Original Greek: δεῖ αὐτὸν κουκύμιον ἔχειν ὅπου θερμὸν ποιήσει τῷ παιδίῳ ἵν’ αὐτὸ λούσῃ. Arrian was a 2nd-century historian and student of the Stoic philosopher Epictetus.</note> He used the word *cucumion* following Greek custom, which we might call a *cucumula* <note>a little kettle</note> quite elegantly.

Furthermore, the word <margin>House of prayer.</margin> *Proseucha* <note>From the Greek *proseuche*, meaning a place of prayer or a Jewish meeting house.</note> is thought to be found only in Juvenal to refer to a place for beggars. However, Cleomedes, in the second book of his *Circular Inspection* while arguing against Epicurus, speaks in almost this very way: "Some of these things are adopted from brothels; some are very similar to those things said repeatedly by women at the festivals of Ceres; while others are taken from the middle of a *proseucha* and from those very people who beg there—plainly Jewish and worn out, and even lower than creeping things." <note>Cleomedes was a Stoic astronomer; here he is disparaging the "vulgar" language used by the philosopher Epicurus by comparing it to the talk of beggars in a house of prayer.</note>

...the meaning is also more acute and elegant for this reason: that the host might not even consider the man he has invited as a guest to be a free man, nor think it right for him to speak as a free man. If trustworthy authors did not prove this, I would be forced to admit that nothing is more carefully considered by Domitius <note>Domitius Calderinus, the 15th-century scholar mentioned on the previous page.</note>.

Therefore, Artemidorus of Ephesus—or if he prefers to be called Daldianus <note>A 2nd-century dream interpreter and author of the *Oneirocritica*. His work is a primary source for the social history of the Roman Empire.</note>—in his first book on the interpretation of dreams, wrote in Greek, but to this effect: "I knew a certain slave," he says, "who dreamed that he had three male organs. But later, having been made free, he consequently had three names instead of one, having taken on the two names of his patron." This is from Artemidorus.

Septimius Florens Tertullian <note>An influential early Christian author from Carthage (c. 155 – c. 220 AD).</note> also, in his book *On the Resurrection of the Flesh*, says word for word: "I ask you," he says, "if you change your servant's status through liberty, because the same flesh and soul remain which were previously subject to whips, shackles, and branding-scars <note>original: "stigmatibus." Branding was a common punishment for runaway or criminal slaves.</note>, must they therefore suffer the same treatment? I think not. On the contrary, he is honored by the brilliance of a white garment <note>The white toga of a citizen.</note>, the dignity of a golden ring <note>The *annulus aureus*, a symbol of equestrian rank or special imperial favor.</note>, and the patron's name, tribe, and table."

From this, I believe, comes that line of Persius: "He exits as Marcus Dama." <margin>Passage from Persius</margin> <note>original: "Exit Marcus Dama." From Persius, *Satire* 5.76–79. This refers to the ritual of manumission where a slave (Dama) is turned around by his master and becomes a citizen with a full name (Marcus Dama).</note> Cicero also confirms this custom in the fourth volume of his letters to Atticus, writing thus: "I am grateful regarding Eutychis, who, keeping his old personal name, will be Titus Caecilius by his new name; just as Dionysius is Marcus Pomponius, joined from both me and you." <margin>Passage from Cicero</margin> <note>original: "De Eutychide gratum..." From Cicero, *Letters to Atticus* 4.15. Eutychis, a slave, took the names of his patron (Atticus, whose legal name was Titus Caecilius Pomponianus) upon being freed.</note> But so that we may interpret this passage of Cicero in passing—which is not without its obscurities—one must know that even...

...it was the custom that heirs were adopted into the name and family by testament <note>A legal will</note>. This was the case with Gaius Octavius <note>The future Emperor Augustus</note> by [Julius] Caesar, just as Suetonius writes in his *Life of Julius*. Therefore, after that day, he was always called Caesar, as he is also addressed in the letters of Brutus to Cicero. The same was true for Quintus Scipio, a colleague in the consulship of Pompey; although he was the son of Nasica, he was adopted by will into the clan of Metellus Pius and was also called by Metellus's name. This is recorded by Cassius Dio in the fortieth book of his *Histories*. 

For this reason also, Titus Pomponius Atticus, having been left as heir to three-quarters <note>original: "ex dodrante," a Roman legal term for three-fourths of an estate</note> of the property of Quintus Caecilius—as Cornelius Nepos writes in his *Life of Atticus*—was afterwards called Quintus Caecilius Atticus instead of Titus Pomponius Atticus, according to Varro in the second book of *On Agriculture*. 

<margin>The collection under the name of the Suda.</margin>

Furthermore, he called his manumitted <note>A formal process of freeing a slave</note> slave Eutychides "Titus Caecilius Eutychides." He used his own old first name <note>original: "praenomine"</note>—for he was formerly called Titus Pomponius—joined with his new name, because he had only recently begun to be called Quintus Caecilius. In the same way, the slave Dionysius was called Marcus Pomponius Dionysius, having taken the first name of Cicero <note>Marcus</note> and the family name of Atticus <note>Pomponius</note>. Additionally, Alexander of Miletus, who held the surname Polyhistor <note>A Greek title meaning "Very Learned" or "Many-Storied"</note>, was called Cornelius by Cornelius Lentulus, by whom he had evidently been freed, as the collection known as the *Suda* <note>A massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the Mediterranean world</note> testifies.

MISCELLANIES. 43

**A WORD LONG ABOLISHED HAS BEEN RESTORED TO THE MANUSCRIPTS OF PLINY, EXPLAINING WHAT "POPPYSMUS" OR "POPPYSMA" SIGNIFIES, AND THAT IT WAS A CUSTOM TO ADORE LIGHTNING WITH THESE SOUNDS.**

## CHAPTER XXXII.

A passage in Pliny must be corrected, from the twenty-eighth book of his *Natural History*, chapter two, where he treats the question of whether there is any power in words for the purpose of healing. A single word has been removed from this passage, a word which indicates an ancient custom that is also confirmed by even older testimony. Most manuscripts read as follows: *It is a consensus among nations to worship lightning bolts.* <margin>The passage in Pliny</margin>

But in two ancient copies from the noble library of the Medici family itself <note>The Medici family of Florence were famous patrons of the arts and collectors of ancient manuscripts</note>, it is written thus: *It is a consensus among nations to worship lightning bolts with poppysmi.* <margin>Poppysmi.</margin> <note>original: "poppyfmis"; a poppysmus is a smacking, clucking, or whistling sound made with the lips, often used in ancient times to ward off evil or show religious awe</note>

This reading is confirmed even by the most witty poet Aristophanes, in the comedy entitled *The Wasps* <note>original: "σφῆκες" (Sphekes)</note>. For in that play, a certain old man named Philocleon speaks in such a way that he says he will flash lightning at those who "poppys" <note>original: "poppyſſantibus"; the author is Latinizing the Greek verb for making this clucking sound</note> (if I may use such a word). Furthermore, the most ancient interpreter <note>A scholiast, or ancient commentator on a text</note> says that this is the custom: to make a "poppys" sound at lightning bolts. The words of Aristophanes are these: *And I will flash back at those who cluck.* <note>original Greek: "κἀναστράψω ποππύζουσιν" (kanastrapso poppyzousin)</note> Then the interpreter says this: *It is a custom to cluck at flashes of lightning.* <note>original Greek: "ἔθος ταῖς ἀστραπαῖς ποππύζειν" (ethos tais astrapais poppyzein)</note>

H ij

<summary>The author restores a lost word, "poppysmus," to a passage in Pliny the Elder's Natural History, explaining it as a ritualistic lip-smacking sound used by ancients when witnessing lightning.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the philological investigation into the word "poppysmus," moving from its use in religious contexts to its common application in horsemanship and linguistics, before transitioning to an analysis of a famous metaphor in Juvenal.</meta>

->BOOK<-

Just as the word "poppysma" <note>original: "poppyſma"; a smacking or clucking sound</note> and its plural "poppysmata" are read in the poets Juvenal and Martial, so in Pliny we find the form "poppysmus" <note>original: "poppyſmis", the dative/ablative plural</note> derived from the nominative case *poppysmus*. It is an onomatopoeic word <note>original: "factitia de ſono uox"; a word "made from a sound"</note> signifying that specific sound we use most especially to coax horses that are not yet fully tamed, as Maximus says in his lexicon, and likewise Nicas and Suidas <note>Suidas (or the Suda) is a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the ancient Mediterranean world</note>. Sophocles also uses the verb when he writes: "The driver clucks to his team." <note>original: "ποππύζεται ζευγηλάτρις" (poppyzetai zeugelatris)</note> Pollux also mentions this in the seventh book of his *Onomasticon* <note>An ancient Greek dictionary or thesaurus</note>. Indeed, in his first book, he even teaches that a horse is brought to a halt by a *poppysmus*, employing this very word. Pliny also uses it in the thirty-fifth book of that same *Natural History*, where he says: "A similar success is said to have followed the painter Nealces <note>A Greek painter of the 3rd century BCE</note> regarding the foam of a horse; it is said a sponge thrown at the canvas achieved the effect while he was painting a man clucking <note>original: "poppyzonta"</note> as he held back the horse." Dexippus the Platonist philosopher, in the dialogue he published on Aristotle's *Categories*, also writes: "Or if there is some unarticulated sound, such as in smacking or clucking sounds." <note>original: "ἢ εἴτις ἄναρθρος ψόφος, ὡς ἐπὶ ποππυσμάτων" (e itis anarthros psophos, hos epi poppysmaton)</note>

...were drawn from histories, just as some are indeed handled even to this day, either from truth and reality, if perhaps something recent had occurred. Thus, they were accustomed to be proposed with the names of places added, and certainly, they are found collected and published in this manner. Therefore, the "Cambre" <note>original: "Cambre"; a specific title for a rhetorical exercise</note> inscription is a title of a declamation <note>a formal rhetorical exercise or speech practiced in schools</note> named after a place. For Cambre is a town in Teuthrania in the Troad <note>the region around ancient Troy</note>, as Pliny writes; and nearby is the region of Lesbos, in which tyrants occupied Mitylene, as Strabo attests: Nigessilus and Megarogilus. The poet Alcaeus attacked them in his poetry, which is why he is said to have possessed a "golden plectrum" <note>original: "aureum plectrum"; a tool for plucking a lyre, signifying divine or superior poetic skill</note>, according to Fabius <note>Marcus Fabius Quintilianus, the famous Roman rhetorician</note>. Regarding the expelled tyrants, the "Cambre" declamation was always to be repeated by the teachers.

This interpretation is pleasing, as it agrees with what he said:

->*When a numerous fleet destroys the cruel tyrants.*<-
<note>original: "Cum perimit sæuos classis numerosa tyrannos"</note>

As for those who derive the "Cambrian" declamation from Cambres, king of the Scythians, whom Diodorus mentions, that is in no way appropriate. Thus far Domitius <note>Domitius Calderinus, a 15th-century humanist scholar known for his difficult and often criticized commentaries</note> has argued in an altogether winding and evasive speech; from his words, this conclusion is finally gathered—though the reasoning is forced and confused—that the "Cambre" declamation is named after a town in Teuthrania, because names are given to declamations from places, and tyrants occupied Mitylene, a town in Lesbos, so that the poet signifies this with that specific verse:

->*When a numerous fleet destroys the cruel tyrants.*<-

As for us, whether or not these were proposed by the ancients for debate—

...disputes, with the names of the places added, we do not labor over them, even though Cornelius—as he himself says—did not write this, but rather Suetonius Tranquillus <note>Gaius Suetonius Tranquillus, a Roman historian best known for *The Twelve Caesars*</note> wrote it in the short book he composed *On Illustrious Grammarians and Rhetoricians* <note>original: *De grammaticis et rhetoribus*</note>. ->In another part, place only the country-plot of a sated lizard.<-

But now, to address the matter at hand, we flatly deny that "Cambre" is a valid inscription. Indeed, we boldly declare that no such thing is found among reliable writers; and even if it were found in abundance, it should not be referred to those tyrants who occupied Mitylene <note>the capital of the island of Lesbos</note>, since the town of Cambre is not only not in Lesbos, but is not even located on any island at all. Therefore—if I may say so with his permission <margin>With all due respect</margin>—if one examines it carefully, nothing more fabricated, discordant, awkward, or ridiculous than this opinion of Domitius can be said or imagined; it collapses of its own accord without any outside push. Furthermore, there was no reason why Alcaeus <note>a lyric poet from Lesbos</note> should flee Mitylene so that a "numerous fleet" might destroy the tyrants, since Cornelius, in that dialogue he published *On Famous Orators* <note>original: *Dialogus de oratoribus*; a work discussing the education and decline of rhetoric, traditionally attributed to Tacitus</note>, counts the rewards of tyrannicides <term>tyrannicides: the act of killing a tyrant, or those who carry out the killing</term> among the primary subjects of school controversies. Thus, he has obscured everything with such darkness...

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the critique of the scholar Domitius Calderinus, specifically regarding his tendency to use obscure language to avoid being caught in errors, and moves into a philological discussion of a proverb found in Juvenal's satires.</meta>

### MISCELLANIES, 45

<margin>The Attic philosopher.</margin> Domitius spreads clouds and darkness whenever he is unable to disentangle himself, like a little fish caught in a wicker trap. He does exactly what Atticus <note>Atticus was a Middle Platonist philosopher who lived in the 2nd century AD and was known for his fierce defenses of Plato against Aristotle.</note>, the Attic philosopher and a non-inelegant defender of Plato, teaches that Aristotle used to do. Just as the <margin>The nature of the cuttlefish.</margin> cuttlefish escapes by spraying its own ink into the very eyes and onto the hand of the fisherman, so too does Domitius, fearing to be caught, purposefully darken his opinions with obscurities. This description fits no man better than him; never more so than when he is almost cornered, he wraps himself up and clouds the issue with evasive maneuvers, vomiting forth a kind of ink of perplexity.

As for what a certain otherwise learned man—who has deserved well of literature through his industry—brought forward in a recently published commentary concerning "Gambra" and "Anathus," citing Probus <note>Marcus Valerius Probus, a famous Roman grammarian; however, the author here doubts the authenticity of the commentary being attributed to him.</note> as the interpreter of Juvenal: I must confess that I neither know who this Probus is, nor do I think he is the one mentioned by Aulus Gellius and other ancient writers. Therefore, let the credit for this source remain with that author. 

However, I will briefly set forth what seems right to me, for—as some tragic poet or other says—the speech of truth is simple. I believe Juvenal was expressing this <margin>A Greek proverb.</margin> Greek proverb: "Cabbage served twice is death" <note>original: "δὶς κράμβη θάνατος" (dis krambe thanatos)</note>. By this, he meant that <margin>Cabbage.</margin> cabbage served a second time brings death. Now, *crambe* is a type of cabbage; it was used here to denote the disgust one feels for a controversy or story repeated too often, since even in common speech, "reheated cabbage" has already passed into a proverb for something that causes weariness and distaste.

The poet [Juvenal] preferred to adopt the Greek term <note>original: "Græcum... uocabulum"</note> rather than the Latin one, either because that proverb is more common among the Greeks than the Latins, or surely because there is a certain species of cabbage <note>The author is referring to the proverb *Bis krambe thanatos*, meaning "Cabbage served twice is death," used to describe tiresome repetition.</note> properly called *Crambe*. According to Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, author of the *Natural History*, an encyclopedic work on the natural world.</note>, this plant has thinner and simpler leaves, is very dense, and although quite bitter, it is highly effective. Suidas <note>The *Suda*, a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the Mediterranean world, traditionally attributed to an author named Suidas.</note> mentions this Greek proverb and teaches that *Crambe* is even served at banquets because it counteracts drunkenness, just as Cato <note>Cato the Elder, in his work *On Agriculture* (*De Re Rustica*), famously praised cabbage as a medicinal marvel and a cure for the effects of wine.</note> testifies in his book on rural affairs.

### CERTAIN ERRORS IN A LETTER OF CICERO, AND CONCERNING THE WORD *SCIAMACHIA*.
### CHAPTER XXXIIII.

A word that is far too rustic, unrefined, and plainly foul has occupied a letter of Cicero—specifically the one to Brutus <note>Marcus Junius Brutus, the assassin of Caesar and a frequent correspondent of Cicero.</note> which begins with the phrase, "I am wonderfully glad, my Brute" <note>original: "Mirabiliter mi Brute lætor"</note>. For so we read in that letter: "And those vigorous contentions of mine seem as if they were annihilated by <term>scientia</term> <note>literally "knowledge" or "science"; the author argues this reading is a corruption of the Greek word *sciamachia*, meaning shadow-boxing or practice-fighting.</note>." Some readers have attempted to correct this by suggesting the text should say "as if they were *already* <note>original: "si iam"</note> annihilated." I beg the learned, and those skilled in the proper use of words and the reading of ancient texts, to consult their own...

For Cicero feels that he is now clearly losing influence in the senate, as Brutus’s affairs are not succeeding, and that those vigorous contentions of his seem to be shadow-fights. The word itself is extremely elegant among the Greeks and very commonly used. Indeed, Galen <note>A prominent Greek physician and philosopher in the Roman Empire.</note> the physician, in the second book of his *On Hygiene* <note>original: "ὑγιεινῶν" (Hygieinon)</note>, says the following: "It is time to move on to the rapid exercises that lack intensity and violence; these are running and shadow-fighting" <note>original: "μεταβαίνειν δὲ καιρὸς ἐπὶ τὰ ταχέα χωρὶς εὐτονίας καὶ βίας, δρόμοι δ' εἰσὶ ταῦτα καὶ σκιαμαχίαι."</note>. Among the swift exercises, which however are not strained or violent, he includes running and *sciamachias*. Finally, even Eustathius <note>A 12th-century archbishop and scholar known for his extensive commentaries on Homer.</note>, in his commentary on the third book of the *Odyssey*, mentions the word in this way: "Likewise, the phrase 'let us not fight under a shadow' <note>original: "μὴ ὑπὸ σκιᾷ μαχούμεθα"</note> indicates nothing bad; indeed, the compound 'shadow-fighting' <note>original: "σκιαμαχεῖν"</note> is well-formed." And Marcus Varro <note>A prolific Roman scholar and contemporary of Cicero.</note> entitled one of those Menippean satires of his *Sciamachia*. <margin>Organon.</margin> In the same works of Cicero, another word, which is *organon* <note>original Greek: ὄργανον; meaning a tool, instrument, or organ.</note>, having been corrupted from the Greek script, migrated into the Latin. For the senate was my *organon*," as it was the tool and the instrument that Cicero used.

<margin>A passage in Varro.</margin> In Marcus Varro’s first book on agriculture <note>Marcus Terentius Varro (116–27 BCE) was Rome’s greatest scholar. His *Res Rusticae* (On Agriculture) is a foundational text on Roman farming.</note>, that passage is read incorrectly: "The beard of the grain and the kernel are known to almost everyone, the husk to few. I know that this is written only in Ennius, in the verses of the *Ephemeris*." <note>The *Ephemeris* was long thought to be the name of a poem by the early Roman poet Ennius.</note> For regarding what even those manuscripts considered most corrected contain—"in the verses of the *Ephemeris*"—I find it written thus in a copy of venerable antiquity from the public library of the Medici family: "in the translated books of Euhemerus" <note>Euhemerus was a Greek mythographer who argued that the gods were originally mortal kings. OF MISCELLANIES. 47

There is no doubt that by Varro, Ennius’s *Sacred History* is meant, which was translated into Latin from Euhemerus <note>Euhemerus (c. 300 BCE) was a Greek mythographer who argued that the gods were originally great kings or heroes who were deified after death—a theory now called "Euhemerism."</note>, and which, as is evident, Lactantius <margin>Euhemerus</margin> frequently cites, as do Eusebius and Augustine. <margin>And his books.</margin> The words of Lactantius from the first book of the *Divine Institutes* are as follows: "The ancient author Euhemerus, who was from the city of Messina, collected the deeds of Jupiter and the others who are thought to be gods, and wove together a history from the titles and sacred inscriptions found in the most ancient temples, and especially in the shrine of Jupiter Triphylius, where an inscription indicated that a golden column had been placed by Jupiter himself. On this column he wrote out his deeds, so that they might be monuments for posterity regarding his affairs. Ennius translated and followed this history, and these are his words: 'Where Jupiter gives the command of the sea to Neptune, so that he might reign over all islands and all places along the sea.'" Then the same Lactantius brings forward several passages from Ennius concerning this same history time and again, which I, for my part, will bypass for the sake of brevity. Furthermore, Sextus Empiricus <note>A skeptical philosopher (c. 160–210 CE) who cataloged the arguments of different schools of thought.</note> also cites the words of Euhemerus himself, especially in the fourth volume of the *Pyrrhonist Sketches*, and says he was called an 'Atheist' <margin>The Atheist.</margin> <note>original Latin: Atheon; from the Greek meaning "without gods."</note>, just like Diagoras of Melos, Prodicus of Ceos, Theodoras of Cyrene, and several others. And regarding him, Timon of Phlius <note>A satirical philosopher known for his "Silloi," or lampoons, which mocked other philosophers.</note> (as I believe) says this:

"Old Euhemerus, a boastful charlatan, scribbling down impious books." <note>original Greek: Εὐήμερος γέρων ἀλαζὼν ἄδικα βιβλία ψύχων.</note>

The author cites Church Fathers and skeptical philosophers to confirm the identity and controversial "atheistic" reputation of Euhemerus.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the philological investigation into the corruption of the name "Euhemerus" in classical manuscripts. BOOK [OF MISCELLANIES]

But leaving those matters aside, we must look even more closely at a passage in Columella <note>Lucius Junius Moderatus Columella (4–70 CE) was the most important writer on agriculture in the Roman Empire.</note> as well, specifically in book ten, chapter three, where he treats the subject of bees. <margin>A passage in Columella.</margin> In that passage, "Homer" is cited twice instead of "Euhemerus" in the common manuscripts. This is confirmed by a very ancient book from the private library of the Medici family, written in Lombardic script <note>A style of medieval handwriting (often called Beneventan or Germanic script today) used in Italy between the 7th and 13th centuries.</note>. It is also confirmed by another manuscript from the public library of the same family, which the learned and diligent Niccolò Niccoli <margin>Niccolò Niccoli</margin> <note>Niccolò Niccoli (1364–1437) was a key figure in the Renaissance, whose massive personal collection of ancient manuscripts became the foundation of the Laurentian Library in Florence.</note> transcribed by his own hand from an ancient exemplar. 

Furthermore, reason itself supports this correction—and no barriers can withstand reason—since no such fable about bees is found anywhere in Homer. It is much more likely to have been written by Euhemerus instead, as he composed a *History of Jupiter*, and those bees are said to have been the nurses of Jupiter <note>According to myth, when Jupiter was a baby hiding from his father Saturn, bees provided him with honey in the Dictaean cave.</note>. 

Moreover, as I believe, the poet Callimachus <note>A famous scholar and librarian at Alexandria (c. 310–240 BCE).</note> had found it written in the works of this same Euhemerus (whom we discussed above) in that same *Sacred History* that Jupiter finished his life in Crete <margin>The tomb of Jupiter.</margin> and that his tomb is located there in the town of Knossos. On that tomb, it was inscribed in ancient Greek letters <note>original: "Ζεὺς κρόνου"</note> "Zeus [son] of Cronus," which in Latin is "Jupiter [son] of Saturn." I say that this most learned poet both knew of Euhemerus’s writing and saw that this was held as a common belief by the Cretans. For this reason, I believe, he testified in his very *Hymn to Jupiter*: "The Cretans," he says, "are always liars, for they have even built a tomb for you, O King, though you never died, but exist forever." These are the verses: <note>original: "Κρῆτες ἀεὶ ψεῦσται, καὶ γὰρ τάφον ὦ ἄνα σεῖο"</note> "Cretans are always liars; for they even built a tomb for you, O King..."

<summary>Poliziano corrects a common error in Columella's manuscripts where "Homer" was mistakenly written instead of "Euhemerus." He uses ancient Medici manuscripts to prove that the story of Jupiter's bees and his tomb in Crete originated with Euhemerus, not Homer.</summary>

<keywords>Columella, Euhemerus, Callimachus, Knossos, Medici Library, Niccolò Niccoli, Zeus, Cretans are liars, philology, textual criticism</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the argument that the Greek poet Callimachus criticized the Cretans for claiming to possess the tomb of Jupiter. The author is proving that the reputation of Cretans as liars was a common theme among ancient authors, linking the "sacred history" of Euhemerus—who claimed the gods were merely mortal kings—to this tradition of Cretan mendacity.</meta>

->MISCELLANIES. 48<-

"[The Cretans] built [a tomb for you]. But you did not die, for you exist forever." <note>original: "Κρῆτες ἐτεκτήναντο. σὺ δ' οὐ θάνες, ἐσσὶ γὰρ αἰεί." This is a quote from Callimachus’s *Hymn to Zeus*, mocking the Cretans for claiming the immortal king of the gods was buried on their island.</note>

And finally, because of this very thing, I believe Statius <note>Publius Papinius Statius (c. 45–96 CE) was a Roman poet, best known for his epic the *Thebaid*.</note> also says in the first book of the *Thebaid*: "And Crete, having lied about your ghost."

Cyprian the martyr <margin>Cyprian</margin> <note>Saint Cyprian (c. 210–258 CE) was the Bishop of Carthage and an important early Christian writer.</note>, a most eloquent man, says in his book *On the Origin and Kinds of Idols*: "The Cave of Jupiter is visited <margin>passage.</margin> in Crete, and his tomb is shown," although these words are found written with little accuracy in newer manuscripts <margin><unclear>manuscript quality</unclear></margin>. <margin><unclear>textual corruption</unclear></margin> Furthermore, the Cretans were always held to be such liars that by the Greek proverb, <margin>proverb.</margin> which is "to play the Cretan against a Cretan" <note>original: "πρὸς κρῆτα κρητίζειν" (pros krēta krētizein). To "Cretanize" meant to use deception or to out-lie a known liar.</note>, nothing else is meant but to use a lie against a liar. 

But also, this verse of that Cretan Epimenides <note>Epimenides of Knossos (6th century BCE) was a semi-mythical seer and philosopher. This specific line is famous because it creates the "Liar's Paradox": if a Cretan says all Cretans are liars, is he telling the truth?</note> was cited by Paul the Apostle and, because of that, was even sanctified:

"Cretans are always liars, evil beasts, idle bellies." <note>original: "Κρῆτες ἀεὶ ψεῦσται, κακὰ θηρία, γαστέρες ἀργοί." This is quoted in the New Testament, Titus 1:12.</note>

It means: "Cretans are always liars, wicked beasts, and lazy bellies." For Callimachus took the first half-line of the earlier verse from Epimenides. Therefore, Ovid <note>Publius Ovidius Naso (43 BCE – 17 CE), the famous Roman poet of the *Metamorphoses*.</note> also elegantly put this in the *Amores*:

"The Cretans shall be witnesses, and yet the Cretans do not invent everything." And elsewhere:

"I speak of well-known things; not even Crete, which supports a hundred cities, although it be a liar, is accustomed to deny this."

->HERE IS SOLVED AND<-
untangled the riddle from Varro’s book
which Gellius cites.

### CHAPTER XXXVI.

<margin>A passage from Aulus Gellius.</margin> That riddle from Varro, which Gellius <note>Aulus Gellius (c. 125 – after 180 CE) was a Roman author known for his *Attic Nights*, a collection of notes on grammar, philosophy, and history.</note> proposes and leaves unexplained so as to sharpen the conjectures of his readers, I truly profess that I shall solve, even at my own risk. It is presented in three iambic senarii <note>A common meter in Latin comedy and riddles consisting of six iambic feet.</note>:

"I do not quite know whether it be *once minus* [semel minus], or *bis minus* [twice minus], or both of these, as I once heard it said; it refused to yield even to King Jupiter himself." <note>original: "Semel minus ne, an bis minus sit, non sat scio, An utrunq; horum, ut quondam audiui dicier, Ioui ipsi regi noluit concedere."</note>

It seems to signify **Terminus**, <margin>The god Terminus.</margin> the god who refused to yield to Jupiter when the Capitol was being deconsecrated <note>The "exauguratio" was a ritual to clear a site of its previous religious dedications to make room for a new temple.</note>. Ovid writes in the second book of the *Fasti*:

"What happened when the new Capitol was being built? Truly, the whole crowd of gods yielded to Jupiter and gave him place. Terminus, as the ancients recall, being found within the shrine, stayed behind and holds the temple along with great Jupiter." <note>original: "Quid noua cum fierent capitolia? nempe deorum / Cuncta Ioui cessit turba, locumq; dedit. / Terminus, ut ueteres memorant, inuentus in æde / Restitit, & magno cum Ioue templa tenet."</note>

Therefore, the author of this riddle <note>The original uses "scirpus," a Latin term for a complex riddle or "bulrush" (something knotted).</note> expresses doubt whether it was "once minus" [semel minus] or "twice minus" [bis minus], or both of these; however, from both—that is, from once and twice—the result is "thrice" [ter]. Wherefore it was not *once minus* or *twice minus*, but **Terminus** <note>The solution is a linguistic pun: "Semel" (1) + "Bis" (2) equals "Ter" (3). When "Ter" is combined with "minus," it forms the name of the god Terminus.</note> who refused to yield even to King Jupiter himself.

<summary>The author solves a classical riddle from Varro regarding the god Terminus, explaining that the name is a pun on the Latin words for 'thrice' (ter) and 'minus.'</summary>
<keywords>Varro, Aulus Gellius, Terminus, Jupiter, Roman mythology, riddles, Ovid, Fasti, etymology, wordplay</keywords>

### MISCELLANIES. 49

## SERVIUS’S FOUL ERROR <margin>Servius’s error.</margin>
## regarding the river Oaxes and Crete.
### CHAPTER XXXVII.

**E**t we shall come to the rapid Oaxes of Crete. <note>original: "Et rapidum Cretae ueniemus Oaxem." This is a line from Virgil’s *Eclogues* (1.65), where an exiled shepherd laments the far-flung places he might be sent.</note> This line from the first Eclogue is explained by Servius <note>Servius (late 4th century CE) was a grammarian whose commentary on Virgil became a standard textbook in the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</note> word-for-word in this way: "Rapid Oaxes of Crete," he says, means "muddy," because it "seizes the clay" <note>original: "rapit cretam." Servius is making a pun: the river is "of Crete" (*Cretae*) because it carries "clay" (*creta*).</note>. *Creta* is the name for a white earth. For the Oaxes <margin>The Oaxes.</margin> is a river of Mesopotamia which, by its own speed, carries away white earth and is thus made turbulent. So says Servius.

But I would rather believe that the Oaxes is intended to be a river on the island of Crete itself. For Vibius Sequester <note>A late antique author who compiled a geographical dictionary of names found in Latin poetry.</note> also says that the Oaxes is a river of Crete, from which the city of Oaxia is named. Furthermore, Apollonius called the island of Crete "Oaxian land" in the first book of the *Argonautica* <note>An epic poem about Jason and the Argonauts written by Apollonius of Rhodes in the 3rd century BCE.</note> with these words:

"The Idaean Dactyls of Crete, whom once the nymph Anchiale, within the Dictaean cave, brought forth as she grasped the Oaxian earth with both hands." <note>original: "δάκτυλοι ἰδαῖοῖ κρηταέες οὕς ποτε νύμφη / ἀγχιάλη δικταῖον ἀνὰ σπέος ἀμφοτέρῃσι / δραξαμένη γαίης οἰαξίδος ἐβλάστησε." The Idaean Dactyls were mythical beings credited with the discovery of iron and the rhythm of music.</note>

Moreover, Varro <note>Referring here to Varro Atacinus, a Roman poet who translated Apollonius’s work into Latin.</note> translated this last passage in the following manner...

<summary>The author critiques the commentator Servius for misidentifying Virgil's river Oaxes as a Mesopotamian river based on a pun, arguing instead that it is a native river of the island of Crete.</summary>

CHAPTER XXXVIII.

<margin>A passage of Juvenal.</margin>

**W**ho makes the Lacedaemonian circle slippery with a *pitysma*. <note>original: "Qui Lacedæmonium pityſmate lubricat orbẽ." This line comes from Juvenal’s *Satires* (11.175), describing the behavior of wealthy Roman elites during dinner.</note> This verse from the eleventh Satire of Juvenal has been explained in various ways until now, and (as I believe) carelessly. For Domitius <note>Domitius Calderinus (c. 1446–1478) was a famous Italian humanist scholar. His commentaries were widely read but frequently attacked by his rivals for perceived errors.</note> says that a *pitysma* <term>pitysma: a Greek-derived word (πτύσμα) referring to the act of spitting out a small amount of wine, often after tasting or during a drinking bout</term> is a spraying from a light spit, derived from the verb *pityo* <note>likely referring to the Greek *pytizo*, to spit or sputter.</note>. He takes the "Lacedaemonian circle" <note>Lacedaemonian is another term for Spartan.</note> to mean a ring, because, he says, the use of rings originated with the Lacedaemonians, so that senators and knights might be signified by the mention of a ring. Furthermore, another most learned man (whom we refrain from naming out of respect, even where we disagree with him) thinks it is a Lacedaemonian dance, yet he frankly admits he has read nothing of it so far, except only in Pollux <note>Julius Pollux was a 2nd-century Greek grammarian; his *Onomasticon* is a dictionary of Greek terms and customs.</note>, where mention is made of a Lacedaemonian dance. Finally, a third man, also himself most learned, understands it as a hoop <term>trochus: a metal or wooden hoop used as a toy, often rolled along the ground with a rod</term> made from a pine tree, because a pine is called a *pitys* <note>original: "pitys," the Greek word for a pine tree or the nymph associated with it.</note>, from which comes *pitysma*. Of these three opinions, with all due respect to the authors, we approve of none. First, we deny that the Lacedaemonian circle can signify a ring, since it is nowhere read that the origin of the ring flowed from the Lace [catchword: daemonians]

among the Lacedaemonians <note>Lacedaemonian is another name for Spartan.</note>. However, Pliny—or rather the source Domitius cites—reports that even then in Sparta only an iron ring was used. By *pitylisma* <term>pitylisma: a Greek-derived term referring to a specific rhythmic movement or exercise, often involving the arms</term> we understand a type of exercise, about which Galen, the excellent physician, writes in the second book of *On Hygiene* <note>original: ὑγιεινῶν (*Hygieinon*), Galen's foundational text on health and exercise</note>. However, *pitylisma* is correctly formed from it, following a very common grammatical proportion. Nor is it inappropriate to write out Galen’s Greek words first, and then interpret them according to our understanding. "It is," he says, "pitylizing—or if you prefer, *pitylissare*—when someone walking on the tips of their feet extends their hands and moves them very rapidly, namely one backward and the other forward. They practice this type of exercise primarily against walls so that if they slip anywhere, they may quickly recover by grabbing the wall. Thus, they hide the slips of those exercising, and the exercise itself becomes <margin>Lacedaemonian circle.</margin> weaker." Furthermore, I would take the "Lacedaemonian circle" to mean the *Laconicum* itself, as it was in that place that they were accustomed to exercise in such a manner. He then says it was called "Laconicum" because the Lacedaemonians at that time practiced their exercises naked and anointed with oil more than all others. Martial also hints at this *Laconicum* as being so immense...

<meta>Continuity: Politian continues his analysis of the "Lacedaemonian circle" (the Laconicum) by citing Roman authorities like Columella, Seneca, and Vitruvius to explain both the social context of these baths and their architectural form.</meta>

MISCELLANIES. 51

If the Spartan rituals please you,
You can be content with dry vapor,
To be immersed in raw virginity and Martian spirit.
<note>original: "Ritus si placeant tibi Laconum..." Politian quotes the poet Martial (Epigrams 6.42), who contrasts the modest, dry heat of the Spartan-style bath (*Laconicum*) with more decadent Roman luxuries. "Raw virginity" likely refers to the cold waters of the Aqua Virgo aqueduct.</note>

But Columella also writes in the first book of *On Agriculture* as follows:
"And we gaze in awe at the gestures of effeminate men, who counterfeit by womanly movement a sex denied to men by nature, and deceive the eyes of those watching. Soon afterward, so that we might arrive fit for the taverns, we boil away our daily indigestion in the Spartan baths, and seek out a thirst by burning off our sweat."
<note>original: "Attonitiq; miramur gestus effœminatorum..." Columella (De Re Rustica 1.pref.15) complains that his contemporaries used the intense heat of the Spartan-style sweat baths merely to settle their stomachs after overeating so they could begin drinking again.</note>

Now, therefore, it can be clear to anyone from these words of Columella whom the poet <note>Referring to Juvenal, whom Politian is commenting upon.</note> especially means; namely, those without a doubt who would spend the whole day in those Spartan baths and gymnasiums, anointed and naked. For this reason he also says "slippery," on account of the oil and ointment with which they were rubbed down and dried. Seneca also, in the seventh book of his letters to Lucilius, suggests nothing other than these Spartan rooms, relating it thus: "What have I to do with those hot pools? What with those sweating-rooms, in which a dry vapor is enclosed to exhaust the body, so that all sweat might be forced out through effort?" <note>original: "Quid mihi cum istis calentibus stagnis..." Seneca (Epistle 67.6) views the artificial heat of the bath as a poor substitute for the sweat earned through honest physical labor.</note>

It remains now, since he mentioned a "circle," that we also demonstrate that the Spartan rooms were constructed in a circular shape. This is evident from the fifth book of *Architecture* by Vitruvius, in these words: "The Spartan sweat-rooms <term>Laconicum: a circular sweating room with dry heat, similar to a modern sauna</term>, which are to be joined to the warm-room <term>tepidarium: the moderately warm room in a Roman bath complex</term>, should have a height up to the lowest curve of the hemisphere equal to their width; and there should be a central opening for light in the hemisphere..."

->*concerning the daughters of Cadmus, Melon, Cuttlefish ink, and*<-

### CHAPTER XXXIX.

**S**cirpus—which is the word for a riddle—that the ingenious and quite learned poet Ausonius proposed to the Grammarian Theon in acatalectic iambic dimeters, we shall attempt to explain in this way.
<note>The term *scirpus* literally means a bulrush or reed. Because reeds are tangled and difficult to unknot, the word became a common Latin metaphor for an enigma or puzzle.</note>
But first, let us write down the little verses themselves.
<margin>From Ausonius</margin>
"Or let your interpreter of enigmas be present,
He who was the knower of mine,
When he revealed to you the little black daughters of Cadmus,
The white page of Melon,
The marks of dark cuttlefish ink,
And the Cnidian knots."
<note>original: "Aut adfit interpres tuus Aenigmatum..." These lines are from Ausonius’s *Epistles* (Epist. 14.73–76). The "daughters of Cadmus" are a classic poetic metaphor for the letters of the alphabet, as Cadmus was the mythical figure credited with bringing writing from Phoenicia to Greece.</note>

Of these lines, however, the last verse is read erroneously in the more common books, having "measures" <note>original: *modos*</note> or "mine" <note>original: *meos*</note> instead of "knots" <note>original: *nodos*</note> which we have restored. For I find it thus not only in several others, but also in a book written by the hand of Giovanni Boccaccio, which is now in the library of the Holy Spirit <note>The library of the Augustinian convent of Santo Spirito in Florence, where Boccaccio bequeathed his personal library.</note>...
