is preserved in the Florentine manuscript. Furthermore, neither the metrical structure allows for either the words "measures" <note>original: "modos"</note> or "my" <note>original: "meos"</note>. Since in that second position, the foot ought to be an iamb <note>a metrical foot consisting of a short syllable followed by a long one</note> and not a pyrrhic <note>a metrical foot of two short syllables</note>. Therefore, it seems to me that Ausonius has enfolded within these veils the letters—the gift of Cadmus the Phoenician—and the Nilotic papyrus, the scribe’s ink, and the writer’s pen, which is the true instrument of a man of letters. For Cadmus first brought letters into Greece from Phoenicia. <margin>Cadmus brought letters into Greece.</margin> Herodotus testifies to this in his book *Terpsichore* <note>This is the traditional name for Book 5 of Herodotus's *Histories*.</note>, saying that those Phoenicians who came into Greece under the leadership of Cadmus first showed their native and ancestral letters—that is, Phoenician letters—to the Greeks; but with the passage of time, he says, the marks were gradually changed along with the language. And the Ionians were the first to use them. For which reason they were also called *phoenikeia* <note>original: "φοινίκηα" — meaning "Phoenician things" or "Phoenician letters".</note>. Herodotus also writes that he himself saw Cadmean letters engraved upon certain tripods in the temple of Ismenian Apollo at Thebes in Boeotia, from which he cites three epigrams; these letters were, as he says himself, very similar to Ionic letters. Therefore, if the ancient Greek letter-forms were none other than those of the Phoenicians, and the Latin ones were none other than those of the Greeks (as Pliny also indicates, having himself cited the Delphic letters <note>Politian is referring to Pliny the Elder’s *Natural History*, where Pliny argues the ancient Greek alphabet was nearly identical to the Latin one.</note>—for that word "table" <note>original: "tabula"</note> is a recent addition and inserted by the unlearned), it is manifest that even Latin letters are none other than those we learned from Cadmus. He calls the Phoenician signs of Cadmus the "marks of letters."
I pass over the fact that Suidas—or rather Zopyrion—says the letters were called "Phoenician." I also pass over Pliny and many others who say they were brought into Greece by Cadmus. For since various people remember what they have read here and there in Herodotus, I seem to have done enough by restoring these facts to his authority. Indeed, the testimonies of the ancients are not so much to be counted, in my view, as they are to be weighed for their value.

Furthermore, he speaks of "Melo's white page" because the Nile produces the papyrus from which white pages are made.
<margin>Melo, who is also the Nile. A passage in Pompeius <note>Likely referring to Sextus Pompeius Festus, a 2nd-century Roman grammarian.</note>.</margin>
Indeed, the Nile was called "Melo" by the ancient Latins who were not yet accustomed to the Greek language, just as Pompeius declares under the entry for the word *alumento*. Although many manuscripts have "milo" instead of "Melo," this is an ignorant and faulty error. Therefore, we shall support this wavering authority (if it so pleases the reader) with the help of Servius <note>A famous 4th-century commentator on Virgil.</note>, whose words in the first book of the *Aeneid* are as follows:
<margin>Atlas.</margin>
"Certainly *Atlas* is a Greek name, just like *Nilus* [the Nile]. For Ennius <note>An early Roman poet, often called the father of Roman poetry.</note> says that the Nile is called 'Melo,' while Atlas is called..."

...Telamon. Thus he says. Finally, in place of ink smeared into the shapes of letters, we also find "sepia" <note>original: "sepiam" — the ink of the cuttlefish, used as a writing fluid in antiquity.</note> mentioned in Persius. <margin>Sepia.</margin>
"Because the black sepia vanishes when water is poured in." <note>original: "Nigra quod infusa uanescat sepia lympha" — Persius, *Satires*, 3.13. The poet is describing a student making excuses for not studying, claiming his ink is too thick or too diluted.</note>
Clearly this is due to the nature of that fish, which is said to pour out a dark liquid, from which ink was perhaps made in former times. Regarding this, Martianus <note>Martianus Capella, a 5th-century author of an influential encyclopedia on the liberal arts.</note> says: "Then, having applied a little black powder—which was thought to be made from embers or sepia—it was healed through small tubes." Furthermore, *furvum* <note>An archaic Latin word for dark or black.</note> means black, from which Varro <note>Marcus Terentius Varro, a Roman scholar and grammarian.</note> thinks the word *furem* <note>Thief; Varro suggests a thief is so named because they operate in the "furvum" or dark.</note> is derived. But Horace <margin>Dark/Black.</margin> also says of this: "The dark realms of Proserpina." <note>original: "Furuae regna Proserpinae" — Horace, *Odes*, 2.13.21, referring to the underworld.</note> And Valerius Maximus mentions "dark victims for Father Dis," <note>Dis Pater was the Roman god of the underworld; dark animals were traditionally sacrificed to him.</note> though even before him, Varro interpreted these "dark" things as black. Next, understand "Cnidian knots," <margin>Cnidian knots.</margin> since the most esteemed reeds <note>The *calamus*, or reed pen, used for writing on papyrus.</note> come from Cnidus, as Pliny says in the sixteenth book of his *Natural History*. Moreover, the author affected an imitation of Persius when he spoke of "knots," since that poet had written:
"And into the hands come the paper and the knotty reed." <note>original: "Inq; manus chartae nodosaq; uenit harundo" — Persius, *Satires*, 3.11.</note>

### THUMBS are accustomed to be pressed when showing favor, just as they are turned when denying favor; and from this the opinions of Horace, Juvenal, and Prudentius are explained.

### CHAPTER XLII.

**H**orace <note>Quintus Horatius Flaccus (65–8 BC), a leading Roman lyric poet.</note> says in the first book of his *Epistles*: <margin>Horace's passage.</margin>
"Whoever believes that you agree with his pursuits,
As a supporter, he will praise your play with both thumbs." <note>original: "utroque tuum laudabit pollice ludum."</note>

Porphyrion <note>Pomponius Porphyrion, a 3rd-century AD scholar and commentator on Horace.</note> says "with both thumbs" means "with both hands," so that it is a trope of synecdoche <term>synecdoche|a figure of speech in which a part is made to represent the whole, or vice versa</term>, where a part stands for the whole. But lest the authority of an ancient writer perhaps mislead anyone, let us also gently tweak his ear. For it is written in Pliny's *Natural History*, Book 28, in these very words: "When we show favor, we are even commanded by a proverb to press our thumbs." <note>original: "Pollices cum faueamus, premere etiam prouerbio iubemur." This suggests that "pressing the thumb" (likely against the index finger) was the sign of approval, rather than the modern "thumbs up."</note> From this, Juvenal <note>Decimus Iunius Iuvenalis, a Roman satirist of the late 1st and early 2nd centuries AD.</note> most skillfully wrote that famous line:
"And with the crowd's thumb turned, they kill whomever the people..." <note>original: "Et uerso pollice uulgi, Quemlibet occidunt populo"; this refers to the "pollice verso" gesture in the gladiatorial arena where the crowd signaled for the death of a defeated fighter.</note>

<summary>Politian corrects a common misunderstanding of a Horace passage by citing Pliny the Elder, explaining that the Romans "pressed" their thumbs to show favor and "turned" them to demand death in the arena.</summary>

### HOW MANY DIPHTHONGS
### the ancients possessed.

### CHAPTER XLIII.

<margin>Ancient diphthongs.</margin> **D**iphthongs <term>diphthong|a sound formed by the combination of two vowels in a single syllable</term> among the Latins are said to be no more than four, although we find at least seven of them in ancient marbles or coins, of which Lorenzo de' Medici <note>Lorenzo "the Magnificent" (1449–1492), the Florentine statesman and Poliziano's patron, who amassed a vast collection of antiquities.</note> provides us with a huge supply. For besides those most common ones, which are *ae*, *oe*, *au*, and *eu*, one can also find *ai*, and *ei*, and likewise *ou*, in place of those *æ*, *i*, and *u*, in ancient monuments. And indeed, some always pronounced *ai* instead of *æ* with the letter *i*, not *e*, after the Greek fashion, as Quintilian <note>Marcus Fabius Quintilianus (c. 35–100 AD), a celebrated Roman rhetorician.</note> shows. Some did so only in a singular manner, when in the genitive...

->THAT ONE SHOULD READ "PEGASEAN NECTAR"<-
->in Persius, not "melody."<-

->CHAPTER XLIIII.<-

<margin>Persius’s passage.</margin> Persius, in the epigram that serves as his prologue, says:
"You might believe that crow-poets and magpie-poetesses
Sing the Pegasean melody." <note>original: "Pegaseium melos"; referring to the Muses’ spring, Hippocrene, created by the hoof of the winged horse Pegasus.</note>
In an extremely ancient commentary, written in the script called Lombardic <term>Langobardas|Lombardic script; a medieval script used in Italy from the 7th to 11th centuries, often difficult for later humanists to read</term>, which we have also publicly displayed to our students, we found the following word-for-word: "Pegasean nectar, in others melody." <note>original: "Pegaseum nectar, in alijs melos"</note> From this, we believe the old and pure reading was *nectar*, while the recent and faulty one was *melody*. Furthermore, Pomponius Laetus <note>Pomponio Leto (1428–1498), a leading Italian humanist and founder of the Roman Academy.</note>, the leader of the Roman Academy and a most diligent scholar of antiquity, affirmed to many listeners that he possessed an ancient manuscript of Persius that supports our reading. Reason itself also proves this from the meter of the poem. For since the final foot of a choliamb <term>choliambic|also known as a 'scazon' or limping iambic; a meter where the final iamb is replaced by a long syllable, creating a 'thudding' or limping finish</term> must be either a spondee or a trochee, but "melody" <note>original: "melos"</note> can never be so—neither among the Greeks nor among our own poets—unless the first syllable is taken as short, it would surely be an error in the verse if you admitted "melody." For 

<margin>Hephaestion, authority on meters.</margin> as for what some claim, that the letter 'L' <note>original: "λ" (lambda)</note> has the power to lengthen a syllable among the Greeks—a point which that singular authority on meter, Hephaestion <note>Hephaestion of Alexandria (2nd century AD), whose manual on Greek meters became the standard reference for scholars.</note>, is entirely silent upon—I indeed find that...

<summary>Poliziano argues that a common passage in the poet Persius is corrupted, asserting that "nectar" is the correct reading over "melos" based on ancient manuscripts and the rules of Latin meter.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Poliziano’s technical argument regarding the meter of Persius’s prologue. He is refuting the idea that the word "melos" (melody) could have a long first syllable through poetic license, which would be necessary for it to fit the poem's "limping" meter.</meta>

MISCELLANIES. 57

I know [this is claimed], but only in certain obscure and ignoble papers; they bring an example from Homer, in whose works we read "spoils" <note>original: "heloria" / ἑλώρια; Poliziano is discussing a specific line in Homer where a syllable is lengthened for the sake of the meter.</note> with the first syllable lengthened. But how frivolous, how slight, and how trifling that argument is, is most clearly declared by this fact: it is now accepted by the usage and authority of every most learned scholar that this word in Homer is also written with a double "l" <note>original: "λ duplici"; doubling the 'l' (lambda) would naturally make the preceding syllable long in Greek prosody.</note>. <margin>heloria in the first book of the Iliad.</margin> Furthermore, even if we were to grant most fully that this occasionally happens among the Greeks, it is not immediately to be believed that Persius would use such a license in a single Greek term—one which the Greeks themselves are scarcely found to use even once in such a vast forest of writers—especially since, as far as I have observed, "melody" <note>original: "melos"</note> is never found with anything but a short first syllable, whether among the Greeks or the Latins.

For the ancient Latins avoided this license (which was granted only to the Greeks) as much as possible, as the poet Martial declares when he torments himself so much over the name "Earinus" <note>A name meaning "spring-like." Martial (Epigrams 9.11) complained that the name was impossible to fit into Latin hendecasyllabic verse because it began with a short syllable followed by two more shorts.</note>. He does not even permit himself to say "spring-like" <note>original: "eiarinon" / εἰαρινὸν</note> instead of "Earinus"—a license common among the Greeks, that is, the practice of inserting a letter, as in Homer: "on the flowers of spring" <note>original: "ep’ anthesin eiarinoisin" / ἐπ’ ἄνθεσιν εἰαρινοῖσιν</note>. Indeed, listen to Martial himself:

->Poets say "spring-like" nonetheless,<-
->But they are Greeks, to whom nothing is denied,<-
->And who are allowed to sound "Ares, Ares,"<- <margin>"Ares, Ares" in the fifth book of the Iliad.</margin> <note>original: "Ἆρες ἄρες"; in this Homeric line, the name of the god Ares is used twice: the first time the 'A' is long, and the second time it is short. Martial uses this to illustrate the rhythmic freedom Greeks enjoyed but Romans did not.</note>
->But we are not permitted to be so eloquent.<-

<summary>Poliziano argues that the word "melos" cannot fit the meter of Persius's poetry because its first syllable is always short, unlike Greek poetry which allows for flexible syllable lengths.</summary>

# THAT ACHILLES WAS YOUNGER THAN PATROCLUS,

## CHAPTER XLV.

A long-disseminated and now generally accepted opinion suggests that Patroclus was younger than Achilles and was loved by him in much the same way as Hylas <note>In mythology, Hylas was the young, beautiful companion and squire of Hercules.</note> was by Hercules. Martial is believed to imply this very thing when he writes:

->The smooth friend was closer to the descendant of Aeacus.<-
<note>original: "Aeacidae propior levis amicus erat." (Martial, Epigrams 6.32). "Aeacides" refers to Achilles, the grandson of Aeacus. The term "levis" (smooth/beardless) was often used to describe the younger partner in a relationship.</note>

For this reason, a certain most wicked verse from the *Hermaphroditus* <note>The *Hermaphroditus* was a notorious collection of erotic Neo-Latin poems by Antonio Beccadelli, which Poliziano labels "wicked" for its scandalous content despite its popularity among contemporary scholars.</note> books is also widely applauded. *Patroclus follows, even then bound by a great love,*
*and is drawn along as a rival in such great deeds.*
*Equal in studies and the measures of his age, but far off in strength,*

"And yet, he was destined to see Troy <note>original: "Pergama," the citadel of Troy.</note> with an equal fate." 

Plato, however, in his *Symposium*, argues a far different case. For he declares both that Achilles was much younger, <margin>Achilles was younger than Patroclus.</margin> and that Achilles himself was the one loved by Patroclus, seeing as he was still beardless and was more beautiful not only than Patroclus himself, but than all the other heroes as well. Indeed, for this reason, Plato says the gods bestowed extraordinary honors upon him, sending him to the Isles of the Blessed <note>In Greek mythology, the Elysian Fields or Isles of the Blessed were the final resting place for the souls of heroes and the virtuous.</note> because he valued his lover so highly. He chose not only to die for him, but even to die upon him, rather than grow old in his own fatherland. 

Furthermore, Plato rebukes Aeschylus as a trifler <note>original: "nugacem"</note> because Aeschylus also portrayed Achilles as the lover of Patroclus. Plato cites Homer as a witness to the age of both; if anyone demands to see those words for themselves (for Plato did not actually quote them here), let him read in the eleventh book <note>original: "Rhapsodia," referring to a book of the epic.</note> of the *Iliad*, in the speech of Nestor, the instructions with which the father Menoetius sent his son Patroclus off to war.

->is four syllables, not three. Also, the reading should be "stored in a Lydian box," not "unstored." Likewise, a passage in Martianus Capella is explained, quite carefully, I believe.<-

<meta>This page continues Chapter 46, which began on the previous page. Having just introduced the topic of textual corrections in the works of Juvenal and Martianus Capella, the author now examines a specific corrupted term in Juvenal’s Seventh Satire and a character named Lyde.</meta>

BOOK

"The itch for writing remains, and it grows old in a sick heart." <note>original: "Scribendi cacethos, & ægro in corde senescit." A famous line from Juvenal’s *Satire 7*, describing the incurable compulsion to write.</note> 

However, the word written as *cacethos* is used neither by the Greeks anywhere, nor by the Latins; nor does the meter of the poem <margin>*Cacoethes*.</margin> allow it. Therefore, we suspect it should be read as *cacoethes*, so that it is the same as the Greek term *to kakoethes*. <note>original: "τὸ κακόηθες," meaning a malignant habit, an evil disposition, or in medical terms, a worsening disease.</note> For by that very frequent and common word, a "bad habit" is signified. We found this same reading in an ancient manuscript written in Lombardic script <note>A medieval style of handwriting used in Italy between the 7th and 13th centuries, which Renaissance scholars like the author sought out for its age and accuracy.</note>, which Francesco Gaddi of Florence gave me the opportunity to read. He is a secretary to the high magistrate, a prudent and refined man, and well-versed in literature. Furthermore, that verse appears in the same manuscript as follows: 

Juvenal says: "Neither does the swollen Lyde, stored in a box, profit." <note>original: "Turgida nec prodest condita pyxide lyde." This refers to *Satire 2*. The author is correcting the word order and the inclusion of the word "swollen" (turgida).</note> 
Elsewhere, this is wrongly written as: "Lyde stored in a box." <margin>Lyde.</margin> 
<margin>Vessel-like.</margin> You are to understand "Lyde" as an old woman selling medications of that sort—someone fat and, as Plautus says, "vessel-like." <note>original: "doliarem," comparing the woman's shape to a *dolium*, a large earthenware jar or vat used for storing wine.</note> 

Perhaps Martianus Capella had this in mind when, playing quite wittily upon the image of the drunken Silenus <note>In mythology, Silenus is the tutor and companion of the wine-god Bacchus, usually depicted as a jovial, balding drunkard riding a donkey.</note>, he said: "And while he desires to move his step, with Lyde calling him away." For if you hear names like Canthara, Pyrrhia, or Baucis, you would immediately imagine from your reading certain women who are stuffed fat, wine-soaked, and shriveled. Thus, having heard the name of Lyde, you soon remember this "swollen" Lyde of Juvenal. In this way, the characters are wonderfully introduced to provoke laughter: Silenus, his veins inflated with wine, and the pot-bellied Lyde, who calls him away—staggering and slipping as he is—even (if it please the gods) to save him from being mocked.

Z

<summary>The author performs a philological correction of Juvenal's text, changing "cacethos" to "cacoethes" and identifying a specific manuscript source. He also interprets the character Lyde as a comedic, stout old woman, linking Juvenal's description to a scene in Martianus Capella.</summary>

### What Pliny reported regarding the "pending" titles used by ancient artists, and how these were recognized on ancient monuments in Rome; also, the appearance of Virgil's aegis.

### CHAPTER XLVII.

**R**ecently in Rome, in the courtyard of the Mellini family home, we looked upon a certain marble base on which was written in Greek: "King Seleucus; Lysippus was making [this]." <note>original: "σέλευκος βασιλεὺς λύσιππος ἐποίει" (*Seleukos basileus Lysippos epoiei*). King Seleucus I Nicator was a general of Alexander the Great, and Lysippus was the renowned court sculptor.</note> In Latin, this means: *King Seleucus; Lysippus was making.* 

At that time, Giovanni Lorenzo of Venice was with us; he is a secretary to the Pope, a man most learned in both Latin and Greek, and a devoted student of all such "luxuries" of antiquity. <note>Giovanni Lorenzo refers to a humanist scholar and papal official; the term "luxuries" here refers to fine antiquities and rare objects of study.</note> It was he who pointed out to us—and we immediately grasped the point—that it was not by chance that the word "was making" <note>original Greek: "ἐποίει" (*epōiei*), the imperfect tense.</note> was used there rather than "made." <note>original Greek: "ἐποίησεν" (*epoiēsen*), the aorist tense. <margin>He was making.</margin> The author is distinguishing between a completed action and a continuous one.</note> 

For indeed, as Pliny mentions in the preface to his *Natural History*, <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), whose *Naturalis Historia* was the definitive Roman encyclopedia of science and art.</note> those supreme artists inscribed even their finished works with a "pending" title <note>original: *pendenti titulo*. A signature that implies the work is still in progress.</note>—such as "Apelles was making" or "Polyclitus was making"—as if the art were always in an unfinished and imperfect state. This was done so that, in the face of various criticisms, the artist might have a way to seek indulgence, as though he would have corrected whatever was found wanting had he not been interrupted.

K ij

<summary>The author discusses a Greek inscription found in Rome, explaining that ancient masters signed works in the imperfect tense ("was making") to humbly suggest their art was never truly perfect.</summary>

"Therefore," he says, "it is full of modesty that they inscribed all their works as if they were the most recent, and as if something had been snatched away from each one by fate." <note>This quote refers to Pliny the Elder’s *Natural History*, Preface 26-27. He explains that artists like Apelles or Polyclitus used the "pending" signature (*epoiei*—"was making") to suggest that art is never truly finished, and only death or fate interrupted the work.</note> However, no more than three works are handed down as being inscribed "absolutely" <note>By "absolutely," the author means using the completed perfect tense (e.g., "Lysippus made this").</note> (as he believes). He returns those to their proper places. From this, it appeared that the highest confidence in his art pleased the author. <note>original: "summam artis securitatem" — a sense of "artistic certainty" or "security" that borders on arrogance, which is why Pliny notes it was viewed with envy.</note> And for that reason, all those works were viewed with great envy. Moreover, the same Giovanni Lorenzo of Venice asserted that similar titles could be found elsewhere in Rome.

We also saw—which we thought should be included in these Miscellanies of ours—a certain marble statue of Pallas <note>Pallas is another name for the Greek goddess Athena.</note> in that same city, <margin>The Aegis of Pallas.</margin> wearing the Aegis <note>The Aegis was a protective garment or breastplate, often fringed with snakes and bearing the head of the Gorgon Medusa.</note> covered with the scaly hide of dragons, such that little dragons likewise enclosed its outer edge in place of a border or fringe.

With us there at the time was Giuliano of Bologna, <margin>The Antiquarian.</margin> who now rightly bears the surname "Antiquarius" <note>Giuliano "Antiquario" (1443–1517) was a prominent humanist and collector of antiquities in the court of Milan and Rome.</note> because of his expertise. And likewise, Giorgio Italiani of Genoa, a man most experienced as an investigator of hidden things, when we immediately recalled the Virgilian verses, in which that incomparable poet seemed to have wonderfully expressed that very carving of Pallas's shield, writing thus in the eighth book of the *Aeneid*:

> And the horrific Aegis, the arms of enraged Pallas,
> They were polishing with rivalry using scales of serpents and gold,
> And the entwined snakes, and on the breast of the goddess,
> The Gorgon herself, turning her eyes with her neck severed.

### CHAPTER XLVIII.

**P**liny the Elder, in the thirty-fifth volume of his *Natural History*, <margin>A passage from Pliny.</margin> states that Alexander of Macedon <note>Alexander the Great (356–323 BCE), the King of Macedon who conquered the Persian Empire.</note> used to frequent the workshop of the most excellent painter, Apelles, being delighted not only by his artistic skill but also by the man's pleasant company. Pliny says that while Alexander was in the workshop, discussing many things quite ignorantly, Apelles would politely urge him to be silent, remarking that he was being laughed at by the young boys who were grinding the pigments.

What Pliny writes concerning Alexander, Plutarch <note>A Greek philosopher and biographer (c. 46–119 CE) known for his "Parallel Lives" and "Moralia."</note> attributed to Megabyzus <margin>Megabyzus.</margin> in that volume where he disputes the differences between a flatterer and a friend. If we were to translate Plutarch's words into English, they are essentially these: "Apelles," he says, "the painter, said to Megabyzus, who was sitting near him and attempting to say something regarding line and shadow: 'Do you see how those boys grinding Melian earth <note>original: "melida" (from *melinum*), a white earth pigment from the island of Melos used by ancient painters.</note>—who were lately paying close attention to you while you were silent, marvelling at your purple robes and gold jewelry—now mock you as you begin to speak about matters you have not learned?'" Furthermore, Aelian <note>Claudius Aelianus (c. 175–235 CE), a Roman author and teacher of rhetoric who wrote in Greek.</note> also, in his books of *Various History*, attributed this anecdote to Megabyzus and not to Alexander.

### CHAPTER XLIX.

<margin>A passage from Ausonius.</margin>
There is a celebrated epigram by the poet Ausonius <note>Decimus Magnus Ausonius (c. 310–395 CE), a Roman poet and teacher from Bordeaux.</note> in which a statue of Opportunity is described. For he interpreted the god whom the Greeks call *Kairos* <note>original: "καιρὸν" (*kairos*), the personification of the "opportune moment" or the "right time."</note> in this very manner. But I wonder greatly why he attributed to Phidias <note>A famous Athenian sculptor and architect (c. 480–430 BCE) known for the Statue of Zeus at Olympia.</note> what was actually the work of Lysippus <note>A renowned Greek sculptor of the 4th century BCE; he was the personal sculptor to Alexander the Great and famous for his statue of Kairos.</note>. For there still exists a Greek epigram by Posidippus <note>Posidippus of Pella (c. 310–240 BCE), an epigrammatic poet whose poem on the statue of Kairos is a classic of the genre.</note> on a matching subject, upon which Ausonius modeled his own, although in the Greek version the artist is said to be Lysippus of Sicyon, while in the Latin, it is Phidias.

Furthermore, the Greek author says the figure walks on his tiptoes, whereas the Latin author says he stands upon a small wheel. Both writers attach winged sandals to the feet; both have the face covered with hair; both leave the back of the head bare. However, that Greek *Kairos* <note>original: "καιρὸς"</note> carries a razor in his right hand, and Repentance is joined as a companion to the Latin Opportunity. The figures in both poets are similar, yet the Greek versions are somewhat more pleasing; for in all these "embellishments" <note>original: "mangonissandis," a colorful term derived from *mango* (a dealer or furbisher of goods), meaning to artificially polish or dress something up to make it more attractive.</note> (if I may use such a term), somehow the Greeks are more elegant than our Romans. It was inevitable, then, that the copy <margin>Original / Copy</margin> <note>original: "apographon"</note> should fall short of the original <note>original: "antigrapho"</note>. But we also read the description <note>original: "ecphrasis," a rhetorical device where one medium of art attempts to relate to another by defining and describing its essence and form.</note> by Callistratus...

For it happens, I know not how, that Roman gravity <margin>Roman gravity.</margin> dreads those little playful and delightful flourishes—much like a finely woven Milesian cloak <note>original: "textam Mileti chlamydē"; Milesian wool was famous for its luxury and softness, often associated with decadence.</note>—worse than a dog or a snake <note>original: "cane peius & angui"; a proverbial expression used by the Roman poet Horace to describe something avoided at all costs.</note>. ### CHAPTER 50.

<margin>A passage in Pliny.</margin> In the twenty-fifth book of the *Natural History*, in the chapter where hellebore <note>original: *helleboro*. Hellebore was the primary treatment for madness and melancholy in the ancient world.</note> is discussed, the words of Pliny the Elder <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus (23–79 CE), a Roman author and naturalist.</note> are found as follows in the most common manuscripts: 

"The fame of Melampus <note>A legendary Greek seer and healer said to have learned the language of animals after licking the ears of snakes.</note> is well-known for his arts of divination; from him, one kind of hellebore is called *Melampodion*. Some record that a shepherd of the same name discovered it, that shepherd having noticed that goats were purged [after eating it], and that by giving their milk, he cured the mad parotids." <note>original: *parotidas furentes*. This reading is what the author is about to challenge.</note>

However, in one very ancient manuscript from that most noble library of the Medici family <note>The Laurentian Library in Florence, founded by the Medici family, renowned for its collection of rare classical texts.</note>, we found not "by a shepherd" (*pastore*), but "having fed" (*pasto*), and not "parotids" (*parotidas*), but "the Daughters of Proetus" (*Proetidas*). 

Now, regarding the choice between "shepherd" and "having fed," the matter is easily resolved. However, there is clear evidence that one should read "Daughters of Proetus" <note>The Proetides, daughters of King Proetus of Argos, who were struck with madness by Hera or Dionysus, leading them to believe they were cows and wander the wilderness.</note> and not "parotids." That this reading is the most indubitable and true—beyond what reason itself teaches as especially effective (for what, after all, would "mad parotids" be? <margin>Parotids.</margin> Or what have parotids, which are an ailment of the ears, to do with hellebore? <margin>Hellebore (Veratrum).</margin>)—is also most clearly declared by that excellent authority Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 CE), a Greek physician and botanist whose *De Materia Medica* was the definitive work on pharmacy for 1,500 years.</note>, writing thus: "Black hellebore—which some call *Melampodium* <margin>Melampodium.</margin>, others *ectomon*..."

### WHAT THE "ALTAR CROWDED WITH HORNS" IN MARTIAL MIGHT BE
### CHAPTER 52

In the aforementioned epigram, there is this second passage <margin>A passage from Martial.</margin> which, as far as I can see, has been understood neither by Domitius <note>Domitio Calderino (1446–1478) was an influential Renaissance scholar known for his commentaries on classical Latin poets like Martial and Juvenal.</note> nor by others:

For since the most magnificent buildings of the world are being recounted here—those which are counted among the wonders of the world—so that by comparison with them...

Plutarch also, in his *Life of Theseus*, writes about the altar in Delos, stating it was constructed not from right-side horns, but only from left-side horns; he calls it the "Horned Altar" <note>original Greek: κεράτωνα βωμὸν (*keratona bomon*)</note>, unless the manuscript is corrupted. ->At four years of age, Phoebus Apollo laid the first foundations<-
->In beautiful Ortygia, within the circular lake.<-
->Artemis, while hunting, would continually bring the heads<-
->Of the goats of Mount Cynthus; and Apollo wove the altar.<-
->He built the foundations with horns, and fixed the altar<-
->From horns, and placed horned walls all around.<-
<note>original Greek: Τετραέτης τὰ πρῶτα θεμείλια φοῖβος ἔπηξε / Καλῇ ἐν ορτυγίῃ, περιηγέος ἔνδοθι λίμνης, / ἄρτεμις ἀγρώσσουσα καρήατα συνεχὲς αἰγῶν / κυνθιάδων φορέεσκεν. ὁ δ’ ἔπλεκε βωμὸν ἀπόλλω̃ / Δείματο μὲν κεράεσσιν ἐδέθλια, πῆξε δὲ βωνὸμ / ἐκ κεράων, κεραοὺς δ’ πέριξ ὑπεβάλλετο τοίχους.</note>

For this reason, Martial says, "Let Delos look down upon its god," <note>Referring to Martial's *Spectacles* 1.4: *Dissimulet Delos Phoebi*. Indeed, throughout the entire epigram, this most clever poet <margin>Martial</margin> uses pointed words; <margin>wit.</margin> he commands Memphis to be silent because it is barbarian <note>A reference to the Pyramids of Giza.</note>, and he does not want Babylon to boast of its constant labor <note>A reference to the Hanging Gardens or the walls of Babylon.</note>, since that very boasting implies the realization of how much toil was involved.

<margin>Cicero's little errors.</margin>
Aulus Gellius, in those most brilliant *Attic Nights* <note>A famous 2nd-century CE collection of notes on grammar, philosophy, and history by Aulus Gellius.</note> of his, notes a most manifest error of Cicero. It is not regarding a great matter, nor does he wonder that Cicero erred, but rather that the error was not afterwards noticed either by Cicero himself or by his assistant <term>Tiro<note>Marcus Tullius Tiro was Cicero’s freedman and personal secretary, credited with inventing an early system of shorthand.</note></term>. <margin>Gellius’s freedom.</margin> For when Cicero translates certain verses of Homer into Latin, he writes that they are spoken by Ajax in that poet's work, whereas in reality they are spoken by Hector. OF MISCELLANIES. 66

Although we find it written no differently in every most ancient book, it was certainly not a slippery slip from one name to the other because of any similarity in the letters. These are the words of Cicero in the second book of *On Divination* <note>original: "libro diuinationū ſecundo"</note>: "For I would wonder at that, if I believed it, which you said Calchas did in Homer: that he augured the years of the Trojan war from the number of sparrows; regarding whose conjecture, Agamemnon speaks thus in Homer, as we have leisurely translated: <margin>*Iliad*, Book 2</margin>

> Endure, men, and tolerate hard labors with your soul,
> So that we may be able to know the fates of our augur Calchas,
> Whether they are held as certain, or if the portents of his heart be true."

And thereafter he sets forth the rest of the Homeric verses, translated by himself. Therefore, since Cicero relates that Agamemnon speaks these things in Homer—if indeed Cicero left it written so—this is proven to be in no way true. For in Homer himself, in the second <term>Rhapsody<note>A term used to describe a book or "canto" of an epic poem, particularly the *Iliad* or *Odyssey*.</note></term> of the *Iliad*, it is not Agamemnon, but Ulysses who pronounces the verses of that sentiment. Nor is there any reason for anyone to be annoyed that these things are also discovered in Cicero, or to wonder, since it appears from his own letters that sometimes errors of memory of this kind occurred in Cicero’s books, which were noticed and seen to by either <term>Atticus<note>Titus Pomponius Atticus, Cicero's closest friend and frequent correspondent, who often acted as his editor and publisher.</note></term> or <term>Brutus<note>Marcus Junius Brutus, the famous statesman and assassin of Caesar, who was also a literary correspondent of Cicero.</note></term>. Just like that instance in the speech for Ligarius regarding Lucius Cursidius, which Cicero confesses was his own error. But (as they say) he calls it a **slip of the memory** <note>original: "μνημονικὸν ἁμάρτημα"</note>. Just as that which in the *Orator*...

I think it should read, "lest they be marked in many places by your little red wax," so that the meaning is that Cicero had devoted himself to the composing of books—for that is <margin>*syntaxis*</margin> *syntaxis* in Greek. But he feared nonetheless that they would have to be marked in many places by the little red wax of Atticus himself, to whom he was writing and whom he often used as an editor, because Cicero was <margin>*meteoros*</margin> *meteoros*, that is, of a suspended mind, and (as he says) hindered by great thoughts. I suppose Atticus was accustomed, if any passage was handled with insufficient perfection by Cicero, to affix red wax markers to that spot, a practice we also still follow today. **C**ERATINAE <note>The "Ceratine" or "Horned" syllogism is a famous logical fallacy or "catch-22" puzzle.</note>—you will not easily find someone to explain to you who these are in the first book of Quintilian. <margin>Quintilian, book 1.</margin> However, those who seek out both Greek and Latin commentaries <margin>The Horned Arguments.</margin> will clearly understand that this is a type of inexplicable reasoning which introduces certain "handles" and "hooks" of dialectical perplexity. I believe it was named from the very subject matter of the words used, just as it appears in Seneca’s fifth book of letters to Lucilius: "I might sit idle," he says, "and pose little riddles of this kind: 'What you have not lost, you have. You have not lost horns; therefore, you have horns,'" and other such things devised after the pattern of this sharp madness. But you may also find information on this same subject in the *Attic Nights*. <note>A famous work by Aulus Gellius that preserved many fragments of ancient knowledge.</note> Furthermore, *horns* <note>original: κέρατα (*kerata*)</note> is the Greek word for "horns" <note>original: *Cornua*</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Ceratine" (Horned) syllogism, moving from the Latin examples of Seneca and Gellius into the Greek literary and philosophical tradition, citing Lucian, Diogenes Laertius, and Zeno of Citium.</meta>

->B O O K<-

Who would not know of this? Lucian also mentions this in his *Symposium of Philosophers*, just as he does with the *Sorites* <note>original: "Sorite"; the "Heaper" paradox: how many grains of sand make a heap?</note> and the *Therizon* <note>original: "Therizonte"; the "Reaper" paradox.</note>. But he speaks of it likewise in the *Dialogue of the Dead* between Castor and Pollux, saying roughly this: "And they sprout horns among themselves, and create crocodiles <note>A reference to the "Crocodile" fallacy, where a crocodile promises to return a stolen child if the parent correctly predicts what the crocodile will do.</note>, and they teach how to ask such perplexing questions that from these 'horns,' as it were, you should understand this 'Ceratine' reasoning we mentioned—though you might more accurately call it a snare than a piece of reasoning." Furthermore, in another dialogue titled either *The Dream* or *The Cock*, he writes: "And teaching," he says, "that from two negations one affirmation is made, and that it is not night if it is day. Sometimes he even told me that I had horns, and other things of that sort."

In such dialectical little puzzles of inquiry, which the sharpness of wits frequently devises, Eubulides of Miletus <note>A 4th-century BCE philosopher of the Megarian school, famous for inventing logical paradoxes used to confound opponents.</note> also made great use of them, as they are recorded by Diogenes Laertius in his *Life of Euclides of Megara*. To attempt to translate these into English, they are: The Liar, the Deceiver, the Veiled Electra, the Heaper, the Horned (or "the Cornea"), and likewise the Bald Man <note>original: "Mentiens, fallens, electra conuelata, acerualis, & ceratine, uel cornea, & item calua." These are the seven classic logical paradoxes attributed to Eubulides.</note>. From this there is a passage in a certain comic poet, as the same Laertius reports: "But Eubulides, more insultingly posing the Horned arguments and confounding the orators with lying and boastful speeches, departed with the fluency of Demosthenes." <note>The great orator Demosthenes was said to have studied under Eubulides to master the art of debate.</note> This passage was also cited by Suidas <note>The Suda, a massive 10th-century Byzantine Greek encyclopedia of the ancient world.</note>. But even the famous Zeno of Citium <note>The founder of the Stoic school of philosophy.</note>, among the other quibbles of the dialecticians, also numbered the Horned ones and the "No-ones" <note>original: "utidas"; a paradox based on the "Nobody" (Outis) name-trick used by Odysseus against the Cyclops.</note>...

## WHO THE CROCODILITES ARE according to the same author.

### CHAPTER LV.

In the same first book of Quintilian, mention is also made of "Crocodiline" <note>The "Crocodile" fallacy, a famous logical trap used to illustrate self-referential paradoxes.</note>, a term that is obscure and unknown to most. However, we find information about this in Doxapatres <note>John Doxapatres, an 11th-century Byzantine commentator on rhetorical textbooks.</note>, the Greek commentator on Aphthonius, although in his work it is called "Crocodilites" rather than "Crocodiline," which I believe is the truer term. We translate his own words into English as follows: "The Saw," he says, "and the Crocodilites, just as it appears in the Egyptian fable."

A certain woman was walking along the riverbanks with her son. A crocodile snatched her son away, saying he would return him if the woman answered truthfully <insert>regarding what he intended to do</insert>. She replied that he would not return the boy; therefore, she argued it was only fair that he be returned <note>Because if he does not return the boy, her statement is true, and by his own promise, he must return him. But if he does return him, her statement was false, and he shouldn't have done so.</note>. Thus far his account. And soon after, the same author says: "They call this proposition 'Crocodiliten' <margin>Crocodili-</margin> on account of the crocodile; <margin>tes.</margin> he also indicates that it is called *priona* <note>original: "πρίωνα"</note>, <margin>Saw.</margin> which means 'the Saw,' by the Greeks." Lucian supports this in the dialogue titled *The Sale of Lives*, describing Chrysippus <note>A Stoic philosopher (c. 279–206 BCE) renowned for his mastery of logic and his work on such "insoluble" paradoxes.</note> in roughly this way:

<summary>This page explains the "Crocodile" fallacy (Crocodilites), a logical paradox where a crocodile promises to return a stolen child if the mother correctly predicts its actions. It cites historical sources including Quintilian, Martianus Capella, and the Greek commentator Doxapatres to define the term and its alternate name, "The Saw."</summary>

...introducing a speaker, and someone responding to him who claims to be a buyer. Consider it this way: Do you have a son? To what purpose is that asked? If by chance a Crocodile finds him wandering near the river, seizes him, and then promises to return him provided you speak the truth—would you think he ought to be returned or not? What would you say his opinion is? You ask a truly perplexing thing. From these examples, I believe it is clear that "the Crocodile" <note>original: "Crocodiliten"</note>, just like "the Horned" <note>original: "ceratinen"</note> mentioned above, is a type of sophism that is difficult to explain, which the ancient logicians <note>original: "dialectici"</note>, and especially the Stoics, used to employ. Quintilian elegantly called this category "exquisite ambiguities." <note>original: "exquisitas ambiguitates." Quintilian was a Roman educator and rhetorician whose work influenced Renaissance scholarship.</note>

## CHAPTER LVI.

<margin>A passage from Martial.</margin>

There is an epigram by Martial in his work *On the Spectacles* <note>A collection of poems by the Roman poet Martial celebrating the opening of the Flavian Amphitheatre, or Colosseum.</note> regarding the Rhinoceros, of which these are the final lines:
"For with its double horn it tossed the heavy bear, just as a bull tosses the balls <note>original: "pilas." Domitius <note>Domitius Calderinus, a prominent 15th-century scholar and commentator on classical texts.</note> explains it thus: "With a double horn," he says, means strong and powerful. Domitius <note>Domitius Calderinus, a 15th-century scholar.</note> perhaps devised this so that the bear might be more easily tossed upward by these horns. However, Pausanias is not thinking of the Rhinoceros itself, but as we have said, of the Ethiopian bull, which was itself called a "Rhinoceros" by some due to a certain resemblance. Therefore, we think Martial <note>A reference to Martial’s *On the Spectacles*, where he describes a rhinoceros in the arena.</note> should be interpreted in this way: that he means the bear was tossed and thrown by that single horn of the Rhinoceros, and that the Rhinoceros would be "heavy for the double horn" <note>original: "grauem gemino cornu"</note>—that is, it would be a heavy challenge for a bull, which has twin horns. In this reading, "horn" <note>original: "cornu"</note> is in the dative case rather than the ablative. For although bulls might toss stuffed dummies <note>original: "pilas" — these were straw-filled figures used to provoke animals in the arena</note> to the stars with their horns, they still could not lift a heavy bear with a double horn; for that reason, the Rhinoceros lifted him more miraculously with only one horn.

Tetraones. **W**hat kind of birds the *Tetraones* <term>Tetraones: generally identified as birds of the grouse family, such as the black grouse or capercaillie</term> mentioned in Suetonius’s *Life of Caligula* are is entirely unknown. MISCELLANIES. 70

For what we read in his [Pliny's] tenth book of *Natural History*, where he treats geese: "Their own luster and absolute blackness suits the *Erythrotaonae* <note>original: "Decet Erythrotaonas suus nitor, absolutaq; nigritia"</note>." In that most ancient copy of Pliny from the public library of the Medici family <note>The Laurentian Library in Florence, famous for its collection of classical manuscripts curated by the Medici</note>, this is read almost correctly—that is, with only one letter changed. Such is the case with many words interpolated in all ancient volumes; for although they are themselves very faulty in many places, they still preserve traces of the true reading to be tracked down, which are utterly wiped out in new books by dishonest scribes. In that manuscript, therefore, it stands thus: "Decet & traonas," so that if you swap the order of the first two letters <note>Turning "& t" into "te"</note>, "*tetraonas*" leaps out. Therefore, following the authority of Poliziano <note>Angelo Poliziano, a leading 15th-century humanist scholar known for his textual criticism</note>, you should read it as follows: "Their own luster and absolute blackness suits the *tetraones* <term>tetraones: birds of the grouse family, likely the black grouse (*Lyrurus tetrix*) or capercaillie</term>. In their eyebrows is a scarlet redness."

    The other kind of them exceeds the size of vultures, whose color they also reflect. No bird, <margin>Ostrich.</margin> except for the Ostrich <note>original: "Struthocamelo"</note>, fills a greater weight of body; they are increased to such a size that they are even caught on the ground while motionless <note>The author suggests the birds are so heavy they cannot take flight quickly enough to escape capture</note>. The Alps and the northern regions produce them. Thus far he [Pliny] speaks of the *tetraones*.

**CHAPTER LVIII.**

<margin>Origin of the Secular games.</margin>
**I** believe it will be worthwhile to collect here also the origin of the Secular Games <note>The *Ludi Saeculares* were a major Roman religious celebration involving sacrifices and theatrical performances, intended to mark the end of a *saeculum* (the maximum span of a human life, usually 100 or 110 years) and the beginning of a new one.</note>, the reason for the name, and likewise the span of years between them, how many times they were held, by what ritual, and for which gods they were celebrated. For the matter is itself not unworthy of knowledge for anyone with any passion for antiquity, and it brings immense aid to understanding several passages of the poets <note>Roman poets such as Horace, who wrote the *Carmen Saeculare* for the games under Emperor Augustus, frequently refer to these rituals.</note>. 

<margin>Tricks of demons.</margin>
When, therefore, the Romans and the Albans <note>The inhabitants of Alba Longa, an ancient city in Central Italy and a traditional rival to early Rome.</note> were at war, and both battle lines already stood ready for combat, there suddenly appeared a figure of monstrous appearance, wrapped in a dark skin <note>original: "pelle amictus furua"</note>, shouting that Father Dis <note>The Roman god of the underworld, also known as Pluto.</note> and the goddess Proserpina commanded that a sacred rite be performed for them before the battle was joined. 

Terrified by this sight, the Romans immediately built an altar under the earth; and once the sacrifice was performed, they covered it with a mound twenty feet high, so that it might be unknown to everyone except the Romans themselves. 

<margin>Valesus Valesius.</margin>
But it happened that Valesus Valesius <note>A legendary figure in Roman mythology, considered the ancestor of the Valerii, one of Rome's most prominent patrician families.</note>, from whom the name and origin of the Valeria family comes—a most famous man among the Sabine people <note>An ancient Italic tribe neighboring the early Romans.</note> and a wealthy man of the countryside...

...life, this altar of which we spoke <note>The altar of Dis and Proserpina, which was said to be buried twenty feet underground at the site called Tarentus.</note>, he found in such a divine manner. <margin>Origin of the Valerian family.</margin> He possessed a grove in front of his villa with very tall trees, which were suddenly struck by lightning and burned down. Not long after, his two sons and his daughter were seized by a plague-like disease and were suffering to the point that the doctors despaired. Therefore, the father, prostrating himself before his household gods <note>original: "laribus familiaribus," the protector spirits of a Roman home</note>, offered his own life and the life of the children’s mother for the safety of his children. Suddenly, a voice was heard from the grove that had been touched by heaven <note>A Roman idiom for being struck by lightning.</note>, promising that they would be healthy if they were carried down the Tiber to Tarentum and drank water from the river itself, warmed on the hearth of Dis and Proserpina <note>The king and queen of the underworld.</note>. Because he knew that Tarentum was a very long way off—namely, in the furthest part of Iapygia <note>An ancient name for the region of Apulia in the "heel" of Italy.</note>—and that no Tiber river was found near that city, he began to form the worst of hopes. He was also terrified because that voice had mentioned the powers of the underworld in particular. Nevertheless, he placed his children in a small boat and, proceeding toward Hostia <note>Ostia, the port city of Rome.</note>, he soon sailed down to the region of the Field of Mars <note>original: "Campi Martij," a public area of ancient Rome where military training and certain religious rites occurred.</note>. There, wishing to refresh the children, who were thirsty and suffering from heat and fever, he set the sick ones out on the bank where the river flows most peacefully. While he was preparing a makeshift little mat <margin>Small mat.</margin> for them, the small fire having gone out, he was advised by the pilot that he should instead seek out Tarentum <margin>Tarentus.</margin> (for that was what the nearby place was called), for the pilot saw smoke rising there. Then indeed Valesius, becoming more joyful upon hearing the name of Tarentum, adored the gods; promising himself the safety of his children now with almost certain faith, he ordered they proceed immediately to that very spot.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origins and naming of the Tarentine or Secular Games, moving from the legend of the altar's discovery to a scholarly debate over why the games are so named and how often they should be celebrated, citing authorities like Varro, Horace, and Livy.</meta>

# MISCELLANIES. 72

They were called "Secular" <margin>Why they were called Secular.</margin> however, not because they were performed exactly in the hundredth year, or even the hundred and tenth, for although both views have their supporters, the facts themselves thoroughly refute this. Rather, it is more believable that they were named so because they usually occurred once in a human lifetime <note>The Latin word *saeculum* can mean either a century or the maximum span of a human life, roughly 100-110 years.</note>; for common speech often dictates that things which are rare happen "after an age." 

They are called "Tarentine," <margin>Tarentus.</margin> however, from the place itself, which is called Tarentus for that reason—either because the altar of Father Dis <note>The god of the underworld, also known as Pluto.</note> (as we mentioned above) was hidden there in the earth, or because the Tiber river wore away the bank at that spot. For this reason, the river was also called "Rumon" <margin>Rumon.</margin> by the ancients, and in sacred rites it was named "Serra," <margin>Serra.</margin> because it "ruminated," that is, it ate away and cut through the banks <note>The author is connecting the word *serra* (saw) and *ruminare* (to chew/ruminate) to the way a river erodes its banks.</note>. Hence Virgil <note>original: "Maro," referring to Publius Vergilius Maro.</note> says: 

->Grazing the banks, and cutting through the rich cultivated lands.<- <note>A quote from the *Aeneid*, Book 8, describing the Tiber.</note>

Furthermore, concerning the intervals of time at which these games were repeated, the historical evidence is uncertain. <margin>The timing of the Secular Games.</margin> Indeed, in his *Secular Hymn*, Horace declares that they take place every hundred and ten years <note>original: "undenos decies per annos," literally ten times eleven years.</note>, a fact confirmed by the commentaries of the Fifteen-Man College <note>The *Quindecimviri sacris faciundis*, the Roman priests responsible for guarding and interpreting the Sibylline Books.</note>, the edicts of the Divine Augustus, and finally by the oracle of the Sibyl itself, which still survives. On the other hand, Valerius Antias, Titus Livy, and likewise Marcus Varro are said to have testified that the games return after the hundredth year. As for what we read in Herodian <note>A Greek historian of the Roman Empire.</note>, that they were accustomed to be renewed after a space of three generations, I fear the text there is corrupt <note>original: "emendata sit ibi lectio," suggesting the manuscript version of Herodian's text might be error-ridden</note>, and for that reason our translation from the Greek with...

->## BOOK<-

I believe it should be read with indulgence. However, if you count the times, you should not conclude entirely how great the intervals were in the past, or how great they ought to be. It is believed that the first Secular games were established after the expulsion of the kings <margin>Establishment of the Secular games.</margin> by P. Valerius Publicola, who was the first consul. Indeed, while the citizens were suffering from a plague, at this very altar I mentioned, having publicly announced his vows, he sacrificed a black bull to Dis <note>The Roman god of the underworld, often associated with Pluto.</note> for the males and a heifer of the same color to Proserpina <note>The daughter of Ceres and queen of the underworld.</note> for the females. He performed games and *lectisternia* <term>lectisternia: An ancient Roman ritual where images of the gods were placed on couches (lecti) and offered a banquet as if they were guests.</term> for three nights, and he hid the altar by heaping the earth over it as it had been before, inscribed with this title: 

->*I, P. Valerius Publicola, have consecrated the grain-producing field to Dis and Proserpina, and have performed games for Dis and Proserpina for the sake of the Roman people’s safety.*<-

The historian Zosimus <note>A Greek historian who wrote the *Historia Nova* in the 5th century AD.</note> records in Greek that the second games were held in the fifty-second year after the founding of Rome <note>original: "post Romam conditam"</note>, at the warning of the Sibylline verses. When the city was gripped by disease, men were chosen for this purpose to consult the books <note>The Sibylline Books, a collection of oracular utterances consulted by the Roman state in times of crisis.</note>; according to their response, the earth was again dug up at this very altar at the edge of the Campus Martius <note>The "Field of Mars," a public area in Rome used for military training and certain religious rites.</note>. The sacred rites and games were performed according to custom, and safety was once again restored to the Romans. This writer, however, named only one consul—perhaps due to a flaw in his source manuscript—calling him M. Publius Quintius, whereas Censorinus <note>A 3rd-century AD grammarian and author of *De Die Natali*.</note> records M. Valerius and Spurius Verginius as the consuls. The same author mentions the third and also the fourth games during the times of the consuls, whom he also cites by name...

...[he] proves they were performed on the authority of Valerius Antias, Titus Livius <note>Livy, the famous Roman historian.</note>, Varro, Piso, Gellius, and Hemina; he even summons as evidence the commentaries of the Fifteen-Men for Performing Sacred Rites <term>Quindecimviri Sacris Faciundis: The college of priests responsible for guarding the Sibylline Books and overseeing foreign cults in Rome.</term>. Furthermore, Claudius held the sixth games during his own fourth consulship and that of Lucius Vitellius, acting as if they had been anticipated [celebrated too early] by Augustus and not reserved for the legitimate time. Claudius is remembered to have declared in his own histories that Augustus, after a very long interval and a most diligent calculation of the years, had brought them back into order. Wherefore even the voice of the herald is said to have been mocked, as he invited people in the solemn manner to games "which no one had ever seen, nor would see again" <note>This was the traditional herald's cry for the Secular Games, which were supposed to occur only once in a long lifetime (100 or 110 years).</note>, even though there were people who had seen...

One hundred and ten years after the games of Domitian, the Emperor Septimius <margin>Septimius</margin> Severus restored them. <margin>The Arch of Severus</margin> Severus's most beautiful triumphal arch is still seen today in Rome, [commemorating his victories] with his sons, Antoninus and Geta, when Chilo and Libo <note>The Latin text reads "Vibone," likely a scribe's error for Marcus Annius Flavius Libo, who was consul with Lucius Fabius Cilo in 204 AD.</note> were consuls. Zosimus <note>A Greek historian who argued that the abandonment of Roman traditional religion, including these games, led to the empire's decline.</note> denies that they were restored after Severus, because this cycle of nearly one hundred and ten years, having been accepted by authority, would have fallen into the third consulship of Constantine, the Christian prince, and Licinius (whom Constantine later defeated in war). # BOOK 37

[<margin>Philip the Younger.</margin>]
Philip of the household gods, also called Philip the Younger, who is believed to have never laughed, is said to have turned his face away in disapproval when he noticed his father, Philip the Elder, laughing too boisterously. It remains for us to briefly touch upon the ritual itself in a summarized fashion. 

[<margin>The ritual of the Secular Games.</margin>]
As the games approached, heralds were sent throughout all of Italy to summon people to games "which no one had seen before and no one would see again." <note>This was a traditional ritual formula; since the games were ideally held every 110 years, no one could expect to witness them twice in a single lifetime.</note> Then, in the summertime, a few days before the spectacles were presented, the Fifteen Men for Performing Sacred Rites <note><term>Quindecimviri sacris faciundis</term>: one of the four prestigious colleges of Roman priests, tasked with guarding the Sibylline Books and overseeing these specific games.</note> sat upon a platform in the temples on the Capitoline and Palatine hills

[<margin>Purifications.</margin>]
and distributed purifications to the people, which consisted of torch-wood, sulfur, and bitumen. <note>original: "teda, sulfur, & bitumen." These materials were used by citizens for ritual cleansing of their homes before the festival began.</note> However, slaves were not permitted to receive these. The people gathered not only in the places mentioned above, but also at the temple of Diana on the Aventine Hill, where it was the custom to give out wheat, beans, and barley. 

[<margin>In the manner of.</margin>]
Then, in the manner of the initiations of Ceres, <note>original: "ad instar Cereris initiorum." These refers to the secretive and solemn nature of the mysteries associated with the goddess of agriculture.</note> all-night vigils were held. When the festival day finally arrived, they spent three days and three nights focused on the rites, conducted mostly on the banks of the Tiber River. Sacrifices were performed for Jupiter, Juno, Apollo, Latona, and Diana, and additionally for the Fates <note>original: "Parcis."</note> and those known as the Ilithyias <note>The Greek goddesses of childbirth.</note>, as well as for Ceres, Dis <note>The god of the underworld, also known as Pluto.</note>, and Proserpina. Thus, in the second hour of the first night, the Prince <note>Referring to the Emperor.</note> himself, along with the Fifteen Men, sacrificed three lambs at three altars built on the riverbank. Once the altars were soaked with blood, they burned the bodies of the victims. A stage was also constructed in the style of a theater, and lights...

[ornamental paragraph mark] fires were lit, and hymns were sung, composed specifically for this use, and spectacles were solemnly presented as usual. These were celebrated with rewards of wheat, beans, and barley, which we have shown above were divided among the whole populace. In the morning, they were accustomed to ascend the Capitol, perform the sacred rites according to custom, then gather in the theater for the games to be held in honor of Apollo and Diana. On the following day, it was the custom for noble matrons, at the hour prescribed by the oracle, to gather on the Capitol to pray to the god, attend the sacred banquets <note><term>Lectisternia</term>: an ancient Roman ritual where images of the gods were placed on couches and offered a symbolic meal.</note>, and sing hymns according to the ritual. Finally, on the third day, in the temple of Apollo on the Palatine, thrice-nine <note>That is, twenty-seven.</note> boys wearing the bordered toga <note><term>Prætextati</term>: young boys of noble birth who wore the <term>toga prætexta</term>, which featured a purple stripe.</note> and as many girls—all of whom were *patrimi* and *matrimi* <note><term>Patrimi et matrimi</term>: children whose fathers and mothers were both still living, a status required for certain religious ceremonies to ensure they were free from the "pollution" of death.</note>—sang poems and hymns of praise in both the Greek and Roman languages, through which they commended their empire and the safety of the people to the immortal gods. If anyone should perhaps ask what kind of games or sacrifices were performed then, let him know that games were celebrated in all theaters and of every kind, and sacrifices were customary in all temples, both by night and by day. For there is no doubt about the theatrical and Circus games, since we remember that actors participated in the games of Domitian; furthermore, we read that on the day of the Circus games, so that one hundred races <note>original: "missus." A *missus* was a single race or heat in the circus.</note> could be more easily completed, each race was shortened from seven laps to five. By the light of day—for the light is most pleasing to the celestial gods.
To Juno's temple, bring a heifer with a gleaming neck...

### That Ovid, not Virgil, appears to be the author of the Priapean poem. Also, that the little verse found in Martial—"Smile, if you are wise, O girl, smile"—undoubtedly belongs to Ovid, though it seems to be quoted from an epigram rather than from the *Art of Love* or his tragedy *Medea*; and immediately following, something new and remarkable regarding Ovid’s *Medea* is added. CHAPTER 59.

<margin>The *Priapeia*.</margin> Most people attribute the epigrams known as the *Priapeia* <note>A collection of roughly 80 poems dedicated to the fertility god Priapus, characterized by their crude humor and clever wordplay.</note> to Virgil; among these, I believe, is even Servius <note>Maurus Servius Honoratus, a 4th-century grammarian famous for his commentary on Virgil.</note>. However, in the books of Seneca <note>Refers here to Seneca the Elder, whose works on Roman rhetoric were rediscovered and printed in the late 15th century.</note>—which had long lain hidden and only recently came to light, though they were published in a somewhat corrupted state—in which he collects the opinions, divisions, and rhetorical flourishes <term>colores</term> <note>In Roman rhetoric, a "color" was a particular "slant" or interpretation given to a case to make a character's actions seem more excusable or favorable.</note> of the orators and rhetoricians in their persuasive speeches <term>suasoriarum</term> <note>A type of practice speech where a student or orator advised a historical or mythological figure on a course of action.</note> (unless we happen to believe the only surviving manuscript is faulty), I have discovered that these poems are spurious and belong not to Virgil, but to Ovid. Indeed, in Seneca's second volume, the words are as follows: "I remember a certain man of praetorian rank saying this kind of sentiment when he was declaiming a legal case <term>controversiam</term> <note>A mock legal debate used in schools of rhetoric to train students in law and oratory.</note> about that woman who sued her husband for ill-treatment because she was still a virgin even after she had been given to him. Afterward, she sought a priesthood. 'We know,' he said, 'that abstinence of husbands, who even if they spared the first night for timid virgins, nevertheless play in neighboring places <note>original: "vicinis tamen locis ludunt." This is a euphemistic reference to sexual activity in "nearby" parts of the body, a line that appears in the *Priapeia* (Poem 3).</note>.'" Scaurus was listening to him, and not only...

Certainly, such a style suits Ovid, who is quite obscene in his other works; conversely, it is unbecoming of Virgil, who was even called **"Parthenias"** <margin>Parthenias</margin> <note>A Greek-derived nickname meaning "the maiden-like," referring to Virgil’s legendary bashfulness and moral purity.</note> because of his virginal modesty. # BOOK

->Laugh, if you are wise, O girl, laugh.<- <note>original: "Ride si sapis ô puella ride." This is a famous line from Martial (Epigrams 2.41.1), though its authorship was a point of scholarly debate in the Renaissance.</note>

For as to the precept that Domitius <note>Domitius Calderinus, a 15th-century rival scholar of Poliziano.</note> relates to some of the arts, it is certainly refuted by the authority of Martianus Capella. In his work *The Satura*, Martianus recognizes this as a little verse by Naso <note>Ovid's family name.</note> and not by Martial. I have even heard of those who believed it came from the tragedy *Medea* <margin>Medea</margin>—a work praised by both Tacitus and Quintilian. Tertullian is the authority for the claim (which I find astonishing) that this play was patched together <margin>Ovid's.</margin> almost entirely from Virgilian verses <note>A "cento," a poem made up of lines from other works.</note>; I also pointed this out in my poem *The Nurse*.

But this does not seem likely to me. For who has ever read Phalaecian meters <term>Phalaecians</term> <note>A light, hendecasyllabic meter (11 syllables) typically used for playful or erotic poetry, never for the "gravity" of tragedy.</note> anywhere in either a Greek or a Latin tragedy? Or what about this little verse tastes at all of the tragic, since it leaps away from and utterly shrinks from that gloomy gravity? Indeed, does not Martianus himself almost clearly deny that this is from a tragedy, if only his words are faithfully recited and diligently weighed? For does he not say that his *Satura*—because she was austere and censorious, and possessed too much of a tragic brow—chastised his playfulness...

<summary>The author argues against the theory that a specific playful verse belongs to Ovid's lost tragedy Medea, citing the mismatch between the light Phalaecian meter and the serious nature of tragic drama.</summary>

## THE WORD "AGON," WHICH THE SACRIFICER WAS ACCUSTOMED TO USE, OF WHICH OVID ALSO SPEAKS IN THE *FASTI*.

### CHAPTER LX.

**O**vid writes in the first book of the *Fasti*:
*The cause of the name may be the tucked-up attendant,*
*By whose blow the victim falls for the heavenly gods,*
*Who, about to dye the drawn knives with warm blood,*
*Always asks "Shall I strike?" nor does he strike unless ordered.*
<note>original: "Agatne rogat." Poliziano is explaining the etymology of the "Agonalia" festival, linking it to the word "Agon," the ritual question asked by the sacrificer.</note>

Concerning this rite of slaughtering a victim, and likewise concerning the word which the priest's assistant used, I find mention made only in Seneca—specifically in his third book of *Orators and Rhetoricians* <note>Referring to Seneca the Elder’s *Controversiae* 3.3.</note>—recorded in these words: "Then, amidst the greatest roar of the students: 'And you, whoever you are, executioner, when you have raised the drawn axe, look back at the father before you strike.'" Asinius Pollio <note>A famous Roman statesman and critic known for his sharp wit.</note> wittily mocked these dramatic displays. "The son," he said, "stretches out his neck. The executioner stretches out his hand, then let him look back at the father and say: 'Shall I strike? Shall I strike?'" <margin>Agon? Agon?</margin> <note>original: "Agon? Agon?" This is the ritual question (from the Latin "Agone?" meaning "Shall I do it?") asked by the official killer to the priest before the killing blow was delivered.</note>

<summary>Poliziano cites the Medici library's manuscripts to support his textual readings and then analyzes the Roman ritual term "Agon," used by sacrificial attendants, citing Ovid and Seneca.</summary>

## AN ERROR IN THE PLINIAN MANUSCRIPTS
### Concerning hemlock and wine; and a mistake in Macrobius where "Aconite" is used for "Hemlock."

### CHAPTER LXI.

<margin>Pliny’s passage.</margin> The words from the letter of Androcides to Alexander the Great—a man famous for his wisdom <note>Androcides was a physician to Alexander the Great, known for advising the king on temperance.</note>—are found faultily recorded in these manuscripts of Pliny in the following manner: *"King, when you are about to drink wine, remember that you drink the blood of the earth. Just as hemlock is a poison to man, so too is wine."* This is indeed a weak sentiment, and excessively forced and strained; I add also that it is tasteless and false to say that wine is a poison to man in the same way hemlock is.

But indeed, in that most ancient Plinian manuscript of the Medici family, which I have already cited often, you may find it written thus: <margin>Hemlock, wine, poison. The use of wine.</margin> *"Hemlock is a poison to man, but wine is a poison to hemlock."* <note>original: "Cicuta homini uenenum est, cicutae uinum."</note> For just as hemlock kills a man, so a drink of undiluted wine extinguishes the venom of hemlock. From this, Androcides strives to persuade Alexander...

MISCELLANIES. 80

...counseling him to use wine more sparingly, as a most powerful substance, because it is the **poison of poison.** <note>original: "ueneni uenenũ sit" — Poliziano argues that wine is so potent it acts against other poisons.</note> From this, he had taught in a similar sense that wine was the "blood of the earth." Therefore, Pliny concludes concerning Androcides' words: "Nothing is more useful for the strength of the body, nor more ruinous for pleasures, if moderation is absent." Similarly, the same Pliny, writing in his twenty-fifth book about hemlock <note>term: *cicuta* — hemlock, a highly toxic plant used in ancient executions, most famously that of Socrates.</note> as a deadly thing for humans, says: "The remedy, before it reaches the vital organs, is the warming nature of wine." This is the same thing that Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a Greek physician and botanist whose "De Materia Medica" was the primary pharmaceutical text for 1,500 years.</note> recorded. 

Therefore, Saint Ambrose, in the book he wrote *On the Usefulness of Fasting*, said: "Indeed, the power of wine is greater than that of poison. Ultimately, poison is driven out by wine, not wine by poison." <note>St. Ambrose (c. 340–397 AD) was an influential Bishop of Milan. Poliziano uses theological texts to support his botanical and medical claims.</note> Furthermore, Plutarch, in his *Table Talk* <note>original: "Symposiacis libris" — a collection of essays on various philosophical and scientific topics discussed at dinner parties.</note>, says almost the same things as Pliny regarding hemlock. Macrobius also attempted to express this passage—as well as many others—almost word for word in his *Saturnalia*. 

However, in all the manuscripts of Macrobius that I have seen so far, **"Aconite"** <note>term: *Aconitum* — Monkshood or Wolfsbane, a different genus of poisonous plant.</note> is found instead of **"Hemlock,"** even in the very old manuscript belonging to Bernardo Michelozzi, a well-educated man and our friend. <note>Bernardo Michelozzi was a Florentine humanist and secretary to the Medici family.</note> 

But I believe that copy is flawed. For I cannot be persuaded that Macrobius was so unlearned that he would translate what was *Koneion* <note>original: "Conion" — the Latinized form of the Greek word for hemlock.</note> in Greek as "Aconite." I would more readily believe he used the Greek term, just as Ambrose did in his *Hexaemeron* <note>margin: Ambrose's Hexaemeron.</note> <note>The *Hexaemeron* is a treatise on the six days of creation.</note>, where he said *Conium*—which Rufinus <note>Tyrannius Rufinus, a monk and translator known for bringing Greek patristic works into Latin.</note> translated as "hemlock" from Basil’s <note>Saint Basil the Great, whose work Ambrose used as a primary source.</note> book of the same subject and title. It is indeed a slippery slip from *conium* <margin>*Koneion* is not aconite, but Hemlock.</margin> into *aconitum*.
