->WHAT THE RAM’S TESTICLES ARE,
and what the medical finger is, according to Cicero.<-

## CHAPTER LXII.

<margin>Cicero the Rhetorician. A passage from Cicero.</margin>
**C**icero, in the third book of *To Herennius* <note>original: "ad Herennium" — *Rhetorica ad Herennium*, the oldest surviving Latin book on rhetoric, formerly attributed to Cicero but now thought to be by an unknown author.</note>, while dealing with the art of memory <note>term: *memoriæ artificio* — the "method of loci" or memory palace technique, where an orator visualizes objects in a room to remember parts of a speech.</note>, constructs a certain mental image in this way. "And we shall place the defendant," he says, "at his bedside, with a cup in his right hand, writing tablets in his left, and holding the ram’s testicles on the medical finger." <note>original: "Et reũ inquit ad lectum eius statuemus, dextra poculũ, sinistra tabulas, medico testiculos arietinos tenentem." This bizarre image was a mnemonic: the "testicles" (*testiculi*) helped the speaker remember "witnesses" (*testes*), and "ram" (*aries*) likely referred to a specific witness or the victim, such as the Roman citizen Canutius Aries.</note> Regarding this passage, I see that some scholars, even those of no small rank, hesitate to explain what we read: "holding the ram's testicles on the medical finger." 

<margin>The medical finger.</margin>
The "medical finger" <note>term: *medicus* — the ring finger. In antiquity, it was believed a special vein or nerve ran from this finger directly to the heart; physicians used it to stir medicinal ointments.</note> is what one of the five fingers is called—specifically, as I believe, the one closest to the smallest. For Porphyrio <note>Publius Porphyrio, a 3rd-century scholar known for his commentaries on the poet Horace.</note>, commenting on that verse of Horace from his last satire:
    *Who, if by chance anything were hidden,*
    *Would point it out with his index finger.* <note>original: "Qui si quid forte lateret, Indice monstraret digito."</note>

<margin>Names of the fingers.</margin>
He says: "This is because each individual finger was called by a specific name, and these are the names: Thumb, index, famous, medical, and smallest." <note>original: "Pollex, index, famosus, medicus, minimus." Note that "famosus" (the middle finger) was often called "infamous" or "shameful" in other texts.</note> However, certain Greeks—of a more recent age, I believe—write the names of the fingers in the Roman style in this way: *anticheir* (the thumb), *deiktikos* (the pointer), *iatros* (the physician), *daktyliotes* (the ring-wearer), and *otites* (the ear-finger). <note>original: "ἀντίχειρ, δεικτικός, ἰατρὸς, δακτυλιώτης, ὠτίτης." Poliziano is mapping Greek terms to the Latin equivalents: Thumb, index, medical, ring finger, and the little finger (used for cleaning the ear).</note> As if you were to say: Thumb, index, medical, ring-finger, ear-finger. For my part, I neither affirm nor reject this. It is only to be noted that the "medical finger" is said by him to be the one that is in the middle, or...

<summary>Poliziano clarifies a confusing passage in a Roman manual on rhetoric, explaining that the "medical finger" is the ring finger and that the "ram's testicles" was a mnemonic pun used by orators to remember witnesses.</summary>

### THE VERSES OF AUSONIUS EXPLAINED,
### along with Ovid, regarding the Choliambic or Scazon meter.
### CHAPTER LXIV.

<margin>Ausonius.</margin> These are the trimeter verses of the poet Ausonius:
"To whom shall I yoke, from the circular paths of the millstones,
Those who turn the stones with mechanical weight—
Three-legged nags, their backs broken by the whip?"
<note>Ausonius (c. 310–395 AD) was a Roman poet and teacher. These lines use the metaphor of a broken-down mill-horse to describe a specific poetic rhythm.</note>

What do we think he meant by this? Certainly, as I judge, he refers to those verses which are called "Choliambics" or "Scazons." <margin>Choliambics. Scazons.</margin> You might call them "limping" verses. <note>The terms *Choliamb* (Greek for "lame iambic") and *Scazon* (Greek for "limper") refer to a specific poetic meter where the very last beat is reversed, creating a jarring "thud" or "limp" at the end of the line.</note> These are like the ones found at the beginning of Persius, or that line by Martial, which he cited by name:
"My Scazon, go meet my friend Apollinaris."
<note>Poliziano is citing famous examples of this meter from the satirist Persius and the epigrammatist Martial to show how poets personified the meter itself.</note>

For a Scazon is an iambic trimeter; it consists of six feet just like a regular iambic verse, but it differs in this one respect (as Hephaestion <note>A 2nd-century Greek grammarian whose handbook on meters became the standard authority in the Renaissance.</note> also writes): while a regular iambic verse in its last position...

MISCELLANIES. 82

it occupies the seat with an <term>iamb|A poetic foot consisting of one short syllable followed by one long syllable.</term> or a <term>pyrrhic|A poetic foot consisting of two short syllables.</term>; in the third-to-last position, it accepts all feet of which that meter is capable, being indifferent regarding the quantities of the syllables. But this "limping" iambic requires a conclusion that is either <term>spondaic|A foot of two long syllables.</term> or entirely <term>trochaic|A foot of one long syllable followed by one short syllable.</term>. Moreover, it requires the second-to-last foot to be not just any two-syllable foot, but clearly an iamb or a spondee. However, regarding what Hephaestion <note>A 2nd-century Greek grammarian who wrote a definitive manual on meter.</note> says—that a <term>spondee</term> is received in the fifth seat of a choliamb—I do not recall ever finding this among the Latin writers, though he himself cites this Greek verse:
"Drawing it to the tip, just like one cooling a sausage."
<note>original Greek: "ἐπ’ ἄκρον ἕλκων ὥσπερ ἀλλᾶντα ψύχων" (*ep’ akron helkōn hōsper allanta psychōn*). This line is attributed to the poet Hipponax.</note>
And Aristides Quintilianus, a Greek author, in his book *On Music*, speaking about the iambic trimeter, says that it becomes "limping" when it receives a spondee at the very end. Finally, Ovid also, in the first book of the *Remedies for Love*, writes thus regarding both types of Iambics:
"Let the free Iamb be drawn against opposing enemies,
Whether it be swift, or whether it drags its final foot."
<note>Ovid refers to the "swift" (standard) iambic and the "dragging" (limping) choliambic as weapons for poetic attack.</note>
Both types, however, were created for slander and license; they are fierce and bloody. For this reason, Ovid says they should be "drawn" against opposing enemies. Likewise, Dionysius of Halicarnassus <note>A Greek historian and teacher of rhetoric in Rome (c. 60–7 BC).</note>, in his book *On Composition*, says that the choliamb is a "vehement" or "sharp" meter. He also says shortly after that Hipponax <margin>Choliambics.</margin> <note>A 6th-century BC Greek poet known as the inventor of the choliambic meter, used primarily for biting satire.</note>, when he wished to provoke his enemies with insults, intentionally broke the meter; instead of a regular rhythm, he made it "limping" and <term>off-beat|original: *abnumerum*</term> <margin>off-beat</margin>, which is to say, consistent with bitterness and verbal abuse.

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of poetic meters, specifically focusing on the "limping iambic" (choliambic). It follows the previous discussion of how Greek and Latin authors define the rhythmic irregularities of this form.</meta>

BOOK 28

For just as a loose figure prepares a certain sharpness, so (as <margin>Packhorses.</margin> he says) does a loose composition. For this reason, Ausonius certainly names those "limping iambics" (*choliambics*) as "packhorses" (*caballi*). He drew this metaphor from the war-chariot and the vehicle to which packhorses are joined; and to show that they are slow, he says they were taken from the circular paths of the mill-stones <note>Horses were often used to turn heavy mill-stones in a continuous circle, a grueling and repetitive task.</note>. Furthermore, he describes them as having "backs broken by the whip," and then called them "three-footed" (*tripedes*). He used this term because in Greek they are called <margin>Trimeters.</margin> <term>trimeters|A line of verse consisting of three dipodies (pairs of feet), totaling six feet.</term>, although they actually possess six feet; for in iambic verse, every two feet are seen to be counted as one. When he said that they "roll stones with mechanical weight," he did so either as if he were describing the mills he had indicated they turn, or to show them as allegorically violent and fierce, like those who hurl and roll huge stones against an enemy. Regarding both types of trimeter, the same Ausonius says elsewhere in a Greek verse:
<note>original Greek: εἰλιπόδην σκάζοντα κὴ οὐ σκάζοντα τρίμετρον (*eilipodēn skazonta kai ou skazonta trimetron*).</note>
"The dragging-footed limper, and the trimeter that does not limp."
Which you may translate into Latin <note>The author provides a Latin translation of the Greek verse: *Loripedem Scazonta, & non scazonta trimetrum*.</note> thus:
<margin>Scazons.</margin> "The <term>crooked-footed|loripes: literally 'thong-footed,' describing someone whose legs are weak or bend like leather straps.</term> Scazon, and the trimeter that does not limp."
Concerning these same <term>scazons|Another name for the limping iambic meter, from the Greek word for 'limping.'</term>, Pliny the Younger writes as follows in a certain letter from the fifth book: "Therefore, either break off your delays, or take care lest these same little books—which my <term>hendecasyllables|A verse line of eleven syllables, typically used for light, playful, or affectionate poetry.</term> could not draw out from you with flatteries—are extorted by the reproach of scazons." <note>Pliny is joking that if his "sweet" poetry doesn't get a response, he will resort to the "limping" scazon meter, which was traditionally used for biting satire and insults.</note>

It explains the technical counting of feet in iambic trimeter and the meter's historical association with aggressive or satirical content.</summary>

### CHAPTER LXV: ON THE POSE OF THE "PEACEMAKER" IN STATUES, AND STATIUS’S MEANING IN HIS FIRST *SILVAE*.

Statius, in his first collection of short poems <note>The *Silvae*, a collection of occasional poetry by the Roman poet Statius (c. 45–96 AD).</note>, specifically the one written regarding the "Great Horse" <note>The *Equus Maximus Domitiani*, a massive equestrian statue of the Emperor Domitian that once stood in the Roman Forum.</note> <margin>A passage from Statius</margin> says:
"It is a joy to behold a face
Marked with the signs of war, yet bearing a gentle peace."
And then again:
"The right hand forbids battles."

Domitius <note>Domitius Calderinus, a 15th-century humanist and commentator on classical texts.</note> explains this passage as follows: "The right hand forbids battles," meaning it is unarmed in the statue, or perhaps that the Temple of Peace stands to its right—a view he admits he does not like. However, while he may accept the sense that "battles are forbidden" because the hand is unarmed, or perhaps the association with the Temple of Peace (even though that doesn't satisfy the author himself), I will not be bothered if my own opinion on this matter is not approved. For I believe <margin>The Peacemaker's pose.</margin> that a specific "habit" or "pose" is being noted here, which in the context of statues was called the "peacemaker."

We read of this pose in the works of Quintilian <margin>A passage from Quintilian</margin> as follows: "There is also that pose which is customary for the 'peacemaker' in statues; with the head tilted toward the right shoulder and the arm extended from the ear, the hand is stretched out with an assertive thumb <note>Latin: *infesto pollice*. In Roman rhetoric, this specific gesture of the thumb and hand signaled authority and a call for silence or attention.</note>. This pose is particularly pleasing to those who boast of speaking with a raised hand." Thus far Quintilian. Furthermore, there still exists today (unless I am mistaken) a statue in Rome in this very "peacemaker" pose, which can be seen in front of the Lateran church <note>The author is referring to the famous bronze equestrian statue of Marcus Aurelius, which stood at the Lateran Palace before being moved to the Capitoline Hill in 1538.</note> as one of the primary wonders of the city.

N ii

### A PASSAGE IN PLAUTUS’S *THE GHOST STORY*, AND LIKEWISE ANOTHER IN *THE SWAGGERING SOLDIER*, RESCUED FROM ERROR.
### CHAPTER LXVI.

<margin>A passage of Plautus.</margin> In Plautus’s *The Ghost Story* <note>Latin: *Mostellaria* (OCR: *Mustelaria*), a comedy by Titus Maccius Plautus written around 200 BC.</note>, there is a passage written erroneously in most manuscripts in exactly this way: "Do you see the decorations on the doors? I see them. Look with what skill they sleep. They sleep. Indeed, he meant to say as is fitting."

But since no sense can be drawn from these words, and a witty remark by Plautus is lost through the fault of the scribes, I shall do something worthwhile, I believe, if I restore the original writing from a Plautine manuscript I have consulted. It is exactly this: "Do you see the joints <note>original: *coagmenta*. These are the seams or points where the wood panels of the door are joined together.</note> in the doors? I see them. Look with what skill they sleep. They sleep. I meant to say that they blink <note>original: *conniuent*. This is the "witty" correction; *conniuere* means both to "blink/close the eyes" and for things to "fit closely together" or "close up" without a gap.</note>." 

<margin>To blink / To fit together</margin> For those who are drowsy are said to "blink" or "close their eyes" [conniuere], as a line read in Cinna <note>Gaius Helvius Cinna, a Roman neoteric poet of the 1st century BC and friend of Catullus.</note> says:
"Now the heavy pupil begins to blink in profound sleep." <note>original: *Iam grauis ingenti conniuere pupula somno*.</note>
Therefore, when the slave Tranio shows that the joints—for that is what <margin>Joints</margin> *coagmenta* are—in the doors are not at all loose, he says jokingly first that they "sleep," and then that they even "blink," drawing a metaphor from the eyes of those falling asleep. 

Similarly, <margin>Another passage of Plautus.</margin> I believe another passage from Plautus’s *The Swaggering Soldier* <note>Latin: *Miles Gloriosus*.</note> should be corrected, which is currently read erroneously as: "It is a wonder that you live on oil with such cheap wheat. Why now? Because you are dim-sighted. By Pollux, you scoundrel, you are indeed blind, not just dim-sighted." For since no [sense can be] eli[citated]...

<summary>This page features a philological correction of two plays by Plautus, restoring a pun in The Ghost Story regarding the tight joints of a door "sleeping" and "blinking."</summary>

[Marginal note: Fulgentius.] Therefore, when the slave Sceledrus wants to suggest that his fellow slave Palaestrio is dim-sighted and has impaired eyes, he says it is a wonder he lives on darnel, since wheat itself is so cheap. ...may be corrupted, faulty, and rashly written through the ignorance of scribes. But you may find the subject matter of it <note>Referring to the story of the *Coma Berenices* or "Lock of Berenice," a lock of hair belonging to the Egyptian queen Berenice II that was said to have become a constellation.</note> partly in Hyginus <note>Gaius Julius Hyginus (c. 64 BC – AD 17), a Latin author and mythographer.</note>, a Latin author, and partly also in Theon and even Achilles, the Greek commentators on Aratus <note>Aratus was a Greek didactic poet known for his work *Phaenomena* on astronomy.</note>. Yet, in Theon, the interpreter of that same Aratus, I find its beginning to be thus: 

"Either Conon saw me, the lock of Berenice, in the air, or she herself dedicated me to all the gods." <note>original: "ἤ με κόνων ἔβλεψεν ἐν ἠέρι τὸν βερενίκης βόστρυχον, ἤ κείνη πᾶσιν ἔθηκε θεοῖς." Conon of Samos was the court astronomer who "discovered" the constellation.</note>

Furthermore, in the commentaries on Apollonius Rhodius <note>Author of the *Argonautica*.</note> [Marginal note: Commentaries on Apollonius.] collected from Lucillus of Tarrha, Sophocleus, and Theon, where the Chalybes <note>An ancient people of the Black Sea region, legendary as the first smiths and iron-workers.</note> are discussed in the second book, it is to be found thus: 

"Callimachus also mentions them, praying that the race of the Chalybes might perish, who brought to light that evil plant [iron] rising from afar." <note>original: "μέμνηται αὐτῶν καὶ καλλίμαχος χαλύβων ὡς ἀπόλοιτο γένος τηλόθεν ἀντέλλοντα κακὸν φυτὸν οἳ μιν ἔφηναν."</note> 

From this, who would doubt that the verse should be read thus: "Jupiter, let the whole race of the Chalybes perish," so that it is "Chalybon" <note>Poliziano is arguing for the Greek genitive plural *Chalybon* in the Latin text of Catullus 66.48.</note> in the Greek form. And some ignorantly read this as "of weapons" <note>original: *Telorum*.</note>. The most common manuscripts retain "of the heavenly ones" <note>original: *celitum*.</note>, even against the logic of the meter. But I know that this observation of ours has long since been made public; however, that it originated from us, even he who published it knows, and that most learned man from everywhere, Pico <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), the famous Renaissance philosopher and close friend of Poliziano.</note>, also freely admits it.

B O O K
THAT OARION IS THE GENUINE FORM
in Catullus, whereas those who violate good books read it as *Aorion*.

CHAPTER LXIX.

<margin>A passage of Catullus.</margin>
I N that same elegy of Catullus <note>Referring to Poem 66, "The Lock of Berenice," which Catullus translated into Latin from the Greek of Callimachus.</note> translated from Callimachus, the name *Oarion* is read in place of what would be *Orion*. Since certain people are now rashly beginning to attack this phrasing—which remains, until now, intact and unviolated—I must stand against this sinister audacity of the unlearned with every foot (as the saying goes) <note>Poliziano uses the idiom "omni pede," meaning with all one's might or from every angle.</note>. I do so by the authority of Callimachus himself, who speaks thus in his own hymn *To Artemis* [Diana], which is still extant:

"For neither Otus nor indeed Oarion wooed a happy marriage." <note>original Greek: "οὐδὲ γὰρ ὦτος οὐδὲ μὴν ὠαρίων ἀγαθὸν γάμον ἐμνήστευσαν." Callimachus refers here to giants who met tragic ends; Orion was famously killed by Artemis or a scorpion.</note>

But also Nicander <note>Nicander of Colophon (2nd century BC), a Greek poet and physician.</note>, in his book *On Venomous Animals* [Theriaca], speaks similarly:

"...meeting an evil fate for Boeotian Oarion." <note>original Greek: "βοιωτῷ τύχουσα κακὸν μόρον ὠαρίωνι."</note>

And Pindar <note>A celebrated Greek lyric poet from Thebes (c. 518–438 BC).</note> in the *Isthmian Odes*:

"For he did not receive a nature like that of Oarion." <note>original Greek: "οὐ γὰρ φύσιν ὠαριωνείαν ἔλαχεν."</note> And elsewhere:

"...that mountain Oarion travels not far from the Pleiades." <note>original Greek: "ὀρείαν πελειάδων μὴ τηλόθι ὠαρίωνα νεῖσθαι." The constellation Orion appears to "chase" the Pleiades across the night sky.</note>

For which reason Eustathius <note>Eustathius of Thessalonica (c. 1115–1195 AD), a Byzantine scholar and archbishop famous for his commentaries on Homer.</note>, in his fifth commentary on the *Odyssey*, thinks he was named from the word *to converse* or *to dally*. <note>original Greek: "παρὰ τὸ ὀαρίζειν" (para to oarizein). This is a folk etymology suggesting the name Orion/Oarion comes from the Greek word for intimate conversation or dalliance.</note> Therefore, the true reading is not *Aorion*, but *Oarion*.

->OF WHAT THE CAP AND DAGGERS ON THE COIN OF BRUTUS ARE THE SYMBOL.<-

### CHAPTER LXX.

<margin>The image of Brutus marked with a cap and two small daggers.</margin>
I f anyone should find a coin of Brutus <note>Marcus Junius Brutus (85–42 BC), the most famous of Julius Caesar’s assassins.</note> engraved with his own image, and furthermore with a cap and two small daggers, let him no longer labor in explaining the reasoning and the cause, but let him read the forty-seventh book of the *Histories* of Dio <note>Cassius Dio (c. 155–235 AD), a Roman historian who wrote a massive history of Rome in Greek.</note>. For from these things, he says, Brutus signified that the fatherland had been liberated by himself and Cassius. But indeed, the fact that a cap <note>The *pileus*, a brimless felt cap given to Roman slaves upon their manumission to signify their new status as freedmen.</note> was customarily worn by those who had attained liberty is more common and manifest than can be confirmed by citing authorities.

This reading is proven to be truer by the fact that the first syllable of <margin>Electrum</margin> **electrum** <note>original: "electrum"; a natural alloy of gold and silver or a pale yellow amber. Poliziano is arguing that the syllable length in "liquidóue potestur electro" requires specific grammatical forms to be valid.</note> is long everywhere, among both the Greeks and ourselves. While it sounded strange to Renaissance ears, Poliziano defends its use by citing ancient precedents.</note> being otherwise new or unusual. Yet it does not escape me that the ancients also brought this form forth, such as Pacuvius <note>Marcus Pacuvius (c. 220–130 BC), a celebrated early Roman tragic poet.</note> in his *Chryses*, and even Lucretius; likewise, the form *poteratur* was read by Nonius Marcellus <note>A 4th-century Roman grammarian whose work preserved many fragments of lost early Latin literature.</note> in the annals of Caecilius <note>Statius Caecilius (c. 220–166 BC), a Roman comic poet whose works survive only in fragments.</note>. The force of the meter compels this; otherwise, the first syllable of <margin>Evander</margin> **Evander** would be short, contrary to all reason or custom. Indeed, there is a spondee <note>A metrical foot consisting of two long syllables (--). Poliziano is explaining why the rhythm of the verse requires a specific pronunciation.</note> in the fifth position in both verses. In the word *petit*, the latter syllable is rightly lengthened, either because it is at the end of the word or because it has merged from two syllables into one. Martianus Capella <note>A 5th-century author whose work "The Marriage of Philology and Mercury" was a standard textbook for the Seven Liberal Arts in the Middle Ages.</note> teaches that <margin>A syllable ending in T is common.</margin> any syllable ending in the letter **T** is "common" <note>In Latin prosody, a "common" (anceps) syllable can be treated as either long or short by the poet to fit the requirements of the meter.</note>, just as we see in that line of Virgil:

->“For the Evandrian sword took your head, oh Thymber.”<-
<note>original Latin: "Nam tibi Thymbre caput Euandrius abstulit ensis." (Aeneid 10.394).</note>

### MISCELLANIES. 67
### ON THE LINDEN-BARK AND
### ribboned crowns.
### CHAPTER 72.

I observe that many people do not know what **philyra** <margin>Philyra.</margin> <note>original: "philyra"; the inner bark (bast) of the lime or linden tree, used in antiquity as a writing material or for making ribbons for garlands.</note> is, and that it is even understood incorrectly by several interpreters. For Horace uses that word in the first book of his *Odes*:

->“Crowns woven with linden-bark displease me.”<- <margin>Horace, book 1.</margin>
<note>original: "Displicent nexæ philyra coronæ" (Odes 1.38.2).</note>

And likewise Ovid in the fifth book of the *Fasti*:

->“The drunken guest, his hair bound with linden-bark, dances.”<-
<note>original: "Ebrius incinctus philyra conuiua capillos Saltat" (Fasti 5.337).</note>

From these examples, it is clear that the use of this material in garlands was very common among the ancients. Now, first of all, if you compare what Theophrastus <note>Theophrastus (c. 371–287 BC), a Greek philosopher and the successor to Aristotle, often called the "father of botany."</note> writes about the *philyra* in the third book of his *On Plants*, and what Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), author of the "Natural History" (*Naturalis Historia*), an encyclopedic work covering all known knowledge of the natural world.</note> narrates in the sixteenth book of his *Natural History*, you will undoubtedly recognize easily that the tree we call the <margin>Tilia.</margin> **linden** <note>original: "tilia"; the lime or linden tree.</note> is the same as the *philyra* among the Greeks. And even Theodorus Gaza <note>Theodorus Gaza (c. 1398–1475), a Greek humanist and translator whose Latin versions of Theophrastus were highly influential in the Renaissance.</note>, a man as learned as he was diligent, translates it this way. Furthermore, certain thin membranes <margin>Lemnisci.</margin> <term>lemnisci</term> <note>From the Greek "lemniskos," these were ribbons or bandages made of linden-bark that hung down from decorative crowns or garlands as a mark of high honor.</note>—that is, little bands hanging from crowns—or even small books, were specifically called **philyrae** in the Greek tongue, being made from the inner bark of the linden tree itself or crafted in imitation of it. Pliny testifies to a portion of these things in the same book with these lines:

->“Between the bark and the wood are thin membranes, consisting of many layers, from which linden-wood bindings are drawn out; the thinnest of these [are the philyrae]...”<-
<note>original: "Inter corticẽ & lignũ tenues tunicas, multiplici membrana, è qbus uincula tiliæ euocãtur, tenuissimæ earũ..." (Pliny, *Naturalis Historia* 16.25).</note>

...of linden-bark, famous for the ribbons of the crowns, held in honor by the ancients. For a long time they were plain; P. Claudius Pulcher was the first to establish their engraving <margin>To engrave.</margin>, and he even gave gold leaf <note>original: "bracteas"; thin foils of precious metal.</note> to the linden-bark. Furthermore, we repeatedly see ribboned crowns <margin>Ribbons.</margin> <margin>Ribboned crowns.</margin> (if I am not mistaken) on the medals of Lorenzo de' Medici <note>Lorenzo "the Magnificent" (1449–1492), the Florentine statesman and Poliziano’s patron.</note>; and mention is also made of these in Suetonius, just as the word for "ribbons" <note>original: "lemniscorum"; refers to the ribbons hanging from a crown of honor.</note> is diligently explained by Pompeius Festus, and likewise Tertullian <note>An early Christian author (c. 155–220 AD).</note> says something about ribbons in his book *On the Soldier's Crown*. 

But Nicas <margin>Nicas's commentaries.</margin> <note>Likely referring to a Greek grammarian or lexicographer.</note>, in the commentary which he composed in alphabetical order—written in Greek, but interpreted roughly in this sense—says: "**Philyra** <margin>Linden-bark.</margin>, is a plant having an inner bark similar to papyrus, from which they even weave ropes." Moreover, the same Tertullian hints that linden-bark is flexible like wool-work, or rather spinnable, in that little book titled *On the Cloak* <note>original: "de pallio".</note>. 

Indeed, the Greek author Herodian—whom I once translated into Latin and dedicated to Pope Innocent VIII—mentions in the first book of his history a small book made of the thinnest linden-bark, which could be folded in both directions. But even Martianus Capella <note>A Latin author of late antiquity, known for his encyclopedic work on the liberal arts.</note>, in his book on the Marriage of Philology...

MISCELLANEOUS WORKS. 88

...In the second book of *The Marriage [of Philology and Mercury]* <note>A famous 5th-century allegorical work by Martianus Capella that served as a textbook for the liberal arts.</note>, he says: "One could see what books, and what great volumes, the works of how many languages flowed from the virgin’s mouth." Some appeared to be made of papyrus, which had been smeared with cedar oil <note>Cedar oil was applied to ancient scrolls to protect them from insects and decay.</note>. Other books were wrapped in linen scrolls <note>original: "carbasinis"; referring to fine flax or linen.</note>; many also were made from sheep's hides <note>Early parchment or vellum.</note>. A few, however, were noted down upon the bark of the linden-tree <note>original: "philyræ cortice".</note>. Then you may also find these words of Ulpian <note>A prominent Roman jurist (c. 170–223 AD) whose writings comprise about a third of the *Digest*.</note> in the title of the *Digests* concerning legacies and trusts: "Under the designation of 'books' are included all volumes, whether they are on papyrus <note>original: "charta"</note> or on parchment, or on any other material. But even if they are on linden-bark, or the linden-tree <note>original: "philyra, aut tilia"</note>, as some construct them, or on some other skin, the same must be said." Finally, Eustathius <note>The 12th-century Archbishop of Thessalonica, known for his vast commentaries on Homer.</note> also, in his second commentary on Homer’s *Odyssey*, having first made mention of nautical ropes, says that the bark of the linden—which he calls *philyra* in Greek—provides the most material for that kind of rope. It seems to have been accepted from this that they call individual skins, or layers, or even small membranes of a material different from the linden-tree "philyrae" or "philurae" <margin>Philurae.</margin>, with the Greek *y* changed to *u* according to the ancient and common custom of the Latins. Just as Pliny, dealing with papyrus in his thirteenth book, says: "From it are prepared papers, divided with a needle into very thin but as wide as possible *philurae* <note>referring to the thin strips of the papyrus plant used to make sheets.</note>." For that other point certainly does not pertain to this explanation, which the same Pliny mentions in his fourteenth book: that linden-bark <margin>Salt removal.</margin> <note>original: "Philyra"</note> used by cooks, along with fine flour, removes excessive salt from foods. <margin>Example of salt.</margin>

THE WORD *EXPERNATA* <note>original: "expernata"; a rare Latin word meaning "spurned" or "rejected."</note> should be restored to the copies of Catullus <note>Gaius Valerius Catullus (c. 84–54 BC), a famous Roman lyric poet.</note> according to these examples.

### CHAPTER LXXIII.

What we are now about to relate will perhaps be ridiculed and hissed at by some as uncertain, wavering, and as yet insufficiently explored. But I thought I should speak of it, lest this trace of antiquity, already nearly destroyed, might be extinguished, whatever its nature may be. Four years ago in Rome, Manilius Rallus <note>A Greek scholar and poet who lived in Rome during the late 15th century, known for his expertise in both Greek and Latin.</note>—a man of Greek origin but exceptionally well-versed in Latin letters—showed me a certain fragment of Sextus Pompeius Festus <note>A 2nd-century AD grammarian whose work "On the Significance of Words" is a vital source for ancient Roman culture, though it survived mostly in a later, shortened version.</note> (for so it was titled). <margin>The work of Festus Pompeius.</margin> It was certainly quite ancient, but mostly mutilated and gnawed away by mice. This deeply held my interest, since those remains, such as they were, survived from the original volume itself which the author Festus had composed, rather than from this abridged summary <note>A reference to the 8th-century epitome by Paul the Deacon, which shortened Festus’s massive original work, losing much valuable detail in the process.</note> which we now have in hand—constrained, forced, and cut short, undoubtedly by some ignoble and unlearned person, who on that account deserves little credit from the world of letters. <margin>Pomponius Laetus.</margin> Pomponius Laetus <note>Julius Pomponius Laetus (1428–1498), a leading humanist who founded the Roman Academy and was obsessed with rediscovering the physical and literary remains of ancient Rome.</note>, a man most expert in antiquity and good literature, had also kept for himself some pages from the same fragment, which likewise he [gave] to me to be read and transcribed...

MISCELLANY, PAGE 89

...and gave <note>This likely completes a thought from the previous page regarding the "unlearned person" who abridged Festus, perhaps saying they "gave little care" or "gave limited value" to the work.</note>. Therefore, from that reading, I certainly suspected that I might restore a word of Catullus <note>Gaius Valerius Catullus (c. 84–54 BC), a Roman poet whose work often suffered from textual corruption in medieval manuscripts.</note> to its proper place, as if returning it from exile. <margin>A passage in Catullus.</margin> For indeed, where the word *Supernati* <note>original: "Supernati"; a term discussed by ancient grammarians, here linked to the cutting of legs or "hams."</note> is explained in this compendium, <margin>Supernati.</margin> it shows that it signifies those whose legs have been cut off in the manner of swine's hams; and a verse from Ennius <note>Quintus Ennius (c. 239–169 BC), the "father of Roman poetry."</note> is cited:
->The unjust pride of the Carthaginian cut the hams of these men.<- <note>original: "His pernas succidit iniqua superbia Poeni." A line from Ennius’s *Annales* referring to the Carthaginian wars.</note>
In this very place, the old codex has an exposition that is clearly gnawed all around by mice; however, it is such that it appears from the poorly clinging remains of the letters that Catullus is also brought in after Ennius, and that verse is cited which is now faultily read as follows:
->In the ditch of Ligurius he lies, separated by an axe.<- <note>original: "In fossa Ligurii iacet separata securi." This is the "corrupt" version of Catullus, poem 67, line 32.</note>
Now, while I would not rashly claim anything for myself regarding the rest of the poem, I certainly seemed—by sniffing out each syllable one by one—to be able to offer as a proven fact that *expernata* <note>original: "expernata"; a rare word meaning "hamstrung" or "deprived of legs," derived from "perna" (ham/leg).</note> should be read in it, <margin>Expernata</margin> and not *separata* ("separated"). This is as much as I could gather from those few little scraps and shavings, and as much as I could divine even from the nearly obliterated traces. But if the reliability of the truth seems to anyone to still hang in the balance, I would truly prefer it to hang there than to either fall or lie flat. We certainly place some firm foundation in this discovery, and I do not think we do so unjustly.

# BOOK
## A NOT UNPLEASANT STORY
### regarding the painter Zeuxis and Helen.
### CHAPTER LXXIIII.

<margin>Zeuxis. Image of Helen.</margin>
Zeuxis, that most noble painter from Heraclea <note>original: "Heracleotes"; Zeuxis of Heraclea (5th century BC) was one of the most famous painters of ancient Greece, known for his realism.</note>, painted an image of Helen for the people of Croton <note>original: "Crotoniatibus"; Croton was a Greek colony in Southern Italy. According to legend, Zeuxis chose the five most beautiful maidens of the city to serve as models for this composite portrait of ideal beauty.</note> that was praised beyond measure. Cicero wrote most brilliantly about this in his works on rhetoric against Hermagoras <note>original: "in rhetoricis contra Hermagorā"; a reference to Cicero's *De Inventione*, where the story of the painting is used to discuss the concept of ideal beauty.</note>. But what we read in Rome in recent years in the books of Aelian’s *Miscellany* <note>original: "Hæliani libris omnifariæ historiæ"; refers to Claudius Aelianus’s *Varia Historia*, a 2nd-century collection of anecdotes.</note>—while visiting the home of Giovanni Lorenzo of Venice, <margin>Giovanni Lorenzo of Venice.</margin> a man of great authority in the papal court, of no less erudition in literature, and a man of sharp judgment <note>original: "emunctæ naris"; literally "of a wiped nose," a Latin idiom for someone who is discerning, witty, or difficult to fool.</note>—seemed to us by no means unworthy of being read here as well. Aelian says that Zeuxis admitted no one to see the painting unless they had first paid a fee. Because of this, even in those days the painting was commonly nicknamed "Helen the Harlot," <margin>Why Helen was called a harlot.</margin> because she was "for profit" <note>original: "quæstuaria"; a term used for someone earning a living through trade or, more specifically, prostitution. The joke is that Helen was "selling herself" to anyone with a coin.</note>.

MISCELLANIES. 90

...from Aelian, Strabo, Apollonius, and other Greeks, and also from the Latins. <margin>Domitius</margin> In that commentary, he concocts many vain and ridiculous things, and devises them on the spot <note>original: "ex tempore"</note> for his own convenience. By these things, he proves either that he has utterly lost his sense of shame <note>original: "frontem"; literally "forehead" or "brow," which in Latin was considered the seat of shame or modesty.</note>, or that there was such a vast gap between his brow and his tongue (as a certain person says) that the brow could not restrain the tongue. <margin>A gap between the brow and the tongue.</margin> It is sheer madness that some admirers of that man are still found so blind that they do not yet open their eyes, such as they are, to these matters. But what can you do with those masters of "lettering" <term>literatio</term> rather than "literature" <term>literatura</term>, <margin>Masters of lettering.</margin> <note>The author distinguishes between *literatio*—the basic, mechanical knowledge of grammar—and *literatura*—the profound study of high literature and culture.</note> who raise their eyebrows at nothing more than such trifles, like the horns of a snail? Their wits are utterly leaden; the more they are polished, the more they become dull. However, so that we do not entirely waste our labor, having ignored those who deserve to be held in contempt for their own self-interest, let us pursue the rest of our purpose. For me, that one passage among the rest usually turns my stomach, which is currently read in such a corrupted way:

->*And as talkative Agenor was crushed in the throat by his horse,*<-

Regarding this, Domitius says: "Agenor was killed when his hand was thrust into his mouth during a fall from a horse." Is it really so, O most learned man? But I beg of you, my dear Domitius, produce a Greek source if you can, or even a Latin author suitable for this story of yours, and I will no longer refuse...

->BOOK<-

<margin>A passage in Ovid’s *In Ibin*. Anticlus. Helen’s attempts.</margin>
Indeed, I would not say that merely the "Greek-lings" <note>original: *Graecales*; a pejorative term for those who affect Greek style or superficial Hellenism without deep knowledge.</note>, or your admirers, but even literate men, for whom this labor is exhausting, would condemn me now as most unlearned and even most impudent—and indeed by a unanimous vote—because I disagree with you. To me, the writing seems truly correct and emended if there is a free conjecture based on reason: that we should read not "Agenor," but "made of maple" <note>original: *acerno*. The author is proposing a textual emendation, suggesting a scribe mistook the adjective describing the wooden horse's material for a proper name.</note>, so that we may understand the passage as referring to Anticlus. 

Regarding him, there is also an account in the fourth book of Homer’s *Odyssey*. For when Helen had approached that Trojan horse—which was woven of beams and pregnant with soldiers by the impulse of a deity—she began to imitate the voices of each of their wives, so that she might evoke the longings of those hiding within, rub their memories raw, and attempt to draw out responses. But while the others maintained patience and silence, when Anticlus alone was on the slippery edge of responding, at the very point of speaking, he was immediately prevented by Ulysses and suffocated by his pressed hands. Thus, by the interception—or rather the obstruction—of the evidence, the danger was averted. 

Furthermore, Tryphiodorus, a Greek author, also calls this Anticlus the "son of Ortygius" in his poem written about the capture of Troy, and he names his wife <margin>Laodamia.</margin> Laodamia, saying:
"And there the Achaeans, weeping, buried Anticlus, son of Ortygius, who had died right there within the horse." <note>original: ὀρτυγίδης δ’ ἄντικλος ὅμ’ αὐτόθι τεθνεῶτα / ἵππω δακρύσαντες ἐνεκτερέϊξαμ ἀχαιοί.</note>
And likewise elsewhere:
"But when Anticlus received the sting of Laodamia’s voice, he alone, opening his mouth, was beginning to utter a reply." <note>original: ἄντικλος δ’ ὅτε κέντρομ ἐλέξατο λαοδαμείης / μῦνος ἀμοιβαίην ἀνεβάλλετο γῆρυμ ἀνοίξας.</note>

## NOT ANTILOCHUS BY HECTOR
## but that it seems it should be read otherwise in the first epistle of the *Heroides*. <term>Heroides: A collection of poetic letters written by Ovid in the voices of mythological heroines.</term>

### CHAPTER LXXVI.

[Large initial I] N the first epistle of Naso’s <note>Ovid's full name was Publius Ovidius Naso.</note> *Heroides*, it is read thus: <margin>A passage in Ovid’s *Heroides*.</margin>
*Or if someone told that Antilochus was conquered by Hector.*

But that Antilochus was conquered and killed by Hector will certainly not be found anywhere in the ancient records. Therefore, I would believe either that "Amphimachus" should be read, or that it was not "by Hector," but by Memnon. 

For it is established from the thirteenth rhapsody <term>rhapsody: A term used by scholars to refer to the individual books of Homer’s *Iliad* or *Odyssey*.</term> of Homer, <margin>The slaughter of Amphimachus.</margin> 
that Amphimachus, son of Cteatus, was killed by Hector; he is even numbered among the Greek leaders in the Catalogue of Ships. It is likewise established <margin>The slaughter of Antilochus.</margin> 
that Antilochus, the son of Nestor, was killed by Memnon <note>Memnon was an Ethiopian king who came to the aid of Troy; his duel with Antilochus and subsequent death at the hands of Achilles was a famous episode in the lost epic *Aethiopis*.</note>. Wherefore, so that in that which had held a flaw until now...

# BY WHAT ARGUMENT IT SHOULD BE CALLED
## Vergilius, not Virgilius. Chapter LXXVII.

<margin>Vergilius.</margin> Whether it should be called *Vergilius*, or *Virgilius*—as people now commonly say, that is, with the second vowel <note>The letter 'e', following the alphabetical order A, E, I, O, U.</note> or rather the third <note>The letter 'i'.</note> in the first syllable—I see is still a matter of dispute among the learned. But as for why I say *Vergilius* rather with an *e* (which has now, through our efforts, begun to please certain learned men) than *Virgilius* with an *i* (which has become far too prevalent in common usage), the reason is several very ancient monuments <note>In this context, *monumenta* refers to physical evidence such as stone inscriptions or extremely old manuscripts.</note> that support my observation. I shall gladly put these forward on account of certain shameful men, I know not who, full of rust <margin>Shameful men.</margin> <note>original: *eruginis*; literally "copper rust," but used as a classical metaphor for the "canker" of envy or spite.</note>, most worthy of all hatred and loathing, who, although they are always mere beginners <note>original: *elementarii*; a term for those still at the level of learning their basic letters or first principles.</note>, claim <margin>Rust/Envy.</margin> <unclear>vindic...</unclear>

<summary>This page marks the beginning of a philological defense for the spelling "Vergilius" over the common "Virgilius," citing ancient inscriptions and manuscripts as evidence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s defense of his scholarly focus on orthography and "minor" details before moving into specific archaeological evidence—a marble inscription at Bolsena—to support the spelling of "Vergilius."</meta>

->MISCELLANIES. 92<-

we spend effort on things so frivolous and slight, and as if we were spending time—that most precious thing—on superfluities. Yet if those people had read that sometimes the greatest men composed entire volumes about single letters, and that not even Caesar himself feared to descend into such minutiae <note>original: *tenuitatem*; literally "thinness" or "slenderness," referring here to the fine details of grammatical study.</note>, they would perhaps grant me pardon—a man not overly busy—if sometimes, amidst higher studies, <margin>The illustration of matters concerning letters.</margin> I also treated matters of this kind; small indeed, but by which even great things are aided. Nor do I think I am justly blamed if I pursue these things too, but only as a diversion; I turn to them not as one going from leisure to study, but from study to leisure, and I do not so much pursue them professionally as stumble upon them by chance, just as if, walking comfortably along the shores, I were to gather shells meanwhile without a care. Yet I do not raise my eyebrow <note>An ancient metaphor for arrogance or dismissiveness.</note> at this, nor would I easily allow myself to be judged by it, as one who knows and confesses that these things are not only "Apinae and tricae" <margin>Apinae.</margin> <note>original: *Apinas et tricas*; a proverbial Latin expression for worthless trifles, named after two insignificant towns in Apulia.</note>, as the playful saying goes, but even almost nothing. <margin>Tricae.</margin> But yet, it is better to do this very "nothing" than to draw out long yawns after their own example, perhaps, and to grunt shamefully in the mud, or than to do nothing at all; lest by doing absolutely nothing, we learn to do ill. Therefore, we must provide the evidence, so that our credibility does not limp anywhere, and so that nothing is unavailable for those who wish to know. You will find, therefore, at Bolsena <note>original: *Volsinis*; the ancient Etruscan and Roman city of Volsinii, modern Bolsena.</note> a certain marble tablet, with very ancient and almost faded char[acters]—

O iij <note>This is a signature mark used by early printers to help binders assemble the book in the correct order.</note>

# **BOOK**

characters <note>The start of the word "characters" (characteribus) was likely split from the previous page.</note> inside the church of the virgin Christina <note>The Basilica of Santa Cristina in Bolsena.</note>, which serves as the altar of the Apostle Peter, where **VERGILI** is read. You will also find this name **Vergilius** noted in the same way at Sutri on a stone table, which is also used within the church of the virgin in place of an altar. I inspected both of these closely, not without several witnesses; for I desired to be not merely an "ear-witness" <note>original: *auriti testes*; those who have only heard reports.</note> to ancient inspections, but an "eye-witness" <note>original: *oculati*; those who have seen with their own eyes.</note> as well. Indeed, even in those Pandects <note>The *Digest* or *Pandects*, a massive compilation of Roman law commissioned by Emperor Justinian I in the 6th century. The Florentine manuscript is one of the most famous legal codices in the world.</note> which are now publicly preserved at Florence—the very archetype volume of Prince Justinian—the name is recorded with nothing other than an **e**. ### A NEGATION FALSELY ADDED
### in the first volume of the Digests, in the chapter concerning the office of the proconsul and the legate. Chapter LXXVIII.

<margin>Location in the Digests.</margin>
**P**aulus the jurist <note>Julius Paulus (3rd century AD), one of the most influential Roman legal scholars, whose writings make up a large portion of the Pandects.</note>, in the second book *On the Edict*, writes as follows: "A legate to whom jurisdiction has been delegated has the right to appoint a judge." <note>original: *Legatus mandata sibi iurisdictione iudicis dandi ius habet.*</note> But you will find these words in the first volume of the *Digests* <note>The *Digest*, also known as the *Pandects*, is a massive compendium of juristic writings compiled by order of Emperor Justinian I in the 6th century AD.</note>, in the chapter where the office of the proconsul and the legate is treated, with a negation wrongly added in most manuscripts, which reads: "A legate... does *not* have the right to appoint a judge." However, in those <margin>The Archetypal Pandects.</margin> Florentine Pandects <note>The *Littera Florentina*, a famous 6th-century manuscript of Justinian’s Code discovered in the Middle Ages; it is considered the most authoritative surviving copy of the text.</note>, which we also believe to be the original source, there is no negation at all. Thus it happened that Accursius <note>Accursius (c. 1182–1263), a celebrated legal scholar whose "Great Gloss" became the standard interpretation of Roman law for centuries.</note>, the Florentine interpreter of the laws, having himself obtained a faulty manuscript, tormented himself—I might almost say miserably—trying to reconcile the error.

**WHY THE FACE OF KING LYSIMACHUS**

[Large initial L] The face of King Lysimachus <note>Lysimachus (c. 360–281 BC) was a Macedonian general and one of the "Successors" of Alexander the Great who became King of Thrace, Asia Minor, and Macedon.</note> on coins is <margin>**Why the face of Lysimachus is horned.**</margin> horned. For when Pico demands something of me, it is not permitted for me to be ignorant of what I do not know, nor to be unable to do what I cannot; this is either because I feel I am most dear to the man, or because I am convinced that he sees more in my own affairs than I do myself.

Children, once in Thebes, Athena loved one nymph beyond all others—the mother of Tiresias—and she was her dearest companion. They were never apart. Even when the goddess drove her horses toward ancient Thespiae, or toward Coroneia, or to Haliartus, passing through the tilled fields of Boeotia <note>The poet lists famous cult sites of Athena in the region of Boeotia, establishing the geographic setting of the myth.</note>—to Coroneia, where her fragrant grove and altars sit by the river Curalius—the goddess often placed the nymph beside her on her own chariot. ...at the Horse’s Spring <note>original: "Ιππω κρανᾶ" (Hippō kranā). This refers to Hippocrene on Mount Helicon, a spring famously created by the strike of the winged horse Pegasus’s hoof.</note> on Helicon, they were bathing their beautiful limbs. A midday stillness held the mountain. Both were bathing; it was the hour of noon, and a great silence possessed that hill.

Tiresias, still alone with his hounds—his beard just beginning to darken with the first down of youth <note>original: "αρτι γενειᾱ περκαζων" (arti geneia perkazōn). The poet uses a term often associated with ripening fruit to describe the transition from boyhood to manhood.</note>—was wandering through that sacred place. Driven by an unspeakable thirst, he approached the flowing waters of the fountain. Wretched youth! Unintentionally, he looked upon that which is not lawful to behold.

Then Athena, though she was filled with anger, addressed him: "O son of Everes, what divinity has led you on this bitter road? You shall never carry your eyes away from here again!"

So she spoke, and night <note>A poetic metaphor for the onset of blindness.</note> fell over the boy’s eyes. He stood there, struck silent, for pangs of grief gripped his knees and helplessness held his voice.

But the nymph <note>Chariclo, the mother of Tiresias.</note> cried out: "What have you done to my child, Lady? Is this the way...

...you goddesses are friends? You have stolen the eyes of my son!" <note>This concludes the lament of Chariclo, mother of Tiresias, who is distraught over her son's sudden blindness.</note>

Then the goddess Athena answered her: 
"Noble woman, take back all the words you have spoken in your anger. It was not I who made your child blind; Athena finds no sweetness in stealing the sight of children. But the laws of the gods <note>original: "Κρόνιοι νόμοι" (Kronioi nomoi). These are the ancient, primordial laws established by Cronus, the father of Zeus, which even the younger gods must obey without exception.</note> decree this: whoever looks upon an immortal when the god does not choose to be seen, that person shall pay a heavy price for the vision."

"Noble lady, the deed that is done cannot be called back. The thread of the Fates <note>The Moerae, or Fates, who spun the thread of every mortal's life at their birth, determining their destiny from the start.</note> spun this end for him from the moment he was born. But now, son of Everes, receive the payment due to you."

"Consider how many sacrifices the daughter of Cadmus <note>Autonoe, the mother of the hunter Actaeon.</note> shall burn in the future, and how many things Aristaeus shall pray for, just to see their only son, Actaeon, even if he were blind!"

->**How many things shall be burned together with Actaeon...**<-
<note>original: "Οσσα μὲν ἀκταίωνι συνέμπορα καύσεται" (Hossa men Aktaiōni synempora kausetai). This refers to the funeral pyre and the vast offerings that would be sacrificed upon the death of Actaeon. Athena uses this comparison to show Chariclo that while Tiresias is blind, he is still alive—unlike Actaeon, who suffered a much worse fate for a similar mistake.</note>

"For he shall be a companion to Artemis in the hunt, yet neither his running nor his archery shall save him when, though unwilling, he glimpses the lovely bath of that goddess. Then his own hounds shall feast upon their former master, and his mother shall go through the woods gathering his bones. She will call you the happiest of mothers, for you received your son back from the mountains only blind."

Four times he shall breathe his last <note>original: "Τετάκις εἰπνεύσαντι" (Tetakis eipneusanti). She shall call you blessed and say you are fortunate in your life, for having received such a child from the mountains.

"He shall understand the birds: which ones are auspicious, those that fly aimlessly, and those whose wings bring no good omen. He shall deliver many oracles <note>original: "θεοπροπα" (theopropa). This refers to the special grace given to Tiresias in the Underworld, where he retains his intellect while other ghosts are mindless shadows.</note>."

Come out now, come, whoever will wash the limbs
Of Pallas with clear waters, come out!
She herself comes; we hear the noise of the sacred horses;

Until the sweat, the foul drops, and all the foam
Departed from their mouths, where they champed the stiff, jagged bits <note>term: "lupata". A sharp horse-bit shaped like wolf's teeth, used for control.</note>.

“Unhappy one, you have seen the secret parts of Pallas; but you shall not see the sun again. Alas, miserable me! O mountain, O Helicon, never to be traversed by me again. Thus, embracing her dear boy with both arms, the mother repeats the song of the mournful nightingales <note>original: "Aedonidon"; referring to the myth of Aedon (often identified with Procne), who was turned into a nightingale and forever mourned her son. It suggests a high-pitched, tragic wailing.</note>, groaning sadly. But indeed, Minerva, having taken pity, then addressed her companion with these words:

“Noble woman,” she said, “take back these words which your anger has brought forth. The boy was not deprived of his sight by me, for it is not sweet to Minerva to take away the eyes of children; rather, these things are ordained by the laws of Saturn <note>Saturn (the Greek Cronus) represents the ancient, unchangeable laws of the universe that even the Olympian gods must obey; here it refers to the decree that mortals may not look upon gods without permission.</note>, that whoever looks upon any of the immortals, unless the god himself chooses it, must look upon them at a great price indeed.

Therefore, noble woman, this thing cannot be undone, for so the threads were spun by the hand of the Fates <note>The Parcae, the three goddesses who spin, measure, and cut the thread of human life at the moment of birth.</note> from the moment the boy was first born; but receive in return <note>Text continues from the previous page's fragment.</note> a gift of great magnitude. For I shall make him a seer <term>seer: a prophet or someone who can see the future or the will of the gods</term>, celebrated by men of the future, surpassing by far all others in the knowledge of things to come.

He shall understand the flights of birds—knowing which are favorable and which fly to no purpose—and he shall discern their many omens. He shall deliver many prophecies to the people of Thebes <note>original: "Thebaeis"</note>, many to Cadmus <note>The legendary founder of Thebes.</note>, and in later days to the renowned descendants of Labdacus <note>The "Labdacids," the royal house of Thebes that included Oedipus.</note>. I will also bestow upon him a sturdy staff to guide his feet, and I will grant him a great length of life. Even when he passes to the world below, he alone among the dead shall retain his wisdom; his spirit shall move through the underworld endowed with reason, honored by the great Sovereign of the Dead <note>A reference to Hades; Tiresias famously retains his prophetic powers in the afterlife, as seen in Homer’s Odyssey.</note>.”

ON OCNUS AND THE LITTLE DONKEY, AND THAT WHICH IS ERRONEOUSLY READ AS "ORNO" IN PROPERTIUS.

CHAPTER LXXXI.

[Large initial A] In the same Propertius, in the fourth book of his Elegies <margin>A passage in Propertius.</margin>, two short verses are read in this way:
*He is more worthy who twists the rope by the slanted mountain-ash [orno],*
*And eternally feeds your hunger, little donkey.*
For a long time, I confess, a certain scruple <note>original: "scrupulus"; literally a small sharp stone, used here to mean a nagging doubt or intellectual irritation.</note> had pricked and goaded my mind, until an ancient manuscript plucked it out—one which Bernardino Valla <margin>Bern. Valla</margin>, a celebrated legal scholar and a man of the highest nobility in Rome, lent to me nearly five years ago. In that book, I read not *orno* [mountain-ash], but *ocno* [Ocnus]. I immediately showed what I had read to two very learned young men who had opportunely <margin>Opportunely.</margin> visited me at my home. It is clear, moreover, that Propertius is hinting at the subject of that Socratic painting <note>The author refers to a famous allegorical painting of Ocnus, which was often used to illustrate the philosophical concept of wasted effort.</note>, about which Pliny writes thus in the thirty-fifth book of his *Natural History*: "And the lazy man," he says...

P v <note>A printer's signature mark indicating the sequence of the gathered pages.</note>

<summary>This page concludes a hymn to Minerva and begins an investigation into a textual error in Propertius's poetry, where the author identifies "Ocnus" as the correct reading over "orno."</summary>
<keywords>Propertius, Ocnus, Bernardino Valla, philology, Latin poetry, Pliny the Elder, Argos, Minerva</keywords>

--- Page 260 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological discussion of a corrupted passage in the poet Propertius regarding Ocnus and then moves into a legal correction in the Digest of Justinian.</meta>

101 [space] BOOK <unclear>MISCELLANIES</unclear>

[margin] Ocnus twists the rope [/margin] ...who is called "Ocnus twisting the broom-fiber rope," which a little donkey
[margin] twists [/margin] gnaws away. But indeed, this lazy Ocnus (as I see), having suddenly become a wanderer and a jumper through the inducements of many, now changes his seat every day. Therefore, so that he may at last rest in a certain *siliquastrum* <note>original: "siliquastro"; a stool made of Judas-tree wood, often depicted as the seat of Ocnus in ancient art.</note>, faith must be sought especially from that manuscript which we cited—that is (to speak truly) from the very stock from which the occasion for this conjecture sprouted. For thus anyone will understand that I am by no means claiming for myself what belongs to others, but only what is mine, and that in my books nothing is more important than good faith.

-> THAT IN THE TITLE OF THE DIGEST *DE EDENDO* <note>original: "de ædendo"; the legal title regarding the production of documents or evidence in court.</note> THE READING "TO ANOTHER" SHOULD BE READ AS "TO THE WHITE BOARD." <- [A large letter 'A' is printed to the right]

### CHAPTER LXXXII.

[margin] Digest [location] [/margin] In the title *De Edendo* [On Production], which is read in the second volume of the *Digest* <note>The Digest, or Pandects, is a massive compilation of Roman legal opinions commissioned by the Emperor Justinian in the 6th century.</note>, the words of Ulpian <note>Domitius Ulpianus, a celebrated Roman jurist whose writings make up a large portion of the Digest.</note> in the fourth book *Ad Edictum* [On the Edict] are as follows: "Labeo <note>Marcus Antistius Labeo, an influential Roman jurist of the Augustan age.</note> says that he also 'produces' who leads his adversary to the *album* [the white board] and shows him what he intends to declare." For thus it is written in the Florentine Pandects <note>The *Littera Florentina*, a 6th-century manuscript preserved in Florence, considered the most authoritative source for the Digest.</note> themselves. And yet all the manuscripts commonly in use have not "to the *album*" [white board], but "to *alium*" [another person]. From this, of course, arose much hesitation for Accursius <note>Accursius was a 13th-century jurist famous for his "Great Gloss," the standard commentary on Roman law which often struggled with corrupt texts.</note>. You shall understand it, however, as referring to the *album* of the praetor <note>The *album praetoris* was the white-painted board where the Roman praetor posted legal notices and the rules of his court.</note>.

<summary>Poliziano defends the originality of his correction in Propertius before moving to a legal correction in the Digest, proving that the word "alium" should be "album" (the praetor's notice board) based on the Florentine manuscript.</summary>
<keywords>Ocnus, Propertius, Digest, Ulpian, Labeo, Florentine Pandects, Accursius, legal philology, textual criticism</keywords>

[Large initial E spanning two lines]
**E**pigram is by Catullus. <margin>A passage of Catullus</margin> <note>original: "Epigramma est Catulli." Referring to the Roman poet Gaius Valerius Catullus.</note>
  Lælius had heard that his uncle was accustomed to scold,
  If anyone spoke of or practiced illicit pleasures.
  Lest this should happen to himself, he thoroughly defiled <note>original: "perdespuit"; literally "spat upon," but here used by Catullus to imply sexual defilement as an act of defiance.</note> his uncle's own
  Wife, and turned his uncle into a Harpocrates.
  He did what he wanted; for who would now mock
  The uncle himself? The uncle will not say a word. <note>original: "uerbum non faciet patruus." This is Catullus 88, where the uncle is silenced by the shame of the situation.</note>
It has long been asked who this Harpocrates might be, whom Catullus mentions both in this place and also in another epigram, saying thus:
  If anything has been entrusted to a silent and faithful friend,
  Whose soul’s loyalty is deeply known,
  You will find me to be one of those duly bound by their law,
  Cornelius, and consider me to have become a Harpocrates. <note>original: "factum me esse puta Harpocratem." This is Catullus 102, emphasizing his ability to keep a secret.</note>
Nevertheless, both in Venice and in Verona (as we have said), nearly eight years ago, I responded publicly regarding him to several scholars. I now call upon their faith and conscience as witnesses, lest I seem to someone to be ascribing the work of others to myself, rather than reclaiming what is mine. <margin>Harpocrates</margin> Plutarch, therefore, in the book he wrote *On Isis and Osiris*, <margin>His meaning</margin> writes that Harpocrates was born from Isis herself and Osiris, who had intimacy with her after he was already dead; yet he was born from a premature birth and with weak legs. <note>Plutarch's *De Iside et Osiride* is a primary source for Egyptian mythology as understood by the Greeks and Romans. Harpocrates is the Greek interpretation of "Horus the Child" (Har-pa-khered), typically depicted with a finger to his lips—a gesture the Romans misinterpreted as a command for silence.</note>

BOOK [unclear]

Furthermore, he <note>Harpocrates, the Greek interpretation of the Egyptian Har-pa-khered (Horus the Child).</note> is said to have been brought forth around the time of the winter solstice, incomplete and underdeveloped—at the time, one might say, when the offspring of the earth are just beginning to bud and turn green. For this reason, the first fruits of lentils are offered to him. Yet, Plutarch says, one must not think of him as an imperfect god, or an infant, or even as some sort of legume, but rather as a certain guardian and moderator of unrefined and imperfect speech, and of that which is least articulated. This is why he presses his lips with his finger, a gesture signifying reticence and silence. Indeed, in that month which they call Mesore <note>The twelfth month of the ancient Egyptian calendar, roughly equivalent to August.</note>, those offering the first fruits of legumes repeatedly say: "The tongue is fortune; the tongue is a guiding spirit." <note>original: "lingua fortuna, lingua daemon." This reflects the belief that one's words can dictate one's destiny.</note> And among Egyptian trees, they say the Peach tree is primarily sacred to him, because its fruit is very much like a heart, and its leaf is very much like a tongue.

<margin>The form of the Peach tree</margin>

This much from Plutarch regarding Harpocrates, selected, plucked out, and at intervals. Lucillius also, a Greek poet, mentions him in an epigram to Dionysius, warning that if he has an enemy, he should not pray to Isis against him, nor to Harpocrates, nor to those who make the angry gods blind, for he will soon learn what a god and what Simo <note>Likely a reference to a specific character or a satirical name in the epigram.</note> are capable of. But Tertullian also speaks thus in his *Apologeticus*: "Serapis, and Isis, and Harpocrates, with his dog-headed companion <note>original: "cynocephalo"; referring to the god Anubis, who was often depicted with the head of a jackal or dog.</note>, were prohibited from being brought into the Capitol—that is, they were driven from the council of the gods. The consuls Piso and Gabinius, who were certainly not Christians, abolished them and even overturned their altars, checking the vices of shameful and idle superstitions; yet you, once these were restored, have conferred upon them the highest majesty." <note>Tertullian (c. 155–220 AD) was a prolific early Christian author who criticized the Roman adoption of Egyptian deities.</note> But Marcus Varro...

### ON FREEDMEN WHO ARE CALLED "DEDITICII." CHAPTER 84.

<margin>Passage from the *Institutes*.</margin>
These *Institutes* <note>The *Institutes* are a foundational legal textbook commissioned by the Emperor Justinian I in the 6th century CE.</note> which are cited in civil law were published under the name of the Emperor Justinian, but were actually composed by Tribonian <note>A famous jurist and advisor to Justinian.</note> and other learned men. They are also found written in Greek with exactly the same meaning, except that certain rites and old Roman customs are related somewhat more fully and freely in the Greek text than in the Latin code. This was done so that the entire matter might become thoroughly known to foreigners and those unfamiliar with Roman ways and customs. 

<margin>Freedmen *Dediticii*.</margin>
But regarding those freedmen who were called *dediticii* <note>In Roman law, *dediticii* were the lowest class of freedmen. They were former slaves who had been punished or branded during their servitude and were thus denied full citizenship or even basic residence in Rome upon being freed.</note>, which is discussed more strictly and briefly in our *Institutes*, it did not seem inappropriate to draw from the Greek volumes...

Among the ancients, those who were manumitted <term>Manumission: The formal process by which a slave owner grants freedom to an enslaved person.</term> sometimes attained a greater and "just" liberty and became Roman citizens; at other times, they received a lesser liberty and became <margin>Latins.</margin>
Junian Latins <margin>Junians.</margin> <note>original: "Latini Iuniani." At other times, they received even an [Marginal note: Dediticii.]

For if anyone, while serving a long term of slavery, had undergone [Marginal note: Dediticius.]
punishment for a crime—for instance, if he had been "inscribed," which means he had received marks or brands burned into his forehead, or if he had been thrown into a public prison, or beaten for an offense and had confessed to his crime—and was then later manumitted by the grace of his master, he was called a "Deditician Freedman." This was modeled after the example of the "surrendered foreigners" <note>original: "peregrinorum dedititiorum." These were foreign peoples who had surrendered unconditionally to Rome after a war.</note>. For when certain foreigners who were subjects of the Romans, in an arrogant spirit, suddenly took up war against them, but were then defeated by force and, having thrown down their arms, surrendered themselves to the victors, they were treated by the Romans with enough humanity that they were granted their lives. However, they were marked with this brand of disgrace, so to speak: they were henceforth called *dediticii* because they had "surrendered" themselves. For this reason, this class of freedmen was also designated as *dediticii* by the Aelian-Sentian law, so that those who had been similar in the commission of a crime would also be
->called by the same<-
->name.<-

101 BOOK

CONCERNING THE NATURE OF THE GAMES OF CERES MENTIONED IN JUVENAL, AND THE FOUNDER OF THE CERIALIA, MEMMIUS.

CHAPTER LXXXV.

<margin>Passage from Juvenal.</margin>
Juvenal, in his fourteenth Satire, speaks thus:
"Therefore, you may leave behind all the stage-curtains of Flora,
And of Ceres, and of Cybele."
<note>original: "Ergo omnia Floræ, Et Cereris licet, & Cybeles aulæa relinquas." Juvenal (Satire 14, 262-263) is mocking those who find more entertainment in the theater and public festivals than in the real-life drama of human greed.</note>
None of the commentators on this poet have spoken anything regarding these games of Ceres—neither what they were called, nor what occurred during them, nor by whom they were first celebrated, nor where else they are mentioned. Each of these details, however, was not inconsistent with the duty of a good and learned commentator. Therefore, we must remedy this whole oversight, lest the thoroughness of our care be found lacking even in these small matters.

<margin>The Cerialia.</margin>
Therefore, the games of Ceres mentioned here were called the *Cerialia* <term>Cerialia: A major Roman festival held in honor of the grain goddess Ceres, typically celebrated in mid-to-late April.</term>, in which horse races were celebrated in the circus; Gaius Memmius, a curule aedile <term>Curule Aedile: A high-ranking magistrate in Rome responsible for public works and the organization of festivals and games.</term>, was the first to establish them. Cornelius Tacitus, in the sixteenth book of his *Histories* <note>original: "libro historiarum sextodecimo." Although the author cites the "Histories," this passage actually appears in Book 15 of Tacitus's *Annals*. The author likely had a manuscript where these works were grouped or numbered differently.</note>, speaking of the Emperor Nero, says: "At length," he says, "he decided to carry out his designs on the day of the Circus games which are celebrated for Ceres, because Caesar [Nero], who rarely went out and kept himself closed within his house or gardens, used to frequent the spectacles of the Circus."
<margin>Games of the Circus.</margin>
And a little later: "And so that the spectacle of the Cerialian Circus games [might be celebrated] with more horse races..."

### ON THE TENTH OR
### decuman wave, which very many poets
### have mentioned.

### CHAPTER LXXXVI.

[Marginal note:]
<margin>The tenth or decuman. A passage from Ovid.</margin>

There are some verses by the poet Ovid in the first volume of his *Tristia* <term>Tristia: "Sorrows," a collection of poems written by Ovid during his exile from Rome.</term>:
*The wave that comes here towers over all the waves;*
*It follows the ninth and precedes the eleventh.*
<note>original: "Qui uenit hinc fluctus, fluctus superminet oẽs / Posterior nono est, undecimóq; prior." From Ovid, *Tristia* 1.2.49-50.</note>
He understands the "decuman" <term>decuman: Derived from "decimus" (tenth). In Roman belief, every tenth wave was thought to be significantly larger and more dangerous than the others.</term> wave to be that which is said to be the greatest, just as eggs are also called "decuman" when they are the largest. For it is said that the tenth egg laid is born larger than the rest. From this, "decuman" shields, which were the most ample in size, were also named. Similarly, the "decuman gate" in a military camp is the one turned away from the enemy. And just as the decuman boundary line in a field runs from the east to the west...

<meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the "decuman wave," moving from the initial definition to a refutation of certain technical etymologies, followed by a compilation of poetic evidence for the phenomenon's significance in classical literature.</meta>

MISCELLANIES. 107

to be called "decuman," <note>original: "decumanum dictum essum." This completes the sentence from the previous page.</note> it does not seem to me to be named for that reason—as if it were "double-handed," <note>original: "duocimanus."</note> as Marcus Junius Nypsus <term>Marcus Junius Nypsus: A Roman land surveyor (gromaticus) of the 2nd century AD who wrote technical manuals on land division.</term> believes; for that explanation is too harsh and forced. Rather, it is so named because it is the longest wave. For even the *cardo* <term>cardo: In Roman surveying, the primary north-south line that divided a territory.</term> itself is considered to be halved when the *decumanus* <term>decumanus: The primary east-west line in Roman land measurement; the author suggests the term implies the "greatest" or "primary" element.</term> naturally takes precedence. But regarding the tenth or "decuman" wave, the same poet [Ovid] also mentions it in the eleventh book of the *Metamorphoses* with these words:

*Rising more vastly, the force of the tenth wave rushes on.*
<note>original: "Vastius insurgens decimæ ruit impetus undæ." From Ovid, *Metamorphoses* 11.530.</note>

And Silius Italicus in his fourteenth book:
*Not otherwise does the North Wind from the peak of Mount Rhodope rush headlong; when it has cast itself forth, it drives the sea onto the lands with its tenth surge.*
<note>original: "Non aliter Rhodopes Boreas à uertice præceps, / Cum sese immisit, decimoq́; uolumine pontum / Expulit in terras." From Silius Italicus, *Punica* 14.121-123.</note>

But also Seneca in the tragedy *Agamemnon*: [small decorative mark]
*This ship sinks under its load, that one submits its shattered side to the waves; the tenth wave covers this one.*
<note>original: "Hæc onere sidit, illa conuulsum latus / Summittit undis, fluctus hanc decimus tegit." From Seneca, *Agamemnon* 501-502.</note>

Valerius Flaccus also says this in the second book of the *Argonautica*:
*How great and how often, even with the art of Pallas Athena, did the towering swell of the tenth wave fall in vain.*
<note>original: "Quanta quoties & Palladis arte / Incassum decimæ cecidit tumor arduus undæ." From Valerius Flaccus, *Argonautica* 2.198-199.</note>

Lucan as well in the fifth book of the *Pharsalia*:
*The tenth wave—marvellous to tell—lifts this ship together with its destiny, even with its weakened stern.*
<note>original: "Hæc fatum decimus, dictu mirabile, fluctus / Inualida cum puppe leuat." From Lucan, *Pharsalia* 5.670-671.</note>

So that this very thing is notable and remarkable: that this tenth wave is celebrated so greatly by what seems like a harmonious choir of poets.
