->THAT THE WORD *PHILOTHEOROS* WAS IN CICERO'S<-
->letter, which was thereafter wickedly corrupted<-
->into the name *Philocteten*<-

->CHAPTER LXXXVII<-

<margin>A passage of Cicero.</margin> In Cicero's letter to Trebatius <note>Gaius Trebatius Testa was a prominent Roman jurist and a close friend of Cicero.</note>—the beginning of which is, *You know it is in the Trojan horse*, and at the end, *they are wise too late*—it is found written thus in almost all copies: *Then, because you showed yourself in Britain to be no less than Philoctetes, I certainly do not blame you.* <note>original: "Deinde quod in Britãnia nõ minus quàm Philocteten te præbuisti, planè non reprehendo." Philoctetes was a Greek hero of the Trojan War, famous for his bow but also for being abandoned on an island due to a foul-smelling wound.</note> I had tormented and worn myself out for a long time trying to draw the meaning out of these words, and it was not progressing. For why should Cicero speak of Philoctetes here? But indeed, afterwards, through the efforts of Lorenzo de' Medici, I obtained that most ancient manuscript which I also cited above, which is much less deformed, defiled, perverted, and confused than the others. For in it, it was written thus: *Then, because you showed yourself in Britain to be no less a **philotheoros***, <margin>Philotheorus.</margin> *I certainly do not blame you.* For a <term>philotheoros: Derived from the Greek φιλοθέωρος (*philotheōros*), meaning a lover of spectacles, a sightseer, or someone who observes rather than participates.</term> is one who is fond of viewing spectacles, which is how Trebatius had conducted himself in Britain; while avoiding battle, he devoted himself to leisure in the midst of arms. From this comes the material for Cicero's jest and mockery. <note>Cicero is teasing Trebatius for going to Britain with Caesar's army only to act as a "tourist" or observer to avoid the actual fighting.</note>

<summary>The author corrects a corrupted passage in Cicero's letters, showing that a Greek-derived word meaning "spectator" was mistaken by later scribes for the mythological name Philoctetes.</summary>

# MISCELLANIES. 108

## WHENCE THAT WAS DERIVED,
"We were Trojans," and what appears in literature
concerning the Lacedaemonian <note>Lacedaemonian is the formal name for the Spartans.</note>
choir and song.

### CHAPTER LXXXVIII.

<margin>"We were Trojans."</margin> **W**e were Trojans: <note>original Latin: "Fuimus Troës." This famous phrase is from Virgil's *Aeneid* (2.325), spoken by the priest Panthus to describe the fall of Troy: "We have been Trojans; Troy has been."</note> this figure of speech is so effective, derived from the custom of that Lacedaemonian choir, and adopted as if into a proverb. For as it is written in Vergil—and there is a passage in Plutarch <note>Plutarch (c. 46 – 119 AD) was a Greek philosopher and biographer whose works were foundational to Renaissance humanism.</note> in his little book titled *On how a man may praise himself without envy*—the Lacedaemonian choir was threefold: of the old men, the boys, and the youths.

The old men would sing thus: *We were once brave young men.* <note>original Greek: "ἄμμες πότ' ἦμες ἄλκιμοι νεανίαι" (*ammes pot' ēmes alkimoi neaniai*)</note> By which they signified that they had once been robust youths. But the boys [sang] thus: *And we shall be much better than you.* <note>original Greek: "ἄμμες δέ γ' ἐσόμεσθα πολλῷ κάρρονες" (*ammes de g' esomestha pollō karrōnes*)</note> From which they declared that they would be far superior to these in the future. Moreover, the song of the youths was this: *And we are so now; if you wish, look at us.* <note>original Greek: "ἄμμες δέ γ' εἰμὲν, αἰδ' ἐ λῇς αὐγάσδεο" (*ammes de g' eimen, aid' e lēs augasdeo*)</note> Indicating that they were already what the others had been, or what the boys hoped to become, and were ready to put the matter to the test.

This same thing is written in the collections of Diogenianus <note>Diogenianus was a 2nd-century Greek grammarian who compiled a famous collection of proverbs.</note>. Aristophanes poses a very similar proverb regarding the Milesians in his comedy *Plutus*: *Long ago the Milesians were brave.* <note>original Greek: "πάλαι ποτ' ἦσαν ἄλkiμοι μιλήσιοι" (*palai pot' ēsan alkimoi milēsioi*)</note> So that we may understand they are no longer the brave men they once were.
                                                                       Q. iij

<summary>This chapter explains that the phrase "We were Trojans" (Fuimus Troes) draws its rhetorical power from an ancient Spartan choral tradition where different generations compared their strength. The author cites Plutarch and Aristophanes to show how such expressions signify a lost or passing era of glory.</summary>

<margin>Passage from Virgil.</margin>

In Virgil’s *Bucolics*, <note>Also known as the *Eclogues*.</note> these two small verses are read thus:
*Begin, little boy; him on whom his parents have not smiled,*

Although among the ancients the word was the same in both instances and written with the same letters—as an attentive reader <note>original Latin: "non oscitans lector," literally "a non-yawning reader."</note> will find in the first book of Quintilian’s *Education of the Orator*—nonetheless... Bringing up Terence’s *Phormio* <note>original: "Phormionem Terentianam"; a famous Roman comedy.</note>, he also cited this very verse of Virgil <note>original: "Maronis", referring to Publius Vergilius Maro.</note>, and reported that Probus <note>Marcus Valerius Probus, a 1st-century Roman grammarian known for his critical signs on texts.</note> had also noted the same. Therefore, the **table** and the **bed** are proofs of vitality <margin>To laugh is the mark of humanity's origin.</margin> because they were employed at the very beginnings of life. Moreover, man has his own unique capacity for laughter. Indeed, for one who does not laugh, how can life be a "life worth living" <note>original: "uita uitalis". A common Latin idiom for a life of quality and social connection.</note>? As Ennius <note>Quintus Ennius, the "father of Roman poetry."</note> says. Truly, you cannot refer the god and goddess in this passage to Varro’s <note>Marcus Terentius Varro, a prolific Roman scholar and antiquarian.</note> deities **Educa**, **Potina**, and **Cuba**, because these are all female deities, as Augustine also declared in the sixth book of *The City of God*.

But by equal reasoning, this refers to **Pilumnus** and **Pitumnus**, the gods of marriage. Concerning them—as Martianus <note>Martianus Capella, a late antique author of an influential encyclopedia on the liberal arts.</note> writes that Varro states in the second book *On the Life of the Fathers*—if a newborn was "vital" <note>original: "uitalis"; meaning the child was healthy and likely to survive infancy.</note>, once they had been lifted by the midwife and laid upon the ground so that they might begin their life upright, it was also customary to spread a bed in the house for these gods. It remains, therefore, that what we have said concerning the **Genius** and **Juno** <note>In Roman belief, every man had a *Genius* and every woman a *Juno*, protective spirits that accompanied them through life.</note> should also be supported by the "pillars" <note>original: "tibicinibus"; literally "struts" or "props," used here metaphorically for supporting evidence.</note> of authority. Pliny, therefore, says in the first book of his *Natural History*: "Since individuals make for themselves just as many gods—Genii and Junos—by adopting them for themselves." Seneca, in his 110th <note>The Latin text says "undeuigesimo" (19th), but the citation refers to Letter 110 in the *Epistles to Lucilius*.</note> letter to Lucilius, says: "I want you to set this aside, however, so that you remember our ancestors, who believed in the Stoic way. For they gave both a **Genius** and a **Juno** to each individual." From which I think Juvenal's line comes: <margin>A passage from Juvenal.</margin> "And," he says, "with the master’s servant swearing by the **Juno** of his master." ...let the judge be one who has sworn allegiance to the truth of the matter rather than to the words of any man <note>original: "in hominis uerba... in ueri fidem iuratus"; Poliziano is alluding to Horace’s famous line about not being bound to any single master’s school of thought.</note>. And if judges are indeed to be named, I certainly do not want Tarentines <margin>Tarentines</margin>, Consentines <margin>Consentines</margin>, or Sicilians to read these works of mine—just as Lucilius <note>Gaius Lucilius (c. 180–103 BC) was the first great Roman satirist. Consider such a man, if I may say so, to be he who provides me with so many tools for my studies and such deep leisure <note>original: "ocium"; in the Renaissance, "otium" was not mere idleness, but the productive, scholarly peace required for intellectual work.</note>: Lorenzo de' Medici <margin>Lorenzo</margin> <margin>de' Medici</margin> <margin>Praise.</margin>, powerful by his ancestors and forefathers, the pillar of the Florentine Republic. While both cities and princes experience his most circumspect judgment and a certain natural elevation of mind, especially in the conduct of affairs, those who have not looked as deeply into him as I have cannot help but admire him also in the study of letters, humanity, and wisdom. 

Such a man also is my friend Giovanni Pico <margin>Praise of Pico della Mirandola</margin>, of the princes of Mirandola—a most perfect work of nature—through whom I believe philosophy, now preparing to speak in Latin, will reach its highest summit.

MISCELLANIES. 112

...are said to be caused by <term>black bile</term> <note>Also known as melancholy, one of the four humors in ancient medicine believed to cause both genius and madness.</note>; and first he treats of Hercules, <margin>The fury of Hercules</margin> gathering that he was certainly of such a temperament by these sorts of signs and arguments: that the "sacred" <term>comitial disease</term> <margin>The sacred comitial disease</margin> <note>A historical term for epilepsy, so called because if a seizure occurred during the Roman "comitia" (assembly), the meeting was suspended as an ill omen.</note> was named after him; that in his madness he killed his own sons, as is described in the tragedy by Seneca; and that before he died, he suffered from an eruption of seething ulcers. 

From this, I believe, the story of the <margin>The Tunic of Nessus</margin> Tunic of Nessus <note>In myth, Hercules was killed by a tunic soaked in the poisonous blood of the centaur Nessus, which burned his skin like fire.</note> entered into legend, for this too is a symptom of black bile. Because of this, he says, that same kind of <margin>The ulcers of Hercules</margin> ulcers emerged on Lysander the Spartan shortly before his death. Thus Theodore Gaza, as far as the sons and the ulcers of Hercules are concerned, translates the passage in this way: 

"The disturbance of the mind regarding the boys," he says, "explains this same thing, as does the eruption of ulcers which sometimes precedes death." 

Whereas the Greek text reads: "And the <term>ecstasy</term> <note>original: "ἔκστασις" (ekstasis); literally a 'standing outside oneself' or displacement of the mind.</note> concerning the children, and the eruption of ulcers that occurred before his disappearance in the [place]." <note>original: "καὶ ἡ περὶ τοὺς παῖδας ἔκστασις καὶ ἡ πρὸ τῆς ἀφανíσεως αὐτοῦ ἐν τῇ ἑλκῶν ἔκφυσις γενομένη"</note>

Nor would I doubt that it is useful, before we translate the passage ourselves, to consider more diligently what that phrase "in the" <note>original: "ἐν τῇ" (en tē)</note> means, which Gaza ignores, or what meaning can be drawn from that preposition and the detached article. Indeed, I think it should be read and corrected to "among the" <note>original: "ἐν τοῖς" (en tois)</note>, so that the literal sense is: "And the terror—or if you prefer, the displacement of the mind—concerning his children, and before his death, the eruption of ulcers on Mount Oeta." 

As for why we preferred to translate it as "terror" or "displacement of the mind" rather than "disturbance of the mind" <margin>Ecstasy</margin> as Gaza did—which in Greek is *Ecstasis*—we follow the author in the former...

He proposes a philological correction to the Greek text to clarify that Hercules' ulcers broke out on Mount Oeta.</summary>

<margin>Hilary.</margin> ...which **Hilary of Poitiers** <note>A 4th-century bishop and theologian noted for his Latin translations and commentaries on the Psalms.</note> approves as an interpreter of the Psalms, and later **Jerome**, who nevertheless in some places rendered that solitary term "departure" <note>original: "excessus"; used here to mean a departure or "standing out" of the mind.</note> into Latin as <term>ecstasy</term> <note>original: "Ecstasis"; from the Greek "ekstasis," meaning a displacement or being "beside oneself."</note>. <meta>This page continues Poliziano’s critique of the scholar Theodore Gaza. The previous page ended with Poliziano arguing that scholars should be willing to admit mistakes and "retract their move" in the pursuit of truth. Here, he offers general advice on the rigor required for scholarship before pivoting back to a specific criticism of Gaza’s arrogance regarding his predecessors.</meta>

[unclear] BOOK [unclear]

...let them not relax their focus, nor spare effort and labor, nor indulge themselves, nor yield to the burden. Let them look around on all sides, weigh, ponder, and demand an account of every single thing; let there be nothing they do not scent out, shake out, examine, and correct again and again, bringing it back often to the **anvil** <note>Poliziano uses the metaphor of a blacksmith’s anvil to describe the process of rigorous, repetitive editing and refinement.</note>. And let them, in the meantime, consult even the less learned, and not despise the "little strengths" (if I may call them that) of those who are more humble. For it is almost always the case that we are as sharp-eyed as **Lynceus** <note>In Greek mythology, Lynceus was an Argonaut gifted with such keen sight that he could see through trees, stones, and even underground.</note> regarding the errors of others, but bleary-eyed and blind to our own, failing to see—as Catullus says—**what is in the part of the wallet hanging on our back** <note>A reference to Catullus 22.21, based on Aesop’s fable: every person carries two bags; the one in front contains our neighbors’ faults, while the one behind our back contains our own.</note>.

But above all, let those who write take care that, while they hasten too quickly toward the desire for praise, they do not thrust their still-raw studies into the public eye. I confess I wonder at this in **Theodore** <note>Theodore Gaza.</note>: why he wrote in the preface to his books on the *History of Animals* by Aristotle—the works for which he is most highly praised—that he was aided by no one, nor was he even competing with other translators, whom he claims it is "no trouble" to surpass. He says this even though **George of Trebizond** <margin>George of Trebizond</margin> <note>A Greek scholar and rival of Gaza. Trebizond’s translations were often criticized by later humanists for their style, but Poliziano here defends his technical contribution.</note> had already translated those same books so brilliantly before him. By either restoring terms found among the ancients or by inventing and devising new words for himself that perfectly reflect the Latin character, George was the first of the moderns, I believe, to teach us by our own fault why we ourselves have fewer names for things than the Greeks do. Therefore, if anyone reads these books diligently, he will surely praise Gaza less, who followed almost in his <note>Trebizond's</note> footsteps...

<summary>Poliziano advises scholars to practice extreme rigor and humility in their work, using the rivalry between Theodore Gaza and George of Trebizond to illustrate the danger of academic arrogance. He argues that Gaza unfairly dismissed Trebizond’s earlier, foundational work on Aristotle’s zoological texts.</summary>

Theodore translates that term, "marrow of Persuasion" <note>original Latin: *Suadaemedulla*</note>, as *meligeryn* <note>original Greek: μελίγηριν; meaning "sweet-speaking" or "honey-voiced"</note>, as if you were to say "sweet-speaking." <margin>Marrow of Persuasion</margin> Although the same Cicero, in his work *Brutus*, explains this passage of Ennius and its words quite fully. He says: "As for 'the marrow of Persuasion'—the goddess whom the Greeks call *Peitho* <margin>Peitho</margin> <note>original: *Pitho* (Πειθώ), the Greek goddess of persuasion.</note>, and whose mastery is the goal of the orator—Ennius called her *Suada*. He intends for Cethegus to have been her 'marrow,' so that just as Eupolis wrote that this goddess sat upon the lips of Pericles, here he says our orator was the very marrow of this goddess." Thus far Cicero in the *Brutus*. From this it clearly appears that the passage was not understood by Theodore. For he should not have translated it as "sweet-voiced," but as "marrow of persuasion" <note>original Greek: πειθῶς μυελὸν (*peithous myelon*)</note>. Yet we will not argue this point at greater length, because the matter of this verbal dispute has no need of a judge or investigator other than Marcus Tullius [Cicero] himself. Since, however, Marcus Tullius also cites Eupolis, who wrote that this goddess of persuasion sat upon the lips of Pericles—the goddess whom he calls *Peitho* and Ennius calls *Suada*—come now (if it please you), let us set down the verses themselves from the *Demes* <note>original Greek: δήμοις (*Demoi*); a lost comedy by Eupolis</note> (for that is the name of the comedy). ...let us restore a certain body [of the text]. These are the verses which we found:

He was the most powerful of men at speaking;
whenever he came forward, just like the good runners
who take a lead of eleven feet, he would overtake the orators;
swift in speaking, and yet alongside that very speed,
a certain Persuasion sat upon his lips;
thus he cast a spell, and alone among the orators,
he left his sting behind in those who heard him.
<note>original Greek: κράτιστος οὗτος ἐγένετ' ἀνθρώπων λέγειν / ὁπότε παρέλθοι, ὥσπερ οἱ ἀγαθοὶ δρομεῖς / ἐκ καί δεκα ποδῶν ᾕρει λέγων τοὺς ῥήτορας / ταχὺς λέγειν μὲν, πρὸς δέ γ' αὐτῷ τῷ τάχει / πειθώ τις ἐπεκάθιζεν ἐπὶ τοῖς χείλεσιν / οὕτως ἐκήλει καὶ μόνος τῶν ῥητόρων / τὸ κέντρον ἐγκατέλιπε τοῖς ἀκροωμένοις. These lines are from the lost play *Demes* by the Old Comedy poet Eupolis.</note>

Nor did we draw these verses of the poet Eupolis directly from his own sources, as his works have perished over time; but we received them partly from a certain very accurate commentator on the orator Aristides, and partly from a letter of Pliny the Younger. <note>See Pliny, *Epistles* 1.20, where he cites these lines to justify a more copious style of oratory.</note> Indeed, Aristides himself, in the oration entitled *For Pericles*, calling upon the testimony of that same comic poet, speaks thus: "And only a certain Persuasion sat upon his lips." <note>original Greek: μόνον δὲ πειθώ τις ἐπεκάθιζεν ἐν τοῖς χείλεσιν.</note> Now, what Ennius called *Suada* <note>original: *Suada*, the Roman goddess of persuasion.</note>, I believe Horace calls *Suadela* <note>original: *Suadela*.</note>. As he says: "But *Suadela* and Venus appropriately adorn the well-moneyed man." <note>original Latin: *Sed bene, inquit, nummatū decorat Suadela, Venusq;.* From Horace, *Epistles* 1.6.38.</note> For I do not agree with Porphyrio <note>A 3rd-century scholar and commentator on Horace.</note>, who considers that to be an epithet of Venus. For Cicero, in the third book of *On the Orator*, when he speaks of Pericles, preferred to name it <margin>Suadela.</margin> "Charm" <margin>Horace, book 1.</margin> <margin>Charm.</margin> rather than "Persuasion," saying thus: "On whose lips the old comic poets—even when they were slandering him, as was then permitted at Athens—said that Charm dwelt, and that there was such force in him, that in the minds of those who had heard him, as if..." <note>Cicero, *De Oratore* 3.34. Poliziano is highlighting how Cicero translates the Greek concept of the "sting" of oratory into Latin "charm" (*lepos*).</note>

MISCELLANIES. 116

...he might leave certain stings behind. On the other hand, Quintilian, not even daring to utter the Latin name of that goddess <note>Poliziano refers to the Greek goddess Peitho; Quintilian often preferred Greek terminology for rhetorical concepts for which Latin lacked a precise, elegant equivalent.</note>, writes thus in the tenth book of his *Institutes of Oratory*: "And that which is the testimony of Old Comedy concerning Pericles might most justly be transferred to this man: that a certain goddess of persuasion sat upon his lips." <note>See Quintilian, *Institutio Oratoria* 10.1.82.</note> Furthermore, Martianus Capella calls her sometimes *Suada*, sometimes *Pitho*. We ourselves, in our poems *Manto* and *Ambra*, have called her sometimes *Suada*, sometimes *Lepos* (Charm), following the example of Ennius and Cicero.

->### WHO THE *CAUSARII* ARE IN LIVY.<-

->### CHAPTER XCII.<-

**L**ivy, in the seventh book *From the Founding of the City*, <margin>Livy’s passage.</margin> says: "Let a third army be enrolled from the <term>*causarii*<gloss>Soldiers discharged for medical reasons.</gloss></term> and the elders by Titus Quintius, to <margin>*Causarii*.</margin> serve as a protection for the city and its walls." You will not easily find someone who can readily explain to you who these *causarii* are. But you may easily gather it from the words of the legal consultant Ulpian in the sixth book of his commentary *On the Edict*. These words are written in the third volume of the *Digest* <note>The *Digest* (or *Pandects*) is a vast collection of juristic writings on Roman law compiled by order of the Emperor Justinian I in the 6th century AD.</note>, under the title concerning those who are marked with infamy. The words are as follows: "Discharged for the sake of ignominy; this was added because there are many types of discharges. There is the honorable discharge, which is granted when service is completed or earlier by the favor of the Emperor; and the *causaria* discharge, which releases one from the labors of military service on account of ill health." From these words, we can reason that *causarii* is the proper name for soldiers sent home for the sake of their health.
->R iii<- <note>This is a "signature mark," used by early printers to help binders assemble the pages of a book in the correct order.</note>

### THE PREFACE OF PRINCE JUSTINIAN VINDICATED
### FROM CERTAIN VICES AND ERRORS. CHAPTER XCIII.

**I** am sometimes ashamed of this age in which I was born, far too unlearned and arrogant, as I repeatedly turn over in my mind what monsters of things and words have occupied even the books of the laws themselves by which we are governed. Just as recently, I noted—hesitating whether to laugh or be angry—in the letter of Justinian <note>This refers to the constitution *Omnem*, the introductory decree to the *Digest* (or *Pandects*) addressed to law professors regarding the new legal curriculum.</note>, which is placed before the books of the *Digest*, written to the illustrious men Theophilus, Dorotheus, Theodorus, Isidore, Anatolius, Thalelaeus, and Cratino, the law-professors, and to the most eloquent man Salaminius. For while the Emperor teaches there that students of the fourth year are called by the Greek name <term>*lytae*<gloss>Greek *lytai* (λύται), meaning "looseners" or "solvers," referring to students who have mastered the law's complexities.</gloss></term>, <margin>prolytae.</margin> and likewise those of the fifth year <term>*prolytae*<gloss>Greek *prolytai* (προλύται), meaning "advanced looseners."</gloss></term>, as it appears in those original archetypes <note>Poliziano refers to the *Littera Florentina*, a famous 6th-century manuscript of the *Digest* kept in Florence, which he studied to correct the errors of medieval legal scholars.</note> of the *Pandects*, all current copies now have *hircos* <note>Latin for "he-goats." A scribal corruption of the Greek *lytas*.</note> in the first place and *coloritas* in the second—words of no value, but which are nonetheless explained by Accursius <note>Accursius was a 13th-century jurist whose "Great Gloss" on Roman law was the standard authority of the time; humanists like Poliziano often mocked his lack of Greek knowledge and historical context.</note> as if they were most appropriate. Indeed, the listeners of the first year were called not *dispondii* (as is commonly said) <margin>Dupondii</margin> but *dupondii* <note>Literally "two-pounders," a term for a coin of little value. It was used as a humorous nickname for freshmen students, perhaps suggesting they were "worth two pennies."</note>, by a nickname as frivolous, as he says, as it is ridiculous, as I find in the archetypes. But also in that same letter (to show this too in passing), words are missing concerning the sixth book of the *Iliad* when the Emperor mentions Glaucus and Diomedes exchanging dissimilar things between themselves <note>A reference to *Iliad* 6.234-236, where Glaucus exchanges his gold armor for Diomedes' bronze armor—a "bad deal" that Justinian uses as a metaphor for legal or educational exchange.</note>. For after that word, this verse of Homer ought to follow immediately, if we believe the Archetype.

### A CHARMING COMMENTARY OF SEVERIAN CONCERNING THE MAKING OF THE SUN AND MOON, AND CONCERNING MONTHLY AND ANNUAL DAYS. CHAPTER XCIV.

**I** and this man, Pico della Mirandola—a man who can never be sufficiently praised—at one time perused during our leisure hours the ancient commentators of the old sacred instrument <margin>of the Old Testament</margin> <note>Poliziano uses the term *instrumentum* in its technical sense to mean the "Testament" or "Covenant."</note>, primarily the Greek ones: Diodorus, Philo, Gennadius, Aquila, <margin>commentators.</margin> Origen, Basil, Didymus, Isidore, Apollinaris, Severian <note>Severian of Gabala (d. c. 408 AD), a popular preacher in Constantinople whose works were often preserved under the name of John Chrysostom.</note>, and many others of that kind. Pico, in his usual way, sifts through and weighs everything <margin>Pico's diligence.</margin>, comparing all the opinions of the Latin writers as diligently as possible with those of the Jewish and Chaldean <note>By "Chaldean," Poliziano likely refers to Aramaic or Syriac sources, particularly the Targums, which Pico studied as part of his interest in the Kabbalah and Semitic philology.</note> interpreters, so that from them either the truth might be drawn out, or obscurity removed, or faith strengthened, or impiety refuted.

But what I remember us reading some days ago in the works of Severian, a brilliant author, seemed not unworthy of being admitted into this collection. The Hebrew lawgiver <margin>The making of the Moon and Sun.</margin> <note>A common Renaissance epithet for Moses.</note> relates that the sun and moon were first made by God on the fourth day after He had begun the world. Since, however, He had set one over the day and the other over the night, it can also be believed that He placed the former at the boundary of the East and the latter at the boundary of the West. Nor should God (as indeed this author thinks) have fashioned the moon from the very beginning in such a state that she was affected by loss, or clipped, or growing thin, or divided <note>Severian argues that the moon was created "perfect" or "full," rather than as a thin crescent, so that the divine creation would not appear deficient at its inception.</note>.

R iiij <note>Printer's signature mark.</note>

->**B O O K**<-

...as if it were empty, or faint, or drained of light, as we see it now when it is either newly born, or between months, or growing old, or in conjunction with the sun, or even in its very failing <note>original: "deliquo," likely referring to an eclipse or the moon's disappearance.</note>. Rather, it was in a full sphere, a complete circle—not humped <note>gibbous</note>, not over-swollen, not curved, not horned <note>crescent</note>, not divided, not silent <note>original: "silēs." The "silent moon" (interlunium) refers to the period when the moon is dark and invisible to the eye.</note>. For that subsequent variety of phases was intended to serve as a marker and divider for the intervals of nights and days. 

Therefore, when the Sun was first brought forth and was entering its morning gates, the Moon was directly opposite, skimming the western horizon, performing its duty as a globe for the first time, and in what I might call its "apprenticeship" <note>original: "tirocinio." Poliziano uses a term for a young soldier’s first service to describe the Moon’s first appearance.</note>. But as the Sun itself was driven toward the west, the Moon in turn was carried by its own course toward the border of the east. 

Thus, obeying the command, they presided equally over both day and night. But why then did the Moon’s face not show itself as a "fourth-day" moon <note>The moon was created on the 4th day of the Hexameron (the six days of creation).</note>, since that was the day it was brought forth? 

<margin>The fourth-day Moon.</margin>

Because, he <note>Severian of Gabala</note> says, a fourth-day moon could not have held the furthest limit of the west. Thus, appearing in a rounded shape with a completely full face—showing a fifteenth-day moon on the fourth day—it had, as it were, claimed eleven days for itself; and the Sun naturally surpasses the Moon by exactly that many days <note>The solar year of 365 days is roughly 11 days longer than the lunar year of 354 days. This difference is known as the "epact."</note>. From this it happens that the Moon, as if in good faith, restores those eleven days to the Sun by right. For since the monthly calculation of the Moon is twenty-nine and a half days, the annual total of twelve months results in exactly three hundred and fifty-four days. This sum, by joining to itself the other eleven days which the Moon had taken as an addition from the beginning...

<summary>Poliziano explains Severian of Gabala's theological argument that the Moon was created full on the fourth day of creation to account for the eleven-day difference between the solar and lunar years.</summary>

->**AN ELEGANT GREEK ADAGE**<-
->regarding guest-gifts, restored to the<-
->copies of the Digests. CHAPTER 95.<-

I am pleased, for the sake of the legal experts <note>original: "iureconsultorum," referring to those learned in Roman law.</note>, to restore a Greek proverb which has been plainly removed from their books, but remains whole in the Archetype <note>The "Archetype" likely refers to the *Littera Florentina*, a famous 6th-century manuscript of the Pandects/Digests held in Florence, which Poliziano studied closely.</note>, just as it is found in a certain letter of the divine Severus and the Emperor Antoninus. This letter is cited by Ulpian <note>Domitius Ulpianus (c. 170–223 AD), one of the great Roman jurists whose work makes up a large part of the Digest.</note> in the first book on the *Office of the Proconsul*, and it is placed under the same title in the first book of the Digests <note>The *Digest* (or Pandects) is a massive compendium of juristic writings compiled by order of Emperor Justinian I.</note>. The words of the letter are these. Since they are most elegant and full of good fruit, I think they should be learned by those who are going to manage the republic:

"As far as guest-gifts <note>original: "xenia." In Roman law, these were gifts offered to provincial governors or officials, which were strictly regulated to prevent bribery.</note> are concerned," he says, "hear what we think. There is a Greek proverb: **Neither everything, nor always, nor from everyone.**" <margin>Proverb.</margin> <margin>Greek.</margin> <note>original Greek: οὔτε πάντα, οὔτε πάντοτε, οὔτε παρὰ πάντων (*oute panta, oute pantote, oute para pantōn*).</note> For it is very inhumane to accept from no one, but to accept everywhere is most cheap, and altogether most greedy.

Thus they spoke. Furthermore, you might interpret the Greek words in this way: *Not everything, not everywhere, and not from everyone.*

**I**n the seventh book of *Epigrams* <note>Martial, *Epigrams* 7.10.</note>, in the commonly circulated manuscripts, it is written thus in Martial:
<margin>Martial, Book 7</margin> *For when you call me your life, when you call me your eyes.*
But indeed, all the oldest manuscripts have it thus:
*For when you call me your mouse, when you call me your eyes.*
This, however, has until now been noticed or corrected by no one. It can indeed be asked with what meaning a girl, flattering her lover, calls him a "mouse." Or is it in the same way she is accustomed to call him a "little sparrow" <note>A reference to Catullus’s famous poems about a lover's pet sparrow, often interpreted with double meanings.</note>, because mice also are said to be most salacious <note>original: "salacissimi," meaning lustful or sexually active.</note>?

"Finally, a detestable madam <note>original: "lena," a procuress or brothel-keeper.</note> approached me, swearing by the girl, by Diana, by Persephone <note>original: "Persephata."</note>, that she was a heifer, that she was a virgin, that she was an unbroken foal. But she was a mouse-hole <note>original: "cauus murinus." A comic metaphor suggesting she was not a virgin or was sexually exhausted.</note>." By this exaggeration [calling] her most salacious...

BOOK

...or versatile machines of some kind, in which we marvel at little figures <note>original: "imagunculas."</note> running about and playing by a hidden force. There are also fountains or even fires which, by a certain skillful arrangement and a novel appearance, shoot out water or flames at certain intervals. For we can interpret "Automaton" <note>original: "Automaton."</note> in English as "voluntary" or "spontaneous." Thus Homer, in the second book <note>The "second rhapsody" or second book of the *Iliad*.</note> of the *Iliad*, says that Menelaus came to the feast "automatically" <note>original: "automaton," referring to *Iliad* 2.408, where Menelaus arrives without being summoned.</note>, meaning of his own accord, not called by anyone, as if moved by himself. This is also how Eustathius <note>Eustathius of Thessalonica (c. 1115–1195), a Byzantine scholar and archbishop famous for his commentaries on Homer.</note> explains it, who then says that younger writers use the word "automata" <note>original Greek: "αὐτόματα."</note> for things that happen by chance, as if "in vain" <note>original Greek: "μάτην," meaning "matēn."</note>—that is, rashly and "just as it happened" <note>original Greek: "καὶ ὡς ἔτυχεν," meaning "kai hōs etychen."</note>. And a little later, the same author adds almost the same sentiment. Such a meaning also named those "automata" of the mechanics. Furthermore, the philosopher Hero <note>Hero of Alexandria (c. 10–70 AD), a Greek mathematician and engineer who described early steam engines and wind-powered machinery.</note> in his *Pneumatics* says that water is pressed upward "automatically" <note>original: "automaton," used here to mean spontaneously.</note> from a concave sphere which he describes; he also fashions a trumpeter who turns himself "automatically" <note>original Greek: "αὐτομάτως," meaning "automatōs."</note> toward little birds that are likewise artificial. He even teaches how to construct a small shrine <note>original: "ediculam."</note> whose doors open "automatically"—that is, of their own accord—when a fire is lit, and close when it is extinguished, so that everything should be understood in the sense we have described.

### WHAT THE "SILENCE OF THE MOON" MEANS IN VIRGIL, OR THE "SILENT VIRGIN" IN HORACE; AND THE REFUTATION OF THE OPINIONS OF SERVIUS AND ACRON THEREUPON. CHAPTER 100.

<margin>Passage from Virgil.</margin> **V**irgil, in the second book of the *Aeneid*:

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the "silent moon" mentioned by classical authors. The author moves from literary interpretations of Virgil and Horace to a scientific and musicological discussion, linking the "silence" of the moon to the Pythagorean theory of the "Music of the Spheres" and the mechanics of sound frequency as discussed by ancient philosophers like Aristotle, Porphyry, and Macrobius.</meta>

...the Moon, for those attempting to hide and deceive; or whether the "silent Moon" <note>original: "Lunam silentem." In Roman agricultural and ritual contexts, this referred to the period of the new moon when it was invisible.</note> in Marcus Cato, and likewise in Pliny, refers to that same harmony of the planets. But as for his claim that the last string of the lyre sounds less, this only holds true if he takes "last" not to mean the lowest string, but the highest—which in Greek is called *hypate*—from which a deeper <margin>Hypate. <note>The *hypate* was the "highest" string in terms of its physical position on the lyre, but it produced the lowest, deepest pitch. This inversion often confused later commentators.</note></margin> sound is produced. Just as from the outermost and thinnest string (which I would more willingly call the "last"), a sharp pitch is brought forth, which seems to sound more for the reason that it is stretched tight. Porphyry <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 234–305 AD) who wrote extensively on music theory.</note> writes that Theophrastus <note>Aristotle's successor in the Peripatetic school.</note> mentions this in his second book *On Music*, in those commentaries he left on Ptolemy’s *Harmonics*. <margin>Of the sounds of the strings.</margin> A low sound is heard less, he says, because it is diffused all around. However, what we read in Macrobius <note>A 5th-century Roman author whose commentary on the *Dream of Scipio* preserved much of ancient planetary and musical theory.</note> and likewise in others—that the sphere of Saturn, whose revolution is <margin>Macrobius, book 1.</margin> swifter, gives off the sharpest sound—I think this will <margin>Saturn's course.</margin> hardly be approved by Plato, either in the *Timaeus* or in the *Republic*. Plato writes that the sphere of Saturn is the slowest, though perhaps this is in reference to the longer circuit of its orbit. But Aristotle certainly, in the second book of *On the Heavens*, says that the higher spheres are slower because they are more retarded by the force of the eighth, star-bearing sphere. Therefore, to pass over the opinion of Alexander <note>Likely Alexander of Aphrodisias, a famous commentator on Aristotle.</note>, or of Simplicius <note>Another major Aristotelian commentator of the 6th century.</note>, or even of the moderns on this matter: if you wish to grasp the true meaning of Macrobius, you should refer it to the diurnal <note>The "diurnal" or daily motion is the apparent rapid movement of the stars and planets across the sky caused by the Earth's rotation (or, in the ancient view, the rotation of the entire celestial sphere).</note> motion, which is without doubt the swiftest in the highest...

He contrasts the views of Plato and Aristotle with later commentators like Macrobius and Porphyry regarding planetary speeds and acoustics.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of overly complex interpretations of classical texts. After discussing the "Music of the Spheres" and the pitch of strings on the previous page, the author now addresses the meaning of the "silent moon" in the works of Virgil, Pliny the Elder, and Cato the Elder, arguing for a literal rather than a mystical or musical interpretation.</meta>

### BOOK

...most sharp, and it will be found to be most rapid. But once we have dismissed this fictitious (in my opinion) and unnecessary subtlety of Servius <note>Servius was a famous 4th-century grammarian whose commentary on Virgil’s *Aeneid* was the standard reference for centuries. The author here finds his explanations overly complicated.</note>, we rather understand the <margin>The silence of the quiet moon</margin> "silence of the quiet moon" as the moon itself, which they call "silent"—that is, not shining at all at that time—so that ambushes could be better hidden. This is surely a beautiful and elegant <term>metaphor</term> <note>original: "tralatione." In Renaissance rhetoric, this refers to the "carrying over" of a word from one sense (hearing) to another (sight).</note> from the ears to the eyes. For just as in silence nothing is heard, so in darkness nothing is seen. 

Pliny, therefore, in Book 16 of his *Natural History*, says: "The day of the <margin>The silent moon.</margin> moon’s conjunction is called by some the 'silent moon,' and by others the *interlunium*." <note>The *interlunium* is the period between the old and new moon when it is invisible from Earth.</note> Then the same author says in Book 18: "They order that sowing be done during the silent moon." Marcus Cato also writes in his book *On Agriculture*: "Now let manure be hauled out during the silent moon; likewise during the silent moon, let figs, olives, <margin>Fruits.</margin> apples, and pears be grafted." Finally, he even orders that meadows be manured in early spring during that same silent moon. 

It would be ridiculous to refer these passages of Cato and Pliny to the "music of the stars," or to search for such obscurities in a very ancient and widely accepted expression. Indeed, I would take the "silent moon" here, as I said, to mean "not shining at all," which was the specific time the Greeks had to watch for. 

Yet I do not interpret this so strictly <note>original: "ad uiuum resecem," literally "to cut to the quick." A metaphor for over-analyzing a text until its spirit is lost.</note> as to mean the literal conjunction of the moon when the entire night is pitch black. This interpretation is refuted because the poet Virgil soon says: "Presented by the light of the moon." <note>Aeneid 2.340. The author is pointing out a potential contradiction: if the moon was "silent" (new/invisible), there would be no light to see the soldiers later in the story.</note> Therefore, the moon was not yet shining when the Greeks were sailing from Tenedos toward evening, but it began to shine when they had already occupied the city. Therefore, it was either not yet risen, or it was late.

<summary>The author argues that the "silent moon" in classical literature simply refers to the darkness of a new moon or a late-rising moon used for stealth, rather than a mystical reference to the "Music of the Spheres."</summary>
<keywords>silent moon, Virgil, Pliny the Elder, Cato the Elder, Servius, lunar phases, agriculture, Fall of Troy, metaphors, interlunium</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s philological investigation into the word "silent" as applied to the moon and other classical contexts. # MISCELLANIES. 122

...it might have been, or if the moon shone all night, then I think it was not at all a lunar <term>conjunction</term> <note>original: "coitus." The alignment of the moon and sun when the moon is invisible.</note>, but rather a reference to the duration of time that she did not shine. For indeed, a single word in the poets often encompasses more than it might seem. For this reason, then, there is that line in our poem *Rusticus*: <margin>A passage in the *Rusticus*.</margin>
"What the silent moon warns, and what the moon between months warns." <note>original: "Quidq; silens moneat, quidq; intermenstrua Phœbe." Poliziano is quoting his own neo-Latin poem, *Rusticus* (1483).</note>
Yet it is not absurd, beyond the temporary reason for the <term>epithet</term> <note>A descriptive term or label.</note>, that you should interpret the "friendly silences of the moon" <margin>Friendly silences of the Moon.</margin> in another way; for Cicero writes that in the taking of <term>auspices</term> <note>original: "auspicijs." The Roman practice of seeking omens from the flight or behavior of birds.</note>, "silence" is the term used for that which is free from every flaw or ill omen. <margin>Silence in the auspices.</margin> 

Now, consider how "beautiful" and "ingenious" is the comment found in Acron <note>Helenius Acron was a 2nd-century commentator on the works of Horace.</note>, who, while explaining a passage from Horace in the second book of his *Odes*: <margin>A passage from Horace.</margin>
-> "While the priest climbs the Capitol <-
-> with the silent virgin." <-
He understood "silent virgin" to mean "famous" because of the nobility of her priesthood. <margin>The silent virgin.</margin> And so that he might persuade the foolish of this interpretation, he even misuses the authority of Maro <note>Publius Vergilius Maro, known simply as Virgil.</note>, who said, "Through the friendly silences of the silent moon." <note>original: "Tacitæ per amica silentia lunæ." From Virgil's *Aeneid*.</note> It is as if "silent moon" should here also be taken to mean "famous moon." 

Clearly, Acron had not read in Dionysius <note>Dionysius of Halicarnassus, a Greek historian of the 1st century BCE.</note> (at least in his *Roman Antiquities*), or in Plutarch, or in many others, that it was utterly unknown by everyone exactly which sacred objects or statues the Vestal Virgins were in charge of guarding. Thus, for this very reason—because she maintained a sacred silence—the virgin is called "Silent." She was the only one for whom it was lawful to have seen the Trojan Minerva. <note>The "Trojan Minerva" refers to the Palladium, a wooden statue of the goddess Athena/Minerva believed to have been brought from Troy to Rome and guarded in secret by the Vestals.</note>

But so that this book, though it is perhaps already too long, might be crowned with a certain <term>finishing touch</term> <note>original: "coronide." ...not only of letters, but of all literary men, like a great lover of the <term>white line</term> <note>original: "alba linea." I devoted myself to both their philosophies;

He, therefore, with whom I am accustomed to share the sweetest concerns and sometimes to trifle pleasantly, and whom he has chosen as a nearly constant companion in his studies (which is the highest honor), has urged me to explore philosophy—not as before with sleepy eyes, but with vigorous and watchful ones—as if sounding a **signal** <note>original: "classico." For just as I could rightly be considered either uncivil or arrogant if I did not think that both these things and better ones could be born at home for him too, who is so diligent and learned...

### ON ANGER

...since he hoped that he would write better things thereafter. For human intellects <note>original: "Ingenia." In this context, it refers to natural talent or the inherent character of a person's mind.</note> are much like apples: if they are born somewhat hard and bitter to the taste, they nevertheless ripen in due time and become mellow and pleasant. But if they are born soft and watery from the start, like those that ripen too early, they become shriveled and rotten much sooner than they can mature. Therefore, the intellect, like apples, should be left to be mellowed by time and age.

Why do I mention this, you ask? Because indeed, Lorenzo de' Medici, many things exist in the human mind that are similar; for though they may present an appearance of a faulty and depraved nature, they are nevertheless judged by wise men to be signs of future virtue and specimens of an excellent disposition. Such, perhaps, is this very thing which I recently maintained regarding your young son <note>Likely referring to Piero de' Medici or Giovanni de' Medici (the future Pope Leo X), both of whom Poliziano tutored.</note> and my pupil: that his anger, which arose from a rebuke and a sense of shame, was by no means to be disapproved. 

Since you have asked for the reason for our judgment, it will certainly not be out of place to discuss that matter briefly for the present. Nor is it our purpose at this time to praise anger, even though the **Peripatetics** <note>The Peripatetic school was founded by Aristotle. Unlike the Stoics, who sought to eliminate emotions, Peripatetics believed emotions like anger should be moderated and used for virtuous ends.</note> believe it was not uselessly granted to us by nature, acting as a kind of spur (as they say) to incite courage; and indeed, as Aristotle also says, by some it is even considered a part of courage itself. But among every kind of vice, that should certainly be considered the least which deviates the least from the path of reason; and yet anger, if indeed—

->TO LORENZO DE' MEDICI. 126<-

One ought to believe Aristotle, or rather, if one listens in some way to reason itself and follows in its footsteps as far as possible, it is right to believe that this [anger] is deservedly the smallest of all vices. Since indeed, as it says in Flaccus <note>original: "Flaccum." This refers to the Roman poet Horace (Quintus Horatius Flaccus).</note>, "no one is born without vices," and "he is best who is burdened by the fewest," it must be admitted that anger in a boy is surely not altogether detestable. On the contrary, that affection of the mind which is entirely devoid of anger—which the Greeks call <term>aorgēsia</term> <note>original Greek: ἀοργησίαν; a state of being "without anger." In Aristotelian ethics, this was considered a deficiency—the inability to feel rightful indignation.</note>—seems to <margin>Aorgēsia.</margin> me no less blameworthy in a boy than sluggishness in a young horse. For since at such a weak and feeble age it cannot proceed from reason, it must necessarily proceed from stupor and a dullness of mind. For those in whom there is much cold black bile <note>Poliziano is referring to the "humoral" theory of medicine, where black bile caused melancholy or sluggishness.</note>—as Aristotle says in his *Problems*—are dull-witted, stunned, and cowardly. Those in whom it is abundant and hot are quick, gifted, sharp, and prone to every outburst of temper. For natural philosophers <note>original: "physici." In the Renaissance, this term referred to those who studied the "physics" or natural laws of the body and the world.</note> consider heat much more in harmony with nature than cold. The proof of this is clearly understood from many things, but especially from the seasons of the year. For when "harsh winter is dissolved by the pleasing change of spring and the West Wind," <note>original: "Solvitur acris hiems grata vice veris et Favoni." A famous opening line from Horace's *Odes* (1.4.1).</note> soon those most beautiful ornaments of the world and nature revive; the woods are clothed in leaves; the fields are adorned with all kinds of flowers and herbs, from which an incredible sweetness of scent is breathed forth. From here, the now clearer air is soothed by the most charming harmonies of the little birds; from there, the waters in the streams...

<meta>Continuing the argument from the previous page, Poliziano uses the cycle of the seasons to illustrate the necessity of "heat" and vitality in nature, transitioning into a critique of the Stoic ideal of emotionless apathy when applied to the education of Lorenzo de' Medici's son.</meta>

# ON ANGER

...murmur with flowing waters; everything smiles upon the eyes, and everything charms the ears. Meadows, fields, hills, woods, valleys, every kind of beast both wild and tame—moreover, the very sky and (so to speak) the very nature of things—summon our ears, eyes, and minds to their most beautiful and admirable spectacle. All things reach their bloom and frolic; in short, playfulness possesses everything. Behold: summer, as it is much hotter, is also more abundant in its yield of all grain and seeds. Autumn follows this, stained by the treading of grapes. Autumn carries in a wealthy hand that blessed horn of Amalthea <note>original: "Amaltheæ cornu." In Greek mythology, this is the "Cornucopia" or horn of plenty, which provides an unending supply of food.</note>, overflowing with an abundance of fruits and apples. Finally, icy winter soon extinguishes all these things, shaggy with its white hair.

But I return to the point from which I digressed. I say that those who so greatly extol that vacation from all passions—which the Greeks call <term>apatheia</term> <note>original Greek: ἀπάθειαν. While we use "apathy" to mean boredom, in ancient philosophy it meant a state of being unaffected by any emotion or suffering.</note> <margin>that is, insensibility <note>original: "indolentiam." In this context, it refers to the literal lack of "dolor" (pain or feeling) rather than laziness.</note></margin>—seem to me to have fashioned a life not for a man, but certainly for a tree trunk or a stone, which possesses no life at all. For indeed, should I choose for myself a boy of great and excellent hope who, being drowsy, listens to instructions with a drooping head? One whose ear must be constantly tweaked, who must be shaken by the hand, and for whom scoldings, lashings, and even torches <note>A metaphor for intense stimulation or metaphorical "fire" to wake the spirit.</note> must be applied? One must look first, Lorenzo, as it is written in the poet <note>Poliziano is quoting Virgil’s *Georgics*, a poem about agriculture and nature.</note>, at "what grief each feels when conquered, and what glory in the palm of victory." For the Lacedaemonians <note>The Spartans, famous for their rigorous and spirited upbringing of youth.</note> judged that if someone at such a fresh age was so affected that he was moved neither by praise nor by shame, nothing remarkable could ever be hoped for from him. But truly...

--- Page 309 ---
<meta>Continuing the argument that a complete lack of emotion is inhuman, the author now draws upon classical authorities like Quintilian and Aristotle to argue that a capacity for anger is actually a sign of a vigorous and healthy mind, distinguishing between "hot" anger and "cold" resentment.</meta>

TO LORENZO DE' MEDICI. 127

Quintilian <note>Marcus Fabius Quintilianus (c. 35–100 AD), a Roman educator and rhetorician whose work *Institutio Oratoria* was the gold standard for Renaissance education.</note>, that most excellent of teachers, asks that a boy be given to him who weeps when defeated and exults when victorious; indeed, play in a boy does not offend him. This is because play—just like this very subject of anger we are discussing—is evidence of a vigorous mind and an upright nature. For this reason, among those ancient philosophers (as recorded in Plutarch), it had already passed into a proverb: "He who has no anger has no mind." <note>original: "iram non habere, qui mentem non habeat." A common classical sentiment suggesting that emotion is a byproduct of mental vitality.</note> Perhaps it is from this principle that Aristotle judges a man's character by the appearance of his face; he says that those with broad foreheads are stupid, while those with round foreheads are prone to anger. This is clearly a manifest argument that nature herself wishes us to be warned by the features of the face that nothing differs so greatly from stupidity as a capacity for anger. 

But truly, while two different passions—emanating, as it were, from a single head and spring—are understood by different names, one of them has milder first impulses but is much more long-lasting and permanent, striking its roots deeper into the soul. The other, however, is sharper and more violent at first, but in its own time is more easily appeased. The former, which the Greeks call sometimes <term>menis</term> <note>original Greek: μῆνιν. Often translated as "wrath," specifically the long-lasting anger of Achilles in the Iliad.</note> and sometimes <term>kotos</term> <note>original Greek: κότον. Refers to a "grudge" or deep-seated resentment.</note>—and which our people, I believe, call <term>rancor</term>—is judged by wise men to be a sign of an ungenerous and stubborn nature. For it tends toward hatred, vengeance, injury, and insult. Nor does it proceed by the straight path of seeking justice, but more often relies on deceit and snares. When it has most boiled over, then most...

<summary>The author cites Quintilian and Aristotle to argue that a capacity for anger indicates a vigorous mind rather than stupidity. He distinguishes between "rancor" (a deep-seated, deceitful grudge) and a more immediate, "natural" anger which is more easily forgiven.</summary>
<keywords>Quintilian, Aristotle, Lorenzo de' Medici, anger, rancor, physiognomy, education, Stoicism, Plutarch, psychology</keywords>

--- Page 311 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on the utility of anger addressed to Lorenzo de’ Medici. While the previous transcription was numbered 115 (likely a folio number), this page is numbered 128 in the header, suggesting it follows the thematic sequence regarding the psychological and moral benefits of a "noble rage" in education and leadership.</meta>

->TO LORENZO DE' MEDICI. 128<-

...who, before considering whether a friend is arriving, bark at everyone who comes without distinction. This is that vital impulse which (as Homer says) casts strength into the souls of warriors; this is what the trumpets, the horns, the bugles, that mutual military encouragement and shouting, and the commander’s speeches arouse. Even if these other things were not present, Truth alone both praises and commands this impulse. "Be angry," she says, "and do not sin; let not the sun go down upon your anger." <note>original: "Irascimini... et nolite peccare, sol non occidat super iram vestram." A citation of Ephesians 4:26, here used to argue that anger is a natural tool that must be managed, not extinguished.</note> From this also arose that "contention" praised by Hesiod, <note>Hesiod, in *Works and Days*, distinguishes between two types of "Strife" (Eris): one that leads to war and one that encourages healthy competition and excellence.</note> which serves as an extraordinary spur for attaining both virtue and honor. This very thing exterminates all softness, sluggishness, and dullness from the soul. It raises up weak and slumbering minds and makes them greater for performing deeds. 

How would you interpret that anger in a boy of excellent character when he is affected by insults and disgrace, if not as a zeal for glory and virtue? You had not struck the boy with lashes, nor terrified him with threats; yet with your words you struck out tears. Why does my little Piero <note>The Latin "Petrulus" refers affectionately to Lorenzo's son, Piero the Unfortunate.</note> weep? Why does he complain? Doubtless for much the same reason as those most noble Myrmidons, the comrades-in-arms of Achilles: because they could not attain that glory of military praise which they were stirring up in their souls. "He blushed," says the comic playwright, "all is well." <note>A quote from the Roman playwright Terence in his play *The Brothers* (Adelphoe). A blush was seen by humanists as a sign of a "liberal" or noble mind capable of feeling shame and thus being corrected.</note> Or do you not think this childish anger is one and the same as shame? These things are very similar, Lorenzo; if they are valued correctly, they produce the same effects. They are both handmaidens and companions of virtue. Such to me...

<summary>The author argues that anger and shame are "companions of virtue" that drive men toward excellence, using the young Piero de' Medici's reaction to criticism as an example of noble potential.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo de' Medici, Piero de' Medici, anger, virtue, shame, education, Homer, Hesiod, Terence, psychology</keywords>

--- Page 312 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous folio regarding the educational and moral value of a spirited temperament in youth. The author, likely Angelo Poliziano writing to Lorenzo de’ Medici, moves from scriptural and Hesiodic justifications for anger to classical examples from Homer, Virgil, and Ovid to illustrate that a "noble rage" is a precursor to adult virtue.</meta>

->8 ON ANGER<-

Homer depicts Achilles in this way. Thus also does that pious Aeneas show himself to me, when he is kindled by the spurs of immortality and splendor.

Terrible, he stirs up a savage battle without distinction,
And lets loose all the reins of his anger.
<note>original: "Terribilis saeuam nullo discrimine pugnam / Suscitat, irarumq; omnes effundit habenas." Virgil, *Aeneid*, Book 12, lines 498-499. The poet describes Aeneas finally giving in to his fury against Turnus's forces.</note>

In such a way also does the Hercules of Ovid gaze for a long time upon Achelous with a grim eye, and he does not bravely master his kindled rage.
<note>original: "Talis & Acheloum Ouidianus Hercules iamdudum / lumine toruo / Spectat, & accensæ non fortiter imperat iræ." Ovid, *Metamorphoses*, Book 9, lines 27-28. Hercules is depicted struggling to contain his temper during his wrestling match with the river-god Achelous.</note>

Finally, such a noble young horse of Virgil’s also displays the character of its future excellence:

Then, if by chance the sound of arms is heard from afar,
He knows not how to stand still; his ears prick up, his limbs tremble,
And pressing back the gathered heat, he rolls the fire beneath his nostrils.
<note>original: "Tum si qua sonũ procul arma dedere, / Stare loco nescit, micat auribus, & tremit artus, / Collectumq; premens uoluit sub naribus ignem." Virgil, *Georgics*, Book 3, lines 83-85. The "fire" in the nostrils represents the animal's spirited nature (*thumos*), which the author equates to the spiritedness of a noble boy.</note>

But if to anyone, perhaps ignorant of the way nature works, this praise of anger seems too absurd—even if we were to grant the rest willingly—this point will certainly never be wrung from me: that anger in a boy of noble, upright, and free nature is a sign of his character. <note>The author distinguishes between "noble anger," which indicates a high-spirited soul, and mere "irascibility."</note> Years, letters, and discipline will be added. Every day things will become gentler; a nature of this kind will reach maturity in its own time before it withers. 

But you do not praise irascibility in a man of steady age. Granted; let it even be considered shameful and detestable. Certainly, the very same sword, if it were carried in the hand of an executioner, would appear detestable. If it were at the side of a soldier or a Commander, it would present the image of virtue and of an outstanding deed. <note>The "sword of anger" is an ancient metaphor; its moral value depends entirely on the "hand" (the reason or the person) that wields it.</note>

<summary>The author uses classical literary examples—Achilles, Aeneas, Hercules, and a spirited horse—to argue that anger in a youth is a sign of a noble and virtuous nature. He concludes that while uncontrolled anger is a vice in adults, in a well-bred boy, it is a tool of potential excellence that will be refined by education.</summary>
<keywords>anger, education, Homer, Virgil, Ovid, Aeneas, Hercules, noble nature, metaphors of fire, military virtue</keywords>

--- Page 313 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous folio, moving from the description of noble "spiritedness" (thumos) in heroes and animals to the practical role of a teacher or parent in cultivating a young mind. The author addresses Lorenzo de’ Medici directly, using the metaphor of a fertile field to explain why vigorous faults in youth are actually a promising sign of future virtue.</meta>

### TO LORENZO DE' MEDICI. 129

Vices often sprout in a noble mind mixed with virtues, just as in a rich soil, to which the hand of the farmer has not yet been applied, brambles and thorns grow. Nor, however, do those who labor in the study of agriculture think less of that appearance of a rich and fertile soil.

<note>original: "Quamuis ſemihominis ueſano gramine fœtu mandragoræ pariat flores, mœſtãq; cicutã, Nec manibus mitis ferulas, nec cruribus æque Terga rubi ſpinisq; ferat paliuron acutis." These lines use botanical imagery to describe a wild, untamed landscape. The "half-man" likely refers to the mandrake, whose root was famously thought to resemble the human form and was associated with madness or "wild grass."</note>

Although it produces the flowers of the mandrake and mournful hemlock from the wild grass of the half-man's offspring, and the land bears neither stalks gentle to the hands, nor allows the back or legs to be free from brambles and the sharp thorns of the buckthorn.

Therefore, just as good farmers are nonetheless accustomed to pull up and root out those plants and herbs that are harmful to the crops—because they have recognized the natural talent of the fertile soil from those very weeds—so indeed it is necessary that parents and teachers should not indulge still-tender vices any more than a farmer would, even though those faults have provided evidence of an excellent and noble nature. One must resist beginnings, and those things which protrude too far must be struck back. 

<note>original: "Principijs obſtandum." A reference to Ovid’s *Remedia Amoris*, line 91: "Check it at the start; medicine is prepared too late when the disease has grown strong through long delay."</note>

And after the fashion of Tarquin, the highest heads of the poppies must be struck off with a rod, and one must always resist the more powerful impulses.

<note>original: "Tarquinijq; inſtar, papauerum ſumma capita uirga demetenda." The author refers to the Roman King Tarquin the Proud, who, according to legend, signaled to his son that he should execute the leading men of a city by silently striking off the heads of the tallest poppies in his garden with a stick. Here, it is a metaphor for the "pruning" of dominant or prideful traits in a student.</note>

Nor, however, should it be thought meanwhile to be alien to the logic of a well-disposed nature,

<note>original: "ſiquidẽ reddere qui uoces iam ſcit puer, & pede certo ſignat humum, geſtit paribus colludere, & iram colligit, ac ponit temere, & mutatur in horas." These lines are from Horace, *Ars Poetica*, lines 158-160, describing the natural, fleeting volatility of a young child's temperament.</note>

if indeed the boy who already knows how to return words and marks the ground with a steady step, longs to play with his peers, and gathers anger, and sets it aside casually, and changes by the hour. Farewell.

<summary>Poliziano explains to Lorenzo de’ Medici that youthful faults are often signs of a "fertile" mind, comparing a spirited boy to a rich field that produces weeds alongside virtues; he argues that these must be carefully pruned rather than indulged.</summary>
<keywords>pedagogy, Lorenzo de' Medici, metaphors of nature, Tarquin, Horace, moral education, discipline, nobility of soul</keywords>
